The question addressed here is whether the principal results of specified research manuscripts can be recovered as
short corollaries of a smaller collection of proved ideas. The candidate intake is N001, N003, N008, L032, L035,
F013, F016, L088, N013, N016, L108 and N009. Their exact versions, corrected scopes and retained inputs are part
of the mathematical contract. An intake list is not a count of approved replacements, and this draft has no compression
factor.
The useful object is a group with a distinguished family of parabolic subgroups. Its chambers form a torsor when the
action is regular: selecting a base chamber identifies them with the group, and changing the base chamber changes
that identification by translation. Faces are the orbits of specified parabolics. A finite torsor is representable by permu-
tation matrices, but the matrices do not eliminate the crucial information: one must retain the marking, the parabolic
partitions and their common-point properties. Ordinary linear algebra alone neither proves the intersection property
nor guarantees that the resulting incidence system is a geometry.
The first common theorem isolates an exact obstruction. If two marked subgroups have the correct intersection after
applying a homomorphism, their intersection before applying it is correct precisely when the kernel contains no addi-
tional intersection. This gives both independent cyclic edge extensions and the chirality-group criterion for a smallest
regular cover. The proof is one elementary lift of an element with prescribed image. Exact coset transport through
a kernel in the chamber stabilizer is another, separately proved, statement: it preserves the whole geometry. Relative
generation and a Frattini ladder then turn finite p-group geometries into coordinate geometries, and primitive saturation
supplies the same base for the graded two-group argument.
Two further kinds of information remain essential. Forest cuts give actual intersection partitions and laminar flag
completion for symmetric groups; these markings generally have no Boolean deletion maps. Endpoint conjugation
instead gives a subgroup of index at most two. Its algebraic obstruction is an overlap and an intersection-closed support
family, while a graph double cover proves the required geometric flag completion. This operation handles both the odd-
endpoint J1 constructions and generalized-cube halving. The coordinate section identifies the latter’s toroidal lattices
by a Gray-code isomorphism of face posets and an exact affine marking, including the square map’s change of lattice
basis. It never infers a geometry from its order alone.
A further operation keeps actual subsets of retained objects rather than labelled copies of walks. The common retained-
object theorem identifies its exact permutation kernel and also supplies the old coset-core identity and the endpoint
operation’s faithfulness. Thin Petrials still require their own residue-cycle and face-shadow proofs; a general matrix
change is not a substitute for these hypotheses.
The full forest recognition, cyclic-core classification, model presentations, FAP arguments, residue-extension census,
local mirror identifications and minimal-cover proof are printed below. Every required finite program is in an appendix.
Several substantial older inputs remain explicit, including B97, Whiston’s two separate inputs, Burnside, the cyclic
core bound, the complete J1 subgroup catalogue, Danzer’s general construction, the stated Coxeter classifications,
the geometric extension and oriented-polytope interfaces, and the older five tight chiral families. None becomes free
because it appears in a shared notation.
The new integration preserves all source corrections: the unrestricted infinite Frattini converse, F013’s auxiliary kernel
assertions, diagonal translation counts, chiral facet embeddings, L088’s exchanged figure labels, N013’s rank-three
exception and N016’s nonidentity-power wording. L108’s Petrial operation is now handled with its exact action ker-
nel: the source’s unconditional embedding is corrected, while its principal obstruction and valid numbered structural
conclusions are proved in Section 23. No open conjecture, new priority claim, or Lean verification of the complete
geometry is asserted.
The intended reconstruction is explicit: take a source statement in the contract map, use its displayed corollary and the
complete internal lemmas named there, then retain only the declared background. The appendix programs are part of
that proof. Component reviews do not replace independent review of this newly integrated text.
1. Conventions and all retained inputs
All type sets and forests are finite, as are the domains of the symmetric groups. The normal-quotient bounds and nu-
merical invariants 𝜇, 𝜇′ are used for finite groups; the relative parabolic-generation formula and the Frattini results
explicitly allow arbitrary groups with finite rank where stated. Their coset sets may be infinite. The p-group classifi-
cation is for finite p-groups. Write 𝑆Ω and 𝐴Ω for the symmetric and alternating groups on Ω, and 𝑆𝑛 = 𝑆 {1,…,𝑛}.
Products of permutation groups on disjoint subsets act trivially outside their indicated supports.
A family 𝑆 = (𝑠 𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼) in 𝐺 is independent relative to 𝐵 ≤ 𝐺 if
𝑠𝑖 ∉ ⟨𝐵, 𝑠𝑗 ∶ 𝑗 ≠ 𝑖⟩ (𝑖 ∈ 𝐼).
It is a relative generating family if additionally ⟨𝐵, 𝑆⟩ = 𝐺 . Let 𝜇(𝐺, 𝐵) and 𝜇′(𝐺, 𝐵) be the largest sizes of
independent relative generating families and independent relative families respectively. Omit 𝐵 when 𝐵 = 1 . For
a group 𝐷 acting on 𝑁 , a family in 𝑁 is 𝐷-independent if no member belongs to the subgroup generated by the
𝐷-images of the other members; 𝜇′
𝐷(𝑁 ) is its maximum size. Relative strong independence means
⟨𝐵, 𝑠𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∈ 𝑈 ⟩ ∩ ⟨𝐵, 𝑠 𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∈ 𝑉 ⟩ = ⟨𝐵, 𝑠 𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∈ 𝑈 ∩ 𝑉 ⟩ (𝑈 , 𝑉 ⊆ 𝐼). (A1.1)
A typed incidence geometry has a reflexive symmetric incidence relation, with distinct elements of the same type
never incident. A flag is a pairwise incident set; a chamber has every type. Every flag of a geometry is required to
extend to a chamber. Firmness means that every nonchamber flag extends to at least two chambers. A residue consists
of the elements incident with a flag whose types do not occur in that flag. Residual connectedness requires connected
incidence graphs in residues of rank at least two. We keep the specified group action as part of the pair. The N001/N003
source coset convention includes flag transitivity; for the firm-RWPRI assertions it follows from Lemma E0 below.
For subgroups 𝐻𝑖 ≤ 𝐺 , put 𝐻𝐽 = ⋂𝑗∈𝐽 𝐻𝑗, 𝐻∅ = 𝐺 , and 𝐵 = 𝐻 𝐼. We use left cosets, acted on by left multipli-
cation; inversion converts this to the right-coset convention of Cameron–Cara. Their source coset criterion, retained
here as standard background, is:
• (G1) all 𝐻𝐽 , for 𝐽 ⊆ 𝐼 , are distinct;
• (G2) 𝐻𝐽 = ⟨𝐻 𝐽∪{𝑘} ∶ 𝑘 ∈ 𝐼 ∖ 𝐽 ⟩ when |𝐼 ∖ 𝐽 | ≥ 2 ;
• (G3) a family of cosets of distinct types with pairwise nonempty intersections has a common element.
These conditions give a firm, residually connected, flag-transitive geometry, with coset intersection as incidence; every
such group–geometry pair is represented this way. The residue at the standard flag of type 𝐽 is the corresponding coset
geometry for 𝐻𝐽 with subgroups 𝐻𝐽∪{𝑘} . No general assertion that subgroup intersections alone imply (G3) is used.
For a group acting on a geometry, the pair is PRI if its action on every type is primitive, and WPRI if its action on at
least one type is primitive. The residual versions, applied in each residue including the whole geometry, are RPRI and
RWPRI. For flag-transitive coset data these say that every, or at least one, corresponding type stabilizer is maximal;
RWPRI is precisely:
(G4) 𝐽 ⊊ 𝐼 ⟹ some 𝐻𝐽∪{𝑘} is maximal in 𝐻𝐽 .
For the finite groups in the N001 branch, a subgroup is quasi-maximal if the interval from it to the ambient group in
the subgroup lattice is a chain, equivalently if it has a unique saturated subgroup chain to that group; QPRI requires this
for every element stabilizer. Property (2𝑇 )1 means that each panel stabilizer is two-transitive on the rank-one residue.
Property (𝐼𝑃 )2 means that each rank-two residue is either a complete bipartite graph or has at most one common
neighbor for each pair of distinct same-type vertices. This last condition concerns geometric incidence, not equation
(A1.1).
The diagram has one vertex for each type; two vertices are joined when their rank-two residue is not complete bipartite.
An end of a connected diagram is a vertex whose deletion leaves a connected graph. A middle is a vertex whose
deletion leaves at least two components, all of equal cardinality. This spells out the source’s nonend requirement; in
particular a one-vertex diagram has no middle. A finite firm flag-transitive pair of rank 𝑟 and connected diagram is
minimal if |𝐺| ≤ (𝑟 + 1)! , and inductively minimal if the pair at every residue with connected diagram is minimal,
including the empty flag.
Lemma E0 (residual weak primitivity and flags). A finite-rank geometry on which a group acts residually weakly
primitively is flag-transitive.
Proof. Fix a chamber. Starting with its empty flag, choose a remaining type on which that flag’s stabilizer acts primi-
tively, hence transitively. Given any second chamber containing the chosen flag, use that stabilizer to match its element
of the chosen type to the fixed chamber’s element. Repeat in the residue. At most |𝐼| steps match any chamber to the
fixed chamber. Thus the group is chamber-transitive. Every flag extends to a chamber, so it is transitive on flags of
each fixed type. The fixed chamber’s element stabilizers then give the usual coset representation: the typewise coset
bijections preserve incidence because transitivity on incident pairs supplies a common representative. ■
Thus the firm-RWPRI results below need no extra assumption of flag transitivity or residual connectedness. For a flag-
transitive coset geometry, firmness gives distinct 𝐻𝐽 , and RWPRI gives a maximal next intersection at every proper 𝐽 .
To see distinctness explicitly, firmness gives 𝐻𝐼∖{𝑖} > 𝐵 ; choose 𝑠𝑖 in this difference. For 𝐽 ≠ 𝐾 , a type in one of
their differences supplies an 𝑠𝑖 that lies in one intersection and not the other.
The following are the unexpanded mathematical inputs.
B97 (foundational classification). Buekenhout–Cara–Dehon, Inductively minimal flag-transitive geometries (1997),
as recalled in N001 Lemma 1 and the following paragraph: an inductively minimal pair of rank 𝑟 is thin and residually
connected and has group 𝑆𝑟+1. Its connected diagram has no induced cycle longer than three, each edge lies in a
unique maximal clique, and each vertex lies in one or two maximal cliques. Conversely every such connected diagram
determines exactly one inductively minimal pair up to isomorphism. We retain the classification and uniqueness, not
the four targeted property conclusions of the 1998 paper.
W1 (Whiston bound and extremal generation). An independent family in 𝑆𝑚 has at most 𝑚 − 1 members, and an
independent family with 𝑚 − 1 members generates 𝑆𝑚. We use precisely this implication, not a converse asserting
that every independent generating family has maximum size.
W2 (Whiston omission consequence). If 𝑛 ≥ 7 and (𝑠𝑖) is an independent generating family of 𝑆𝑛 of size 𝑛−1 , each
omission subgroup ⟨𝑠𝑗 ∶ 𝑗 ≠ 𝑖⟩ is intransitive, is transitive imprimitive with blocks of size two, or is 𝐴𝑛. This is the
separate consequence of Whiston’s proof stated on page 4 of the 2002 manuscript. Its dependence on the classification
of finite simple groups is retained. W2 is not deduced here from W1.
We also retain Cayley’s formula: there are𝑚𝑚−2 trees on a specified labelled 𝑚-element set. Elementary group theory,
permutation parity, and the equivalence of primitivity with maximality of a point stabilizer are used throughout. The
source references for these inputs are given at the end. None of the declared target conclusions is used as background.
PΦ (finite-p-group Frattini quotient). For every finite p-group 𝑃 , the quotient 𝑃 /Φ(𝑃 ) is an elementary abelian
p-group, hence a finite-dimensional vector space over 𝔽𝑝. Here Φ(𝑃 ) is the intersection of the maximal subgroups.
We retain this elementary group-theoretic fact in the form recalled in [ACK05], page 174. Zorn’s lemma is retained
for the explicitly infinite-group maximal-overgroup arguments. Neither input is a classification of p-group geometries.
For the action-isomorphism convention in Corollary B7, we retain the classical fact that every automorphism of 𝑆𝑛
is inner for 𝑛 ≥ 7 . Choosing instead the source’s equivalence of conjugate coset data gives the same stated marking
correspondence.
Two earlier results quoted in [ACK05] remain explicit source imports, not conclusions proved here. Its Theorem
4.1 quotes Gaschütz: if a finite Frattini-free group 𝐹 has socle 𝑆 = 𝐴 × 𝐷 , with 𝐴 the product of abelian simple
factors and 𝐷 the product of nonabelian simple factors, then 𝐹 = 𝐴 ⋊ 𝐾 , where 𝐷 = Inn(𝑆) ≤ 𝐾 ≤ Aut(𝑆) =
Aut(𝐴) × Aut(𝐷), and the maximal 𝐾-invariant subgroups of 𝐴 have trivial intersection. Its final paragraph quotes
Archer’s earlier reduction of RPRI geometries that are not direct sums to primitive group actions after removal of the
action kernel. These retained results are not used to prove results E0–E5 or any of Corollaries C1–C11; their original
proofs are outside this intake.
For a group marked by involutions 𝑟𝑖, 𝑖 ∈ 𝑉 , write 𝐺𝐼 = ⟨𝑟 𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼⟩ . Its intersection property (IP) is 𝐺𝐼 ∩ 𝐺𝐽 =
𝐺𝐼∩𝐽 for all subsets. A string C-group has IP and [𝑟𝑖, 𝑟𝑗] = 1 when |𝑖 − 𝑗| > 1 ; it is nondegenerate if consecutive
generators do not commute. Its type records their product orders. All marked isomorphisms preserve the ordered
generators. Write [𝑥, 𝑦] = 𝑥 −1𝑦−1𝑥𝑦, 𝑥𝑦 = 𝑦 −1𝑥𝑦, and 𝐷8 for the dihedral group of order eight. The lower central
series is 𝛾𝑗(𝐺).
We retain the regular-polytope/string-C-group correspondence, its marked quotient/covering correspondence, and its
coset face/stabilizer description. We also retain Burnside’s basis theorem, finite nilpotency of finite two-groups, the
lower-central graded Lie construction and generation in degree one, and the Euclidean algorithm for Gaussian integers.
The cyclic core bound retained in §12 is stated there exactly. The geometric alternating assembly and free-extension
theorems retained in §15 are also stated exactly. The amalgam normal-form theorem is retained. NQ’s universal nilpo-
tent quotient algorithm and exact finite-group/coset algorithms are retained for the new parameterized kernel census
and presentation recognition. Theorem Q5.5 supplies the finite bound and Theorem Q5.6 supplies search completeness;
no SmallGroups catalogue is an input. The standard string-IP criteria, finite square-quotient classification, universal
geometric interpretation and infinite marked decomposition quoted by N009 are retained in their exact forms in §24.
No target classification is accepted as its own background.
For finite p-groups, the PΦ input is used together with Burnside’s basis theorem: irredundant generating markings
map to bases of the Frattini quotient. Theorem S proves the required compatibility with every marked subgroup; that
compatibility is not an imported classification. Equation labels beginning A or Q and theorem labels beginning Q are
unique internal labels in this manuscript. They are not references to another manuscript. All necessary proofs are
printed below.
Oriented geometry and the additional retained inputs
We retain the following standard geometric correspondence, with its actual scope. Set
𝑊𝑛 = ⟨𝑟 0, … , 𝑟𝑛−1 ∣ 𝑟 2
𝑖 = 1, [𝑟𝑖, 𝑟𝑗] = 1 (|𝑖 − 𝑗| > 1)⟩, 𝑈 𝑛 = 𝑊 +
𝑛 = ⟨𝑠 1, … , 𝑠𝑛−1⟩, 𝑠 𝑖 = 𝑟 𝑖−1𝑟𝑖.
The presentation of 𝑈𝑛 has relations (𝑠𝑖 ⋯ 𝑠𝑗)2 = 1 for 𝑖 < 𝑗 . Marked quotients of 𝑈𝑛 satisfying the string 𝐶 +
intersection property correspond to chiral or directly regular polytopes. Recursively, a marked quotient of rank at least
three is such a group precisely when its facet and vertex-figure subgroups are such groups and their intersection is
the medial subgroup. Rank two starts with a nontrivial cyclic group, including the infinite cyclic group; rank one has
trivial rotation group. Direct regularity is equivalent to existence of the automorphism induced by conjugation with 𝑟0.
Marked epimorphisms correspond to covers. The analogous quotients without the intersection property describe the
relevant oriented maniplexes. This standard correspondence is retained, not proved by K.
We also retain the geometric section theorem: the facets of facets and the vertex-figures of vertex-figures of a chiral
polytope are directly regular. This is the Schulte–Weiss result cited as Proposition 9 in the source. All sections of
a directly regular polytope are directly regular. These are the only geometric ingredients needed to remove the local
polytopality assumptions in the cover criterion. In particular, we do not retain the target’s Proposition 2.2 or Theorem
3.1 as background.
For the operation branch we retain Danzer’s regular lattice-polytope construction and its full semidirect-product group
in the exact form stated in §20, the classical finite/affine/compact-hyperbolic Coxeter classifications, and the standard
convex-polytope orders used there. The particular toroidal lattices are proved in §9. The J1 branch retains TomLib’s
complete forty-class subgroup table and its ambient permutation model, together with exact GAP group, coset and
orbit algorithms; its original geometry census is not an input. The chiral branch retains the older five classified tight
families and their embedded chirality property, stated exactly in §22.3. All these are substantial charged inputs. In the
general coset sections use left cosets; the operation certificate uses right cosets. Inversion identifies the conventions,
and every common-point or subgroup assertion is invariant under that conversion. The scripts use their own explicitly
given convention.
2. Exact kernels, relative generation and the Frattini ladder
Theorem K (intersection transport, exact coset transport and retained-object operations).
(0) Three-subgroup transport. Let 𝜋 ∶ 𝐻 → 𝑄 be a homomorphism, 𝐶 ≤ 𝐴∩𝐵 ≤ 𝐻 , and suppose 𝜋(𝐴)∩𝜋(𝐵) =
𝜋(𝐶) . Then
𝐴 ∩ 𝐵 = 𝐶 ⟺ 𝐴 ∩ 𝐵 ∩ ker 𝜋 ≤ 𝐶. (K0)
Proof of (0). Only the reverse implication needs proof. Given 𝑥 ∈ 𝐴 ∩ 𝐵 , choose 𝑐 ∈ 𝐶 with 𝜋(𝑐) = 𝜋(𝑥) . Then
𝑥𝑐−1 ∈ 𝐴 ∩ 𝐵 ∩ ker 𝜋 ≤ 𝐶 , so 𝑥 ∈ 𝐶 . No finiteness, centrality or global surjectivity is needed. This is the generic
algebraic statement formalized as LeemansLift.intersection_of_image_and_kernel; the complete proof is here.
(a) Marked intersections. Let 𝜋 ∶ 𝐻 ↠ 𝑄 preserve a marking, and suppose the image marking has IP. Put𝐾 = ker 𝜋,
𝐻𝐼 = ⟨𝑟 𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼⟩ , and 𝐾𝐼 = 𝐾 ∩ 𝐻𝐼. Then the marking in 𝐻 has IP if and only if 𝐾𝐼 ∩ 𝐾𝐽 = 𝐾 𝐼∩𝐽 for all subsets.
Proof of (a). Apply (0) with 𝐴 = 𝐻 𝐼, 𝐵 = 𝐻 𝐽 , 𝐶 = 𝐻 𝐼∩𝐽 . The image hypothesis is the quotient’s IP, and the kernel
condition is exactly 𝐾𝐼 ∩ 𝐾𝐽 = 𝐾 𝐼∩𝐽 , since the reverse inclusion always holds. This proves both directions. ■
(b) Exact coset transport. If 𝑁 ⊴ 𝐺 and 𝑁 ≤ 𝐵 , the quotient map induces an isomorphism of typed pregeometries
𝑔𝐻𝑖 ⟼ (𝑔𝑁 )(𝐻𝑖/𝑁 ) (A6.3)
between the families in 𝐺 and 𝐺/𝑁 . It preserves all coset intersections, flags, chambers, residues, common-point
witnesses, firmness, residual connectedness, flag-transitivity, RWPRI and RPRI. Subgroup families containing 𝑁 cor-
respond bijectively to quotient subgroup families.
Proof of (b). For the quotient homomorphism 𝜋,
𝜋−1(𝜋(𝑔)(𝐻𝑖/𝑁 )) = 𝑔𝐻 𝑖.
Thus corresponding cosets are in bijection and every intersection pattern is preserved, including common points of any
family. This proves the incidence and flag claims, also for pregeometries lacking flag-transitivity. The acting groups
have the same permutation image on the corresponding cosets. Subgroup correspondence preserves maximality in
each interval above 𝑁 , including all residue intervals, which proves the primitive properties. The usual subgroup
correspondence gives the final assertion. ■
(c) Retained-object operations and the faithful image. Let G act on sets X and Y. Suppose there is a G-invariant
subset Y₀⊆Y and an equivariant surjection p:Y₀→X. Suppose also that Y admits an injective equivariant encoding into
a tagged disjoint union of finite products, each factor being X or its power set, with G fixing the tags. In particular, an
element fixing X pointwise fixes every encoding. Then
𝑁 ∶= ker(𝐺 ↷ 𝑌 ) = ker(𝐺 ↷ 𝑋) = ⋂
𝑥∈𝑋
𝐺𝑥. (Kc1)
If X is the disjoint union of transitive G-sets with selected stabilizers H_j, then
𝑁 = ⋂
𝑗
core𝐺(𝐻𝑗) = core𝐺(⋂
𝑗
𝐻𝑗) . (Kc2)
The induced faithful group is G/N. If it acts by type-preserving automorphisms of a thin residually connected finite-rank
geometry on Y and is chamber-transitive, it is the full type-preserving automorphism group. For a chamber D and a
panel F⊂D in that geometry, the stabilizers in G satisfy
𝐺𝐷 = 𝑁 , [𝐺 𝐹 ∶ 𝑁 ] = 2. (Kc3)
Thus G_F of order two forces N=1. No finiteness of G or X is required.
Proof. An element fixing Y fixes X by the equivariant surjection. An element fixing X fixes each encoded ele-
ment/subset and hence each uniquely encoded object of Y. This proves (Kc1). Every stabilizer in a transitive orbit
is a conjugate of its selected H_j. Intersecting those conjugates proves both equalities in (Kc2); the intersection is
exactly the largest normal subgroup contained in every H_j. The quotient is the faithful permutation image by the
first isomorphism theorem. The chamber assertion follows from the free chamber action in Lemma O0 below: any
automorphism agrees on a base chamber with an element of the transitive image, so their quotient fixes that chamber
and is the identity. The preimage of its chamber stabilizer is N. Chamber transitivity makes G_F transitive on the two
chambers containing F, with stabilizer G_D=N. The orbit–stabilizer bijection proves (Kc3), also for infinite G. ■
The encoding hypothesis uses actual subsets, not independently labelled copies of the same subset. The theorem
identifies a permutation image; it does not say that a changed generator list is a homomorphic image of the old marking.
(d) Exact intersections after a quotient. Let q:H→Q be a homomorphism with kernel N, and let A,B≤H. Then
𝑞(𝐴) ∩ 𝑞(𝐵) = 𝑞(𝐴 ∩ 𝐵) ⟺ 𝑁 ∩ 𝐴𝐵 = (𝑁 ∩ 𝐴)(𝑁 ∩ 𝐵). (Kd1)
Here AB is a set product; it need not be a subgroup. If q is injective on both A and B, the right side is simply N∩AB=1.
If N≤A, equality always holds. Thus, when A∩B=C, these are exact criteria for their quotient images to meet in q(C),
rather than an assumption that arbitrary quotients preserve marked IP.
Proof. Suppose first that the image equality holds and ab∈N, where a∈A and b∈B. Choose c∈A∩B with
q(c)=q(a)=q(b)⁻¹. Then a c ⁻¹∈N∩A and cb∈N∩B, so ab=(a c ⁻¹)(cb). The reverse set inclusion is immediate.
Conversely, if q(a)=q(b), then ab ⁻¹∈N∩AB. Write ab ⁻¹=uv with u∈N∩A and v∈N∩B. The element u ⁻¹a=vb lies in
A∩B and has image q(a), proving the image equality. If N≤A, both sides of the right-hand equality are N. ■
Lemma O0 (chamber galleries and residue stabilizers). A residually connected geometry of finite rank has con-
nected chamber graph. If it is thin, its full type-preserving automorphism group acts freely on chambers. In that thin
case, if a subgroup G acts chamber-transitively, it therefore acts regularly and equals the full group. For the unique
involution r_i carrying a base chamber across its i-panel,
𝐺 = ⟨𝑟 𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼⟩, 𝐺 𝐹 = ⟨𝑟 𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∉ 𝑡(𝐹 )⟩ (O0)
for every subflag F of the base chamber.
Proof. Induct on rank. Rank-one chambers are mutually adjacent. At larger rank, chambers containing an object are
gallery-connected by induction in its residue. A path in the connected incidence graph, extending each incident pair to
a chamber, then joins any two chambers by such galleries. This also applies inside every residue. In the thin case, an
automorphism fixing a chamber fixes its unique neighbour across every panel, hence every chamber and every object.
This proves freeness. A transitive subgroup exhausts the full free action. Its unique panel-changing element r_i has
square fixing the base chamber, so is a nonidentity involution. Reading an arbitrary gallery as successive panel changes
proves generation. Reading a gallery inside the residue of F proves the stabilizer formula, since only types outside F
change. ■
Marked torsors and geometric recognition
For this recognition interface a marking of a group G is an ordered family of nonidentity involutions r_i generating G,
indexed by a finite set I. For J⊆I write G_J=⟨r_j:j∈J⟩. The intersection property, abbreviated IP, is
𝐺𝐽 ∩ 𝐺𝐾 = 𝐺 𝐽∩𝐾 (𝐽 , 𝐾 ⊆ 𝐼). (O1)
A marking satisfying (O1) is a C-group marking. Its maximal parabolics are P_i=G_(I{i}). Right cosets P_i g are
objects of type i, with incidence given by nonempty intersection. The common-point property for every pairwise
incident family of differently typed cosets will be called typed Helly. IP and typed Helly are separate requirements.
Recognition lemma O1.1. If a marking has IP and its maximal-parabolic cosets have typed Helly, their incidence
system is a thin, residually connected geometry on which G acts regularly; G is its full type-preserving automorphism
group.
Proof. A common coset point extends every flag to the chamber consisting of its cosets in all types. Translation
takes every flag to its base flag. By IP, the stabilizer of a base flag of types S is G_(I\S), the full chamber stabilizer
is trivial, and a panel stabilizer is a group ⟨r_i⟩ of order two. Thus panels have exactly two chambers. In a residue of
rank at least two, its stabilizer G_(I\S) is generated by its proper maximal parabolics: every remaining marker belongs
to one of them. The word expressing an element in their union gives a walk in the residue incidence graph. Hence
every such residue is connected. Lemma O0 now identifies the already regular G-action with the full type-preserving
automorphism group. ■
There is a convenient finite certificate for the second requirement. Put
𝑋𝑖𝑗 = {𝑃 𝑖𝑥 ∶ 𝑥 ∈ 𝑃 𝑗}, 𝑃 𝐽 = ⋂
𝑗∈𝐽
𝑃𝑗.
Recognition lemma O1.2. For a finite group, typed Helly is equivalent to
∣⋂
𝑗∈𝐽
𝑋𝑖𝑗∣ = [𝑃 𝐽 ∶ 𝑃 𝐽 ∩ 𝑃𝑖] (𝑖 ∉ 𝐽 , |𝐽 | ≥ 2). (O2)
Proof. The cosets represented in P_J form a subset of the intersection on the left, and their number is the right side.
Equality of sizes is therefore equality of sets. Induct on the size of a flag. Normalize all but its last object to base cosets
using the common point supplied inductively. The last coset lies in the left side of (O2), so has a representative in
the common subgroup of those base cosets. This is a common point for the entire flag. Singletons and pairs start the
induction. Conversely, typed Helly applied to such an anchored family gives the set equality and hence (O2). ■
These lemmas apply to the actual orbit partitions of the regular G-torsor. Replacing each g by its permutation matrix
loses no information, provided the distinguished parabolic partitions are retained. Linearity alone proves neither (O1)
nor (O2).
Theorem R (relative-generation core).
(a) For finite 𝐺 and 𝑁 ⊴ 𝐺,
𝜇(𝐺) ≤ 𝜇(𝐺/𝑁 ) + 𝜇 ′(𝑁 ). (A4.1)
If 𝑁 is abelian, conjugation gives the sharper inequality
𝜇(𝐺) ≤ 𝜇(𝐺/𝑁 ) + 𝜇 ′
𝐺(𝑁 ). (A4.2)
(b) A source coset geometry for finite 𝐺, of rank 𝑟 and Borel subgroup 𝐵, has 𝑟 ≤ 𝜇 ′(𝐺, 𝐵). More generally, for
any finite-rank firm RWPRI geometry with specified group 𝐺, possibly infinite, choose any
𝑠𝑖 ∈ 𝐻 𝐼∖{𝑖} ∖ 𝐻𝑖. (A4.3)
Then for every 𝐽 ⊆ 𝐼 ,
𝐻𝐽 = ⟨𝐵, 𝑠𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∉ 𝐽 ⟩. (A4.4)
The family is strongly independent relative to 𝐵. When 𝐺 is finite, this also gives 𝑟 ≤ 𝜇(𝐺, 𝐵) .
Proof. For (a), take an independent generating family 𝑆 and a subset 𝑇 ⊆ 𝑆 whose images minimally generate 𝐺/𝑁 .
For each 𝑠 ∈ 𝑆 ∖ 𝑇 , choose a word 𝑤𝑠 in 𝑇 with 𝑠𝑁 = 𝑤 𝑠𝑁 . The elements 𝑣𝑠 = 𝑠𝑤 −1
𝑠 ∈ 𝑁 are independent: an
expression for one in terms of the others would express 𝑠 in terms of 𝑆 ∖ {𝑠} . This proves (A4.1). If 𝑁 is abelian, its
conjugation action by 𝐺 = 𝑁 ⟨𝑇 ⟩ equals the action by ⟨𝑇 ⟩. The same substitution applies to expressions in conjugates
of the other 𝑣𝑠, proving 𝐺-independence and (A4.2).
For (b), (G1) makes the difference in (A4.3) nonempty, since its intersection with 𝐻𝑖 is 𝐵. For a general finite-rank
firm RWPRI geometry, Lemma E0 gives flag transitivity and distinct intersections, so the same panel choices exist.
Every other 𝑠𝑗, as well as 𝐵, lies in 𝐻𝑖, so the family is independent relative to 𝐵. This proves the first rank bound.
Under (G4), prove (A4.4) downwards from 𝐽 = 𝐼 . Choose 𝑘 ∉ 𝐽 for which 𝐻𝐽∪{𝑘} is maximal in 𝐻𝐽 . The inductive
hypothesis supplies its generators, while 𝑠𝑘 belongs to 𝐻𝐽 and not to that maximal subgroup. Adjoining it generates
𝐻𝐽 , proving (A4.4). Finally 𝐻𝐽 ∩ 𝐻𝐾 = 𝐻 𝐽∪𝐾 becomes (A1.1), establishing strong relative independence. This
part of the proof uses firmness and residual maximality, with no finiteness of 𝐺 or separate assumption (G2). ■
Lemma E1 (relative finite generation supplies maximal overgroups). Suppose 𝐺 = ⟨𝐵, 𝑡 1, … , 𝑡𝑟⟩ with finite 𝑟.
Every proper subgroup 𝐻 ≥ 𝐵 is contained in a maximal subgroup of 𝐺.
Proof. For finite 𝐺, choose a proper overgroup of largest order. In general, order the proper overgroups of 𝐻 by
inclusion. The union of a chain is a subgroup; if that union were 𝐺, a single member would contain the finitely many
𝑡𝑗, and hence all of 𝐺, a contradiction. Every chain therefore has a proper upper bound, and Zorn’s lemma gives a
maximal element. ■
Let Φ(𝐺) denote the intersection of all maximal subgroups of 𝐺, with the intersection equal to 𝐺 if there are none. It
is characteristic and hence normal. If every proper overgroup of 𝐵 lies in a maximal subgroup, then
Φ(𝐺)𝐻 = 𝐺, 𝐻 ≥ 𝐵 ⟹ 𝐻 = 𝐺. (A6.1)
Indeed a maximal subgroup containing a proper 𝐻 also contains Φ(𝐺). This hypothesis holds for finite 𝐺, and more
generally under Lemma E1. It is important to supply it when 𝐺 is infinite.
Containment and quotient actions
Theorem E2 (normal-subgroup ladder). Suppose all intersections 𝐻𝐽 , 𝐽 ⊆ 𝐼 , are distinct and that some ordering
of the types gives a maximal subgroup chain
𝐺 = 𝐾 0 > 𝐾 1 > ⋯ > 𝐾 𝑟 = 𝐵, 𝐾 𝑗 = 𝐻 {1,…,𝑗}. (A6.2)
If 𝑁 ⊴ 𝐺 lies in every maximal subgroup of 𝐺, then 𝑁 ≤ 𝐵 . The group may be infinite.
Proof. Choose 𝑡𝑗 ∈ 𝐾 𝑗−1 ∖ 𝐾 𝑗. Each maximal inclusion gives 𝐾𝑗−1 = ⟨𝐾 𝑗, 𝑡𝑗⟩, so backwards induction gives
𝐺 = ⟨𝐵, 𝑡 1, … , 𝑡𝑟⟩. Lemma E1 implies that 𝑁 𝐻𝑖 = 𝐺 is impossible for a proper 𝐻𝑖: a maximal overgroup of 𝐻𝑖
would contain both factors.
Suppose 𝑁 ≰ 𝐵 , and let 𝑘 be the first index with 𝑁 ≰ 𝐾 𝑘. Then 𝑁 ≤ 𝐾 𝑘−1, and normality and maximality give
𝐾𝑘−1 = 𝑁 (𝐾𝑘−1 ∩ 𝐻𝑘).
Suppose, in an upward step with 1 ≤ 𝑗 < 𝑘 , that 𝐾𝑗 = 𝑁 (𝐾𝑗 ∩ 𝐻𝑘). Since 𝑁 ≤ 𝐾 𝑗−1,
𝐾𝑗 ≤ 𝑁 (𝐾𝑗−1 ∩ 𝐻𝑘) ≤ 𝐾 𝑗−1.
Maximality permits only the endpoints. Equality with the lower endpoint would imply 𝐾𝑗−1 ∩ 𝐻𝑘 ≤ 𝐾 𝑗, and conse-
quently
𝐾𝑗−1 ∩ 𝐻𝑘 = 𝐾 𝑗 ∩ 𝐻𝑘.
These are different indexed intersections, because 𝑗 < 𝑘 , contradicting distinctness. Thus equality with the upper
endpoint holds. Repeating this step reaches 𝐺 = 𝑁 𝐻𝑘, the contradiction established in the first paragraph. ■
RWPRI supplies (A6.2) by choosing a maximal next intersection repeatedly. Lemma E0 and firmness supply distinct-
ness. With 𝑁 = Φ(𝐺) , Theorem E2 proves Φ(𝐺) ≤ 𝐵 for every finite-rank firm RWPRI geometry, including infinite
𝐺. Its infinite proof does not assume that the whole Frattini subgroup is always superfluous.
Corollary E3 (exact coset-kernel transport). All assertions of Theorem K(b) apply whenever the normal subgroup
lies in the Borel. This is the coset-geometry specialization of the common kernel theorem, including pregeometries
and infinite groups.
The quotient by Φ(𝐺) is Frattini free: its maximal subgroups correspond to the maximal subgroups of 𝐺, whose
intersection is Φ(𝐺). If 𝐺 has no maximal subgroups, then Φ(𝐺) = 𝐺 and the quotient is trivial, giving the same
conclusion. Applying K(c) with X=Y the typed coset objects gives the kernel
⋂
𝑖
core𝐺(𝐻𝑖) = core𝐺(𝐵). (A6.4)
It is the core from (Kc2); K(b) transports through it to the faithful action. Theorem E2 puts Φ(𝐺) inside this kernel.
The bijection here concerns indexed subgroup families with the quotient map fixed. It is not a claim that all auto-
morphisms of 𝐺/Φ(𝐺) lift to 𝐺, or that their respective automorphism orbits have equal counts. The source’s stated
incidence isomorphisms and its quotient-family correspondence are fully recovered without that extra assertion.
Relative generators and the corrected infinite scope
Theorem E4 (Frattini transport of relative generators). Suppose every proper subgroup containing 𝐵 is contained
in a maximal subgroup of 𝐺. For a finite indexed family (𝑠𝑖), it is an independent generating family of 𝐺 relative to 𝐵
if and only if its image is an independent generating family of 𝐺/Φ(𝐺) relative to 𝐵Φ(𝐺)/Φ(𝐺). In particular this
holds for finite groups and for groups finitely generated relative to 𝐵.
Proof. For 𝐻 = ⟨𝐵, 𝑠 𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼⟩ , generation in the quotient means Φ(𝐺)𝐻 = 𝐺 , equivalent to 𝐻 = 𝐺 by (A6.1).
Under generation, independence means that every deletion subgroup 𝐻 ′
𝑖 = ⟨𝐵, 𝑠 𝑗 ∶ 𝑗 ≠ 𝑖⟩ is proper. Apply the
same equivalence to each 𝐻 ′
𝑖 : it is proper precisely when its image is proper in the quotient. This is exactly quotient
independence. ■
The unqualified printed infinite converse of N008 Theorem 2.1 is false. Let 𝐷 = 𝐶 𝑝∞ be a Prüfer group and 𝐺 =
𝐷 × 𝐶 2. Every maximal subgroup of this abelian group has a cyclic prime-order quotient. Divisibility forces every
homomorphism from 𝐷 to such a quotient to be trivial. Hence 𝐷 × 1 is the unique maximal subgroup and equals
Φ(𝐺). The one element generating the second factor generates 𝐺/Φ(𝐺), but does not generate 𝐺. Taking 𝐵 = 1
gives the advertised failure even for a one-element family.
This is a source-scope correction, not a new-priority claim. Theorem E4 gives the required finite version and a precise
sufficient infinite hypothesis. If a finite family is already known to generate 𝐺 relative to 𝐵, Lemma E1 supplies the
maximal-overgroup hypothesis, so its independence equivalence also survives for infinite 𝐺. The independent proof
of Theorem E2 remains valid in full infinite geometric scope.
3. One primitive-coordinate theorem and its geometry specialization
Theorem S (primitive saturation and relative coordinates). Let a finite p-group 𝑃 have an irredundant generating
marking (𝑟𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∈ 𝑉 ) , with |𝑉 | = 𝑑 , and put 𝐹 = Φ(𝑃 ) . For 𝑃𝑈 = ⟨𝑟 𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∈ 𝑈 ⟩ ,
Φ(𝑃𝑈 ) = 𝑃 𝑈 ∩ 𝐹 , 𝑃 𝑈 𝐹 = 𝜋 −1 span( ̄ 𝑟𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∈ 𝑈 ), [𝑃 ∶ 𝑃 𝑈 𝐹 ] = 𝑝 𝑑−|𝑈| . (S.1)
These saturated parabolics intersect according to intersection of label sets. Each omission subgroup has the unique
maximal overgroup 𝑀𝑖 = 𝑃 𝑉 ∖{𝑖}𝐹 . Its coset action is the unique primitive coarsening of that omission action; all
such actions together give the complete d-partite geometry with p elements of each type and faithful chamber-regular
group 𝑃 /𝐹.
More generally, if 𝐵 ◁ 𝑃 , 𝐹 ≤ 𝐵 , and (𝑠𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼) generates 𝑃 irredundantly relative to 𝐵, then 𝑃 /𝐵 has basis
(𝑠𝑖𝐵) and
⟨𝐵, 𝑠𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∈ 𝑈 ⟩/𝐵 = span(𝑠𝑖𝐵 ∶ 𝑖 ∈ 𝑈 ). (S.2)
Its omission geometry is the complete |𝐼|-partite p-point geometry. Finally, if the original marking consists of involu-
tions and 𝑝 = 2 , then 𝐹 = 𝑃 ′, and Φ(𝑃𝑈 ) = 𝑃 ′
𝑈 for every parabolic.
Proof. Burnside’s basis theorem makes ( ̄ 𝑟𝑖) a basis of 𝑃 /𝐹. The image of 𝑃𝑈 has dimension |𝑈 |, so its elementary
abelian quotient has kernel 𝑃𝑈 ∩ 𝐹 and order 𝑝|𝑈| . It follows that Φ(𝑃𝑈 ) ≤ 𝑃 𝑈 ∩ 𝐹 . Since 𝑃𝑈 has |𝑈 | generators,
Burnside gives [𝑃𝑈 ∶ Φ(𝑃 𝑈 )] ≤ 𝑝 |𝑈| ; equality of the two bounds proves the kernel identity. The inverse-image,
intersection and index assertions follow from coordinate subspaces. Every maximal subgroup contains 𝐹 and has
index p; containing an omission subgroup therefore forces equality with the preimage of its coordinate hyperplane.
This proves uniqueness and primitivity. Cosets prescribe independent coordinate values; every assignment extends to
a vector. Their intersection kernel is 𝐹 , and the quotient translations act regularly on chambers.
For the relative assertion, 𝑃 /𝐵 is elementary abelian because 𝐵 ≥ 𝐹 . The images span, and a nontrivial linear
relation would express one 𝑠𝑖 modulo 𝐵 in terms of the others, contrary to irredundancy. They therefore form a basis,
proving (S.2) and the same coordinate-geometry statements. For involution generators, the abelianization is elementary
abelian of exponent two. Thus 𝑃 ′ ≤ Φ(𝑃 ), while the elementary abelian quotient by 𝑃 ′ gives Φ(𝑃 ) ≤ 𝑃 ′. The same
argument applies to each parabolic. ■
Theorem E5 (the coordinate specialization of (A4.4)). Let 𝑃 be a finite p-group and let a firm RWPRI coset geometry
for 𝑃 have rank 𝑟 and Borel 𝐵. Then 𝐵 ⊴ 𝑃 , 𝑃 /𝐵 ≅ 𝔽 𝑟
𝑝, and in a suitable basis (𝑣𝑖) of 𝑃 /𝐵,
𝐻𝐽 /𝐵 = span{𝑣𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∉ 𝐽 } (𝐽 ⊆ 𝐼). (A6.5)
Consequently every type has 𝑝 elements, every choice of one element of each type is a chamber, the geometry is RPRI,
and it is the direct sum of 𝑟 primitive rank-one geometries. Its faithful group 𝑃 /𝐵 acts regularly on chambers.
Proof. The normal-subgroup ladder E2 gives Φ(𝑃 ) ≤ 𝐵 . Since 𝑃 /Φ(𝑃 ) is abelian, 𝐵 is normal. Theorem R(b)
supplies the irredundant relative marking and every parabolic formula. Apply the relative clause (S.2) of Theorem S: it
gives the stated basis and all coordinate cosets. Every assignment is a chamber; fixing a further coordinate has index p,
hence is maximal. Therefore the geometry is RPRI and a direct sum of primitive p-point geometries. Its action kernel
is 𝐵 by normality and Theorem K(b), and the quotient translations act regularly on chambers. ■
Conversely, writing 𝑑 = dim 𝑃 /Φ(𝑃 ), any 𝑟 independent linear functionals on that vector space, each considered up
to nonzero scalar, give the full collection of examples of rank 1 ≤ 𝑟 ≤ 𝑑 by pulling back their kernels. Indeed a basis
of 𝑃 /𝐵 in E5 supplies exactly those quotient coordinate functionals. Every resulting family is firm, flag-transitive,
residually connected and RPRI by coordinate assignments. For clarity this also gives the exact number of indexed
subgroup families, not automorphism orbits:
∏
𝑟−1
𝑗=0(𝑝𝑑 − 𝑝𝑗)
(𝑝 − 1)𝑟 . (A6.6)
The numerator chooses independent ordered functionals; independent rescaling does not change their kernels. This
converse and count are useful extra consequences of the common theory, not extra source targets or an asserted new
result.
Two subsidiary source statements need only elementary arguments. For two subgroups 𝐻1, 𝐻2, every left coset of one
intersects every left coset of the other exactly when 𝐻1𝐻2 = 𝐺, since 𝑔𝐻1 ∩ℎ𝐻 2 ≠ ∅ is equivalent to 𝑔−1ℎ ∈ 𝐻 1𝐻2.
This is the rank-two direct-sum criterion. If 𝑈 , 𝑊 < 𝑉 = 𝔽 𝑘
𝑝, with 𝑈 a hyperplane, 𝑈 ∩ 𝑊 a hyperplane of 𝑈 , and
𝑊 ⊈ 𝑈 , then the image of 𝑊 in 𝑉 /𝑈 is one-dimensional. Therefore dim 𝑊 = (𝑘 − 2) + 1 = 𝑘 − 1 , giving the
source’s maximal-subspace lemma.
Every residue supplies a Frattini test
For every proper 𝐽 ⊊ 𝐼 , apply E2 inside the residue group 𝐻𝐽 . The Borel of that residue is still 𝐵, since
⋂
𝑖∉𝐽
(𝐻𝐽 ∩ 𝐻𝑖) = 𝐵.
Thus every firm RWPRI geometry satisfies
Φ(𝐻𝐽 ) ≤ 𝐵 ≤ 𝐻 𝐽∪{𝑖} (𝑖 ∉ 𝐽 ). (A6.7)
This is a necessary rejection test on candidate residue groups. It neither asserts sufficiency nor assumes Φ(𝐻𝐽 ) ≤
Φ(𝐺). No runtime improvement for a particular enumeration follows from the proof alone.
4. Symmetric groups from forest cuts and laminar flags
Let 𝑇 be a tree with vertex set 𝑉 and edge set 𝐸. Give each vertex a nonempty block 𝐵𝑣, with the blocks pairwise
disjoint, and put Ω = ⨆ 𝑣 𝐵𝑣. For 𝐹 ⊆ 𝐸 , let
𝐾𝐹 = ∏
𝐶

, (A2.1)
where 𝐶 runs over the components of the forest (𝑉 , 𝐹 ). Root 𝑇 , and let 𝐴𝑒 ⊆ Ω be the union of blocks on the side of
𝑇 − 𝑒 away from the root. Set 𝐻𝑒 = 𝐾 𝐸∖{𝑒} = 𝑆 𝐴𝑒

Theorem F (forest intersections and flag completion). The following hold.
1. 𝐾𝐹 ∩ 𝐾𝐷 = 𝐾 𝐹 ∩𝐷. Moreover 𝐾𝐹 is generated by 𝐾∅ together with the groups 𝐾{𝑒}, 𝑒 ∈ 𝐹 .
2. The coset system of the 𝐻𝑒 is a firm, residually connected, flag-transitive geometry. Its type- 𝑒 elements identify
with the |𝐴𝑒|-subsets of Ω, and distinct types satisfy
𝑋𝑒 ∗ 𝑋 𝑓 ⟺ |𝑋 𝑒 ∩ 𝑋𝑓 | = |𝐴 𝑒 ∩ 𝐴𝑓 |. (A2.2)
3. Its Borel subgroup is 𝐾∅ = ∏ 𝑣 𝑆𝐵𝑣
. The residue whose remaining types are 𝐹 is the join of the same construc-
tions on the nontrivial components of (𝑉 , 𝐹 ). Its acting group is 𝐾𝐹 ; components with no edges contribute
only a kernel on this residue.
Proof. A permutation belongs to both groups in (1) exactly when it preserves every intersection of their component
blocks. Two vertices lie in a common block of both forests precisely when their unique tree path belongs to both edge
sets. Thus the common refinement is the component partition for 𝐹 ∩𝐷. Transpositions inside the blocks, together with
transpositions joining adjacent blocks along retained edges, connect each component; they generate its full symmetric
group. This proves (1).
The coset 𝑔𝐻𝑒 maps to 𝑔𝐴𝑒. Two pairs of subsets are conjugate under 𝑆Ω exactly when their four Boolean atoms
have the same sizes, giving (A2.2). The sets 𝐴𝑒 are laminar: any two are nested or disjoint. Equation (A2.2) forces
the identical nesting and disjointness relations on any pairwise incident family (𝑋𝑒). Add Ω as a top container. From
each selected container subtract its maximal selected proper subcontainers. These differences partition Ω, and the
corresponding differences for the 𝐴𝑒 and 𝑋𝑒 have equal sizes. Bijections between these atoms combine into a single
permutation sending every selected 𝐴𝑒 to 𝑋𝑒. This proves (G3) for every partial flag.
Repeated use of (1) gives 𝐻𝐽 = 𝐾 𝐸∖𝐽 . Different edge sets give different component partitions and different groups,
since every block is nonempty and adding an edge allows a transposition between its two sides. Thus (G1) holds. If
at least two edge types remain, the groups obtained by deleting each remaining edge collectively contain 𝐾∅ and each
remaining one-edge group, proving (G2). The coset criterion gives the geometry assertions. Its residue group and
its subgroups factor over the forest components; all elements from different components are incident, which gives the
stated join and the kernel convention. ■
For unit blocks 𝐵𝑣 = {𝑣}, write 𝜏𝑒 for the endpoint transposition of 𝑒. The theorem says
𝐾𝐹 = ⟨𝜏 𝑒 ∶ 𝑒 ∈ 𝐹 ⟩ = ∏
𝐶
𝑆𝐶, 𝐾 ∅ = 1. (A2.3)
This is the unweighted model used for N001. The weighted formulation costs only a change of notation and will also
supply the flag completion for the parity construction.
Theorem P (sign-kernel extension). Let the blocks be singletons and distinguish an edge 𝑑, with 𝑠 = 𝜏 𝑑. For 𝑒 ≠ 𝑑 ,
choose 𝜖𝑒 ∈ {1, −1} and put
𝑟𝑑 = 𝑠, 𝑟 𝑒 = (𝑠𝜏 𝑒)𝜖𝑒. (A2.4)
For 𝑈 ⊆ 𝐸 , set 𝐹 (𝑈 ) = {𝑑} ∪ (𝑈 ∖ {𝑑}) . Then
⟨𝑟𝑒 ∶ 𝑒 ∈ 𝑈 ⟩ = { 𝐾𝐹 (𝑈), 𝑑 ∈ 𝑈 ,
𝐾𝐹 (𝑈) ∩ 𝐴Ω, 𝑑 ∉ 𝑈 . (A2.5)
Both the ordinary marking (𝜏𝑒) and (A2.4) generate 𝑆Ω, are independent, and satisfy the full subset intersection prop-
erty (A1.1) with 𝐵 = 1 .
Proof. Inverting an individual generator leaves its cyclic subgroup unchanged. If 𝑠 is included, 𝑠 and 𝑠𝜏𝑒 recover 𝜏𝑒,
proving the first case. The sign kernel of 𝐾𝐹 , for 𝑑 ∈ 𝐹 , is generated by the 𝑠𝜏𝑒, 𝑒 ∈ 𝐹 ∖ {𝑑} : every even word in
edge transpositions is a product of pairs, and
𝜏𝑒𝜏𝑓 = (𝑠𝜏 𝑒)−1(𝑠𝜏𝑓 ), 𝑠𝜏 𝑑 = 1.
This proves the second case, including the empty set, since 𝐾{𝑑} ∩ 𝐴Ω = 1 . Intersect (A2.5) using Theorem F and
the sign condition. Since 𝐹 (𝑈 ) ∩ 𝐹 (𝑉 ) = 𝐹 (𝑈 ∩ 𝑉 ) , the result is exactly the subgroup for 𝑈 ∩ 𝑉 . The same
conclusion for ordinary markings follows from (A2.3). Omitting an ordinary edge or a twisted label 𝑒 ≠ 𝑑 leaves its
cut invariant; the omitted generator crosses that cut. Omitting 𝑟𝑑 leaves only even permutations, whereas 𝑟𝑑 is odd.
These observations prove independence and the generation assertions. ■
5. Two-block overgroups and inductively minimal property tests
Lemma O (two-block overgroups). Let Ω = 𝐴 ⊔ 𝐶 , with 𝑎 = |𝐴| , 𝑐 = |𝐶| positive, and put 𝐻 = 𝑆 𝐴 × 𝑆𝐶. If
𝑎 ≠ 𝑐 , or 𝑎 = 𝑐 = 1 , then 𝐻 is maximal in 𝑆Ω. If 𝑎 = 𝑐 ≥ 2 , its only proper strict overgroup is the unordered
two-block stabilizer
𝑊 = (𝑆 𝐴 × 𝑆𝐶) ⋊ 𝐶2,
which is maximal and satisfies [𝑊 ∶ 𝐻] = 2 . In all cases the subgroup interval [𝐻, 𝑆Ω] is a chain. A positive weighted
tree has at most one edge whose two sides have equal total weight.
Proof. The case 𝑎 = 𝑐 = 1 is immediate. The group 𝐻 contains the transpositions within each of its two blocks. If
an overgroup element does not preserve their unordered partition, its conjugates of these transpositions include one
crossing the original blocks. Together with the original within-block transpositions the support graph is connected,
so these transpositions generate 𝑆Ω. This remains valid when one block is a singleton: a permutation moving that
singleton produces a cross-block conjugate from the other block. Thus a proper overgroup must preserve the unordered
partition. That stabilizer equals 𝐻 when the sizes differ and has index two over it when they agree. Its only further
overgroup is 𝑆Ω, by the same argument.
If an edge bisects the total weight, the side of any other edge away from it is a proper positive-weight subset of one of
its halves. It has strictly less than half the total weight and cannot be a bisecting side. ■
Proposition I (unweighted tree property profile). For a tree 𝑇 on 𝑁 ≥ 2 vertices, the geometry of Theorem F with
unit blocks has rank 𝑁 − 1 , group 𝑆𝑁 , and diagram 𝐿(𝑇 ), the line graph of 𝑇 . It is inductively minimal, satisfies
RWPRI, (2𝑇 )1, and (𝐼𝑃 )2, and has the following exact properties:
• PRI holds if no edge bisects 𝑁 , with the additional primitive case 𝑁 = 2 .
• Every element stabilizer is quasi-maximal. Failure of PRI is equivalent to a middle in 𝐿(𝑇 ).
• RPRI holds exactly when 𝑇 is a star, equivalently when 𝐿(𝑇 ) is complete.
Proof. Equation (A2.3) gives trivial chamber stabilizer and two elements in each panel; the panel group is 𝑆2, proving
thinness and (2𝑇 )1. A two-edge forest consists either of a three-vertex path or of two disjoint edges. The corresponding
rank-two residue is respectively a six-cycle or a complete bipartite graph 𝐾2,2. Consequently the diagram is 𝐿(𝑇 ) and
(𝐼𝑃 )2 holds.
Each connected remaining edge set with 𝑚 edges is a subtree with 𝑚 + 1 vertices; its residue group is 𝑆𝑚+1, while
all other vertices are isolated and contribute trivial factors. This verifies inductive minimality. In any nonempty forest
residue choose a leaf of a nontrivial component. Its edge cut gives a point stabilizer in that component’s symmetric
group. This is maximal, and multiplying by the other factors preserves maximality. Thus RWPRI holds.
Lemma O gives the global PRI and quasi-maximality tests. Removing the vertex 𝑒 from 𝐿(𝑇 ) leaves the line graphs of
the two sides of 𝑇 − 𝑒 , with respective cardinalities 𝑎 − 1 and 𝑁 − 𝑎 − 1; an empty side of this diagram corresponds
to a leaf cut. A middle therefore exists exactly when an edge has 𝑎 = 𝑁 − 𝑎 ≥ 2 , which is exactly failure of PRI. For
𝑁 = 2 there is no middle and the pair is primitive.
Every nontrivial subtree of a star is again a star, and every edge cut of a star is a leaf cut, so its residues are PRI. A
nonstar tree contains a three-edge path. The residue on those three edges is an 𝑆4 geometry with a 2 + 2 middle cut;
its stabilizer 𝑆2 × 𝑆2 is not maximal by Lemma O. Thus RPRI fails. Finally, a tree has pairwise adjacent edges exactly
when it is a star, proving the line-graph formulation. ■
Lemma D (diagram recognition). Every connected graph satisfying the three diagram conditions in B97 is the line
graph of a tree, unique up to isomorphism. Hence B97 identifies every inductively minimal pair with one of Proposition
I.
Proof. For a diagram with at least two vertices, make a vertex for each maximal clique. A diagram vertex in two
maximal cliques gives an edge between the corresponding clique vertices; a diagram vertex in one maximal clique
gives an edge from its clique vertex to a new leaf. Two distinct maximal cliques meet in at most one vertex, since a
shared edge would violate the unique-clique condition. Thus the resulting graph has no loops or parallel edges. It is
connected, and its edge labels are the original diagram vertices, with exactly their original adjacencies.
A shortest cycle of length at least four in this graph would give an induced cycle of that length on its edge labels in
the diagram. A triangle would give three pairwise adjacent edge labels, which extend to a maximal clique; its three
pairwise edges would then violate uniqueness of the maximal cliques already represented at the three cycle vertices.
Both cases are impossible. The constructed graph is therefore a tree. Conversely the maximal cliques in the line graph
of a tree are its nontrivial edge stars, so this construction recovers the tree. A one-vertex diagram corresponds separately
to the one-edge tree. Proposition I constructs an inductively minimal pair for every such diagram; the uniqueness in
B97 identifies it with the given pair. ■
6. Extremal symmetric markings
Lemma M (the two-point-block obstruction). If 𝑚 ≥ 4 and 𝐻 ≤ 𝐶 2 ≀ 𝑆𝑚 is transitive on its 2𝑚 points, then 𝐻
cannot have an independent generating family of size 2𝑚 − 2.
Proof. Let 𝑁 be the block kernel, a subspace of 𝔽𝑚
2 , and let 𝑃 = 𝐻/𝑁 ≤ 𝑆 𝑚 be the transitive block action. W1
bounds 𝜇(𝑃 ) ≤ 𝑚 − 1 . We first have 𝜇′
𝐻(𝑁 ) ≤ 𝑚 − 1 . This is automatic if 𝑁 is proper. If 𝑁 = 𝔽 𝑚
2 and
an 𝐻-independent family had 𝑚 elements, it would be a vector-space basis. For each omitted basis vector, the span
of the orbits of the others is proper but contains their hyperplane, so that hyperplane is invariant. Intersecting these
hyperplanes shows that every basis line is invariant. Over 𝔽2 every such line is pointwise fixed, contradicting the
nontrivial transitive block action.
If an independent generating family of size 2𝑚 − 2 existed, Theorem R would give
2𝑚 − 2 ≤ 𝜇(𝐻) ≤ 𝜇(𝑃 ) + 𝜇 ′
𝐻(𝑁 ) ≤ 2𝑚 − 2.
Equality forces 𝜇(𝑃 ) = 𝑚 − 1 ; applying W1 to that family in 𝑆𝑚 gives 𝑃 = 𝑆 𝑚. Every nonconstant vector 𝑣
in an 𝑆𝑚-invariant subspace generates a submodule containing the even-weight space: for coordinates with different
entries, 𝑣 + (𝑖𝑗)𝑣 = 𝑒 𝑖 + 𝑒𝑗, and the orbit of this vector spans the even-weight space. That space has codimension one.
Therefore an 𝑆𝑚-independent family containing 𝑣 can have at most one further member; a family of constant vectors
has at most one member. Consequently 𝜇′
𝐻(𝑁 ) ≤ 2 , and (A4.2) now gives 2𝑚 − 2 ≤ 𝑚 + 1 , contradicting 𝑚 ≥ 4 .
■
Theorem E (extremal recognition). Assume 𝑛 ≥ 7 . Every independent family of 𝑛 − 1elements of 𝑆𝑛 is an ordinary
tree marking (A2.3) or a sign-kernel marking (A2.4). If the family is independent relative to 𝐵 ≤ 𝑆 𝑛, then 𝐵 = 1 .
Proof. By W1 the family generates 𝑆𝑛. For its omission groups 𝐺𝑖, W2 and Lemma M exclude the two-point-block
case: in that case 𝑛 = 2𝑚 ≥ 8 , and the remaining 𝑛 − 2 generators are an independent generating family for 𝐺𝑖. At
most one 𝐺𝑖 is 𝐴𝑛, because its omitted generator must be the unique odd member. Label a possible exception by 1.
For each 𝑖 > 1 , choose an edge 𝑒𝑖 = {𝑥, 𝑠 𝑖(𝑥)} crossing two 𝐺𝑖-orbits. Such an edge exists, since otherwise every
generator would preserve those orbits. Choose 𝑒1 from any moved point of𝑠1. If all the omission groups are intransitive,
choose 𝑒1 crossing its own omission orbits as well. For 𝑗 ≠ 𝑖 , the edge 𝑒𝑗 lies inside a 𝐺𝑖-orbit because 𝑠𝑗 ∈ 𝐺 𝑖. Thus
a cycle cannot contain 𝑒𝑖 for 𝑖 > 1 : the remaining edges would give a path within a 𝐺𝑖-orbit between its endpoints. A
cycle cannot consist of 𝑒1 alone. The 𝑛 − 1 edges therefore form a tree on the 𝑛 points.
Deleting 𝑒𝑖, for 𝑖 > 1 , leaves two connected sets each contained in a 𝐺𝑖-orbit; intransitivity makes them exactly its
two orbits. Hence 𝑠𝑗 preserves this cut whenever 𝑗 ≠ 𝑖 . Intersect these cut stabilizers using Theorem F. It follows
that 𝑠1 ∈ 𝐾 {𝑒1}, so 𝑠1 = 𝜏 𝑒1
, and that 𝑠𝑖 ∈ 𝐾 {𝑒1,𝑒𝑖}. If every omission group is intransitive, include the first cut
too to obtain 𝑠𝑖 = 𝜏 𝑒𝑖
for every 𝑖. Otherwise 𝐺1 = 𝐴 𝑛, and all other generators are nonidentity even elements of the
two-edge group. For adjacent edges that group is 𝑆3, whose nonidentity even elements are the two inverse three-cycles;
for disjoint edges it is 𝐶2 × 𝐶2, with a unique nonidentity even element. These are precisely (𝑠1𝜏𝑒𝑖
)±1, giving (A2.4).
Now suppose the original family is independent relative to 𝐵. It is independent in the ordinary sense, so the preceding
classification applies. By Theorems F and P its omission groups are the tree-cut stabilizers, except that in the sign-
kernel case the distinguished omission group is 𝐴𝑛. At each maximal omission group independence forces 𝐵 ≤ 𝐺 𝑖;
otherwise ⟨𝐵, 𝐺𝑖⟩ = 𝑆 𝑛. A pendant edge different from the distinguished edge exists and yields a point stabilizer, so
𝐵 fixes a point. At a nonmaximal cut, Lemma O forces 𝐵 into the unordered two-block stabilizer. Its elements outside
the ordered stabilizer interchange the halves and fix no point. Thus 𝐵 ≤ 𝐺 𝑖 here also. Strong independence from
Theorems F and P gives 𝐵 ≤ ⋂𝑖 𝐺𝑖 = 1. ■
7. Recognition of all maximum-rank symmetric geometries
Theorem G (geometry recognition). For 𝑛 ≥ 7 , each marking in Theorem E gives an RWPRI coset geometry with its
omission subgroups. Conversely every source coset geometry of 𝑆𝑛 of rank 𝑛 − 1 is one of these, and has trivial Borel
subgroup. Among RWPRI geometries the correspondence is bijective after simultaneous conjugation and independent
inversion of the marking generators; sets are unordered, so type renaming is understood.
Proof of existence. Strong independence and generation give (G1) and (G2). Theorem F supplies (G3) for ordinary
markings, and the leaf argument of Proposition I supplies (G4).
For a sign-kernel marking, contract𝑑 to a vertex of weight two. Its other omission groups are precisely the cut stabilizers
of this weighted tree, while the distinguished omission group is 𝐴𝑛. Theorem F gives a common representative for
the cut types of any flag. Every intersection of these cut stabilizers contains the odd transposition 𝑠. Multiplying the
representative on the right by 𝑠 changes its parity without changing any of its cut cosets, so it can satisfy the parity
coset too. This proves (G3).
To prove (G4), if parity is not yet fixed, choose that type: the even subgroup has index two in the residue group,
because the latter contains 𝑠. If parity is fixed, the residue group is the even part of a product of forest symmetric
groups, and the component containing 𝑑 still contains that edge. If another component has an edge, choose a leaf cut
there. Projection from the residue group onto that component’s symmetric group is surjective, since the 𝑑-component
supplies an odd correction. The new stabilizer is the inverse image of a maximal point stabilizer, so is maximal. If only
the 𝑑-component is nontrivial and a type remains, it has at least three vertices and a leaf other than the two endpoints
considered together as the distinguished block. More explicitly, every tree with at least three vertices has a leaf not
belonging to a specified edge’s two endpoints unless that edge is the whole tree; such a leaf supplies a remaining cut.
The group on this component is its alternating group. Its natural action is primitive: for degree at least four it is two-
transitive (adjust the parity of a map of an ordered pair by swapping two remaining points), and 𝐴3 is regular of prime
degree. The leaf stabilizer is again maximal. This proves (G4).
Proof of uniqueness in the RWPRI case. Theorem R and then Theorem E give 𝐵 = 1 , a classified marking, and
equality (A4.4) for all parabolics. Each panel group is cyclic of order two or three. A generator of it is therefore unique
up to inversion. Moving the chosen chamber conjugates all stabilizers and generators together; renaming types reorders
the set. Conversely those operations give the same group–geometry pair. Here isomorphisms intertwine the specified
𝑆𝑛-action, up to an automorphism of 𝑆𝑛; for 𝑛 ≥ 7 all such automorphisms are inner, an elementary symmetric-group
fact retained for this formulation. Equivalently one may use the source convention of conjugate coset data throughout.
Proof for arbitrary maximum-rank geometries. This step does not assume (G4). Choose 𝑠𝑖 as in (A4.3); they are
independent relative to 𝐵. W1 gives ordinary generation and Theorem E gives 𝐵 = 1 and the two marking families.
Let 𝐺𝑖 = ⟨𝑠 𝑗 ∶ 𝑗 ≠ 𝑖⟩ . Then
𝐺𝑖 ≤ 𝐻 𝑖 < 𝑆 𝑛.
Lemma O and the maximality of 𝐴𝑛 show that 𝐻𝑖 = 𝐺 𝑖, except possibly at one balanced cut 𝑘, where 𝐻𝑘 might be
its unordered stabilizer 𝑊𝑘. Every other tree edge has a nonempty cut side wholly in one 𝑘-half. Its stabilizer cannot
interchange those halves, so
𝑊𝑘 ∩ 𝐺𝑗 ≤ 𝐺 𝑘 (A5.1)
for every such cut type 𝑗 ≠ 𝑘 .
Consider a panel intersection ⋂𝑗≠𝑖 𝐻𝑗. If it omits 𝑘, it is already the corresponding intersection of the 𝐺𝑗. If it contains
𝑘, it also contains another cut type 𝑗 ≠ 𝑖, 𝑘 , since there are 𝑛 − 1 ≥ 6 types and at most one is a parity type. Equation
(A5.1) again lets us replace 𝑊𝑘 by 𝐺𝑘. All panels therefore coincide with those of the marking and equal ⟨𝑠𝑖⟩. Starting
from panels and using (G2) downwards now gives 𝐻𝐽 = ⟨𝑠 𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∉ 𝐽 ⟩ for every 𝐽 . In particular every 𝐻𝑖 = 𝐺 𝑖; the
geometry is one already proved RWPRI. ■
8. Deletion maps, compatible products and cyclic edge kernels
Lemma Q2.2 (Boolean deletion). Suppose every assignment 𝑒𝑆(𝑟𝑖) = 𝑟 𝑖 for 𝑖 ∈ 𝑆 , 𝑒𝑆(𝑟𝑖) = 1 otherwise, extends
to an endomorphism. Then 𝐺 has IP, 𝑒𝑆𝑒𝑇 = 𝑒 𝑆∩𝑇 , and
𝐺 = ker(𝑒𝑆) ⋊ 𝐺𝑆, ker(𝑒𝑆) = ⟨ ⟨𝑟𝑖 ∶ 𝑖 ∉ 𝑆⟩ ⟩.
Proof. Endomorphisms agreeing on the generators agree everywhere. The image and fixed subgroup of 𝑒𝑆 are 𝐺𝑆.
For 𝑥 ∈ 𝐺 𝑆 ∩ 𝐺𝑇 , 𝑥 = 𝑒 𝑆(𝑥) ∈ 𝑒 𝑆(𝐺𝑇 ) = 𝐺 𝑆∩𝑇 . The kernel contains the displayed normal closure. Quotienting
by that closure leaves the retained generators, and the induced retraction fixes them; hence its kernel is trivial. Splitting
follows because 𝑒𝑆|𝐺𝑆
= 1. ■
The FAP at a face or coface is precisely the corresponding split product with the normal closure of a final or initial
interval of generators. Thus Boolean deletion implies every such FAP, including those in the contract.
Theorem Q2.3 (compatible products). Let 𝐴, 𝐵 have Boolean deletions, with label sets overlapping in𝑆, and identify
their 𝑆-parabolics by the marking as 𝐶. Their deletion maps give 𝑝𝐴 ∶ 𝐴 → 𝐶 and 𝑝𝐵 ∶ 𝐵 → 𝐶 . Then
𝑃 = 𝐴 × 𝐶 𝐵 = {(𝑎, 𝑏) ∶ 𝑝 𝐴(𝑎) = 𝑝 𝐵(𝑏)} = ( ker 𝑝𝐴 × ker 𝑝𝐵) ⋊ 𝐶
is generated by the common diagonal generators and the exclusive generators in their respective coordinates. It has
Boolean deletions, embeds both marked factors, and, if finite, |𝑃 | = |𝐴||𝐵|/|𝐶| . Two generators appearing together
in neither factor commute.
Proof. The indicated generators include the section 𝑎 ↦ (𝑎, 𝑝 𝐴(𝑎)), its analogue for 𝐵, and therefore all
(1, 𝑏𝑝𝐵(𝑏)−1). Equivalently the exclusive generators normally generate each kernel, by Lemma Q2.2. They generate
the displayed semidirect product. Coordinatewise deletion preserves compatibility: both separator images are deleted
on exactly 𝑆 ∩ 𝐼 . Lemma Q2.2 gives IP, and the sections, order formula and commutation assertions are immediate.

We will also use a non-split variant with cyclic kernels. Let𝐵 have IP and retractions to chosen two-generator subgroups
𝐵𝑒 = ⟨𝑏 𝑖, 𝑏𝑗⟩, killing all other markers. Suppose 𝑞𝑒 = |𝑏 𝑖𝑏𝑗| is even. For each chosen pair choose a multiple 𝑚𝑒 and
a dihedral group 𝐷𝑒 = ⟨𝑢 𝑒, 𝑣𝑒⟩ of order 2𝑚𝑒, mapping by its reflections to 𝐵𝑒. Form the simultaneous fiber product
of 𝐵 and the 𝐷𝑒 over these maps, with the evident marked tuples.
Theorem Q2.4 (independent edge inflation). These tuples generate the whole fiber product 𝐻, with
|𝐻| = |𝐵| ∏

It has IP. Chosen pair orders become 𝑚𝑒, and all other pair orders remain their base values. Thus string diagrams give
string C-groups.
Proof. The word (𝑟𝑖𝑟𝑗)𝑞𝑒 is a generator of the 𝑒-kernel and trivial in every other coordinate: another chosen pair
contains at most one of its endpoints, so evenness kills that reflection. These words generate the entire kernel, while
the tuples project onto 𝐵. The same word lies in 𝐻𝐼 when 𝑒 ⊆ 𝐼 . Otherwise its coordinate in 𝐻𝐼 is at most a single
reflection subgroup and meets the rotation kernel trivially. This proves (Q2.1); its coordinate intersection law and

Its order divides the base pair order because its projection onto 𝐵𝑒 has that same order. An odd-order base pair cannot
meet a chosen edge at exactly one endpoint, since the retraction would map its product to an involution. Hence the
extra coordinates cannot increase an unchosen pair order. ■
Finally, if a marked presentation 𝑃 has finite normal subgroups 𝐴1, … , 𝐴ℎ of orders at most 𝑎𝑖, and its quotient by
their product has at most 𝑏 elements, then |𝑃 | ≤ 𝑏 ∏ 𝑖 𝑎𝑖. A constructed marked quotient of exactly that order identifies
the presentation itself. The proof uses successive product cardinalities; it does not assume the normal factors commute
or intersect trivially. We call this the order comparison principle.

Indeed reduction modulo 2ℤ2 makes the adjacent squares central; conversely [[𝑎, 𝑏], 𝑐] and [[𝑏, 𝑐], 𝑎] give the two
doubled unit translations, up to signs. Thus 𝐻5 is class two of order 32. The squared translations are not always
independent cyclic subgroups: in 𝐻6 they coincide and have order two. Formula (Q3.2) uses a lattice index and does
not make the false independence assertion.
Lemma Q3.1 (square-map recognition). Every finite two-group string C-group of type{4, 4} and order 2𝑛 is marked-
isomorphic to 𝐻𝑛; necessarily 𝑛 ≥ 5 .
Proof. The two dihedral parabolics have orders eight and intersect in order two, so their product already has 32 ele-
ments. The universal group (Q3.1) maps onto the given group. IP gives irredundancy, so Theorem S gives elementary
abelianization of rank three. Hence word parity is a homomorphism onto 𝐶2, and the kernel of (Q3.1) lies in the
subgroup of orientation-preserving plane isometries. It contains no nonidentity torsion: a finite-order plane isometry
fixes a point; the square tiling moves that point into a closed fundamental right triangle; its stabilizer is a subgroup of a
vertex dihedral parabolic or an edge reflection group. Each embeds by the prescribed pair orders and IP. An orientation-
preserving plane isometry without finite order is a translation. Thus the kernel is a 𝐷8-invariant sublattice of ℤ2 of
index 2𝑛−3. Identifying the lattice with an ideal of ℤ[𝑖], its generator has norm a power of two. Unique factorization
(from the Euclidean algorithm) makes it a unit times a power of 1 + 𝑖 . Even powers give the square lattice and odd
powers the diagonal lattice displayed above. There is exactly one such ideal at each index, proving the assertion. ■
An oriented square model for the later cover criterion
Lemma L1 (the square linear-part quotient). In the square tessellation with vertices ℤ[𝑖], the rotations 𝑢 ∶ 𝑧 ↦ 𝑖𝑧 ,
𝑣 ∶ 𝑧 ↦ 𝑖𝑧 + 1 are rotations about a vertex and an adjacent face centre. Their centre coordinates are 0 and (1 + 𝑖)/2,
respectively, and (𝑢𝑣)2 = 1 . They generate the full orientation-preserving grid group: their quotient supplies a unit
translation, and conjugation by the quarter-turn supplies the other unit translation. For a nonzero ideal 𝐽 = (𝑏 + 𝑖𝑐) ,
the marked quotient is (ℤ[𝑖]/𝐽 ) ⋊ 𝐶4. It is the usual square-map model whenever the source parameters specify such
a map. The geometric interpretation is the literal quotient of the tessellation, not an inference from its order.
The linear-part map ℓ sends both marked generators to 𝑖, and is injective on each of their cyclic subgroups: both
generators have fourth power one and linear part of exact order four. If the universal mirror sends (𝑢, 𝑣) to (𝑢−1, 𝑢2𝑣),
then ℓ𝜇 is inversion composed with ℓ. Thus both the relation kernel and its mirror lie in the universal kernel of ℓ;
the chirality subgroup is contained in the translation subgroup. In particular it meets ⟨𝑣⟩ trivially. Here the chirality
subgroup is the image of the product of the relation kernel and its mirror, as defined precisely in §22.1. The argument
uses only the regular marked quotient 𝐶4, and does not claim that a one-cell quotient is an abstract polytope. ■
Cubical and halved lattices in every dimension
Let r≥2 and let L be the r-dimensional cross-polytope with vertices labelled +e_i,−e_i, 1≤i≤r. A proper face of L
chooses at most one vertex from each antipodal pair. Write Q_r for the cubical tessellation of R^r whose vertices are
Z^r and whose cells are products of unit intervals and integer singletons. The quotient Q_r/4Z^r has a formal least and
greatest face adjoined when viewed as an abstract polytope. These formal faces are also understood below.
For each pair of binary coordinates belonging to +e_i,−e_i, use the Gray map
(0,0) ↦ 0, (1,0) ↦ 1, (1,1) ↦ 2, (0,1) ↦ 3 in Z/4Z. (T1)
Lemma T1. The product of these maps is a rank-preserving face-poset isomorphism 2^L ≅ Q_r/4Z^r. Binary weight
modulo two becomes ∑_i x_i modulo two in the cubical coordinates.
Proof. In Danzer’s construction a proper face of 2^L allows the bits indexed by a face of L to vary, and fixes every
other bit. Thus in any antipodal pair either both bits are fixed or exactly one bit varies. Under (T1), varying a single bit
describes precisely one edge of the four-cycle: its four possible edges arise from the two choices of varying bit and the
two values of the fixed bit. A face therefore becomes a product of four-cycle vertices and edges. Conversely every such
product uniquely specifies the varying bit in each free pair and the remaining fixed bits. Its dimension is the number
of free pairs. Inclusion is preserved and reflected coordinatewise, and formal faces match. These products are exactly
the cubical cells modulo 4Z^r, with their incidence. The four cases of (T1) have binary parities 0,1,0,1 respectively,
proving the last assertion. ■
This is a face-poset identification, rather than an inference from orders. It also handles the dual in the target source
explicitly. For a cubical face F with free coordinate set S, write its free intervals as [m_i,m_i+1] for i∈S and its fixed
coordinates as m_j for j∉S. The dual face F* has fixed coordinate m_i+1/2 for i∈S and free interval [m_j−1/2,m_j+1/2]
for j∉S. This reverses containment: whenever an endpoint is fixed in a subface of F, the corresponding free interval
of the dual contains the fixed midpoint of F*. It is a bijection onto the shifted cubical tessellation, with dimensions
adding to r. Subtracting (1/2,…,1/2) identifies that tessellation with Q_r. The construction commutes with translations
by 4Z^r, and consequently induces an order-reversing bijection on the quotient. Thus for the r-cube K=L*, the source’s
polytope (2^{K*})* is indeed the cubical toroid {4,3^(r−2),4}_(4,0,…,0). The string is {4,4} when r=2. Reversing
the source marking moves its last-end halving to first-end halving of 2^L. Type reversal is kept explicit; no unproved
compatibility of a chosen self-duality with halving is needed.
The marking and the exact affine quotient
Use the following reflections of R^r:
a(x_1,x_2,…,x_r) = (1−x_1,x_2,…,x_r),
b_i swaps x_i and x_(i+1) (1≤i<r),
c(x_1,…,x_(r−1),x_r) = (x_1,…,x_(r−1),−x_r). (T2)
They generate W=Z^r⋊B_r, where B_r is the signed permutation group. Indeed the b_i,c generate B_r, including the
reflection x_1↦−x_1; its product with a is translation by ±e_1. Signed permutations then supply every integer transla-
tion. The simplex 1/2≥x_1≥x_2≥⋯≥x_r≥0 is a fundamental chamber. One way to verify this without a presentation
theorem is to translate each coordinate to [−1/2,1/2], make it nonnegative, and order the coordinates; a point with
strict inequalities has a unique such representative. Its reflected walls are the displayed generators. Its vertices are the
barycentres of a nested flag of cubical faces, so its chamber system is the barycentric subdivision of Q_r.
The marking in (T2) also agrees with Danzer’s marking under (T1). Flipping the +e_1 bit induces x_1↦1−x_1 modulo
four; interchanging antipodal pairs induces the b_i; swapping the two bits in the last pair induces x_r↦−x_r. These
are the usual base vertex/base flag generators of 2^L. Hence the isomorphism of Lemma T1 identifies the marked
action, not only the unmarked face poset. The action of W on Q_r/4Z^r has kernel exactly Λ=4Z^r: an affine signed
permutation acting trivially on all vertices has translation zero modulo four, and its linear part is the identity modulo
four; testing the unit vectors forces that signed permutation to be the identity.
Put b=b_1 and replace a by
d=aba: (x_1,x_2,x_3,…,x_r)
↦(1−x_2,1−x_1,x_3,…,x_r). (T3)
Let E={v∈Z^r : ∑v_i is even}.
Lemma T2. H=⟨d,b_1,…,b_(r−1),c⟩ is exactly E⋊B_r. It has index two in W and contains Λ. Its fundamental chamber
is
x_1≥x_2≥⋯≥x_r≥0, x_1+x_2≤1. (T4)
Its chamber system is the halved cubical geometry, and the marked finite halving group and geometry are H/Λ and the
quotient of that chamber system by Λ, respectively.
Proof. Every displayed generator preserves the parity of an integral vertex’s coordinate sum, so H≤E⋊B_r. It contains
B_r. The product of d with the B_r-reflection (x_1,x_2)↦(−x_2,−x_1) is translation by e_1+e_2. Conjugating by
B_r gives every ±e_i±e_j. These span E: subtract multiples of e_i−e_r, i<r, to leave an even multiple of e_r, and use
2e_r=(e_r+e_1)+(e_r−e_1). This works also for r=2. Thus equality holds and the index is two. Since every 4e_i is in
E, Λ≤H.
The region (T4) is the union of the preceding W chamber and its reflection in the a-wall x_1=1/2. On their common
wall the two chambers glue; the other wall x_1=x_2 is paired with its a-image x_1+x_2=1. All other walls agree.
Because W=H⊔Ha and the interiors of W chambers are disjoint, these doubled chambers tile R^r under H, each once.
Their wall reflections are exactly d,b_1,…,b_(r−1),c. Equivalently, their chamber labels are the old cubical flags with
the initial vertex chosen in one parity class, pairing the two old flags differing only at that vertex. The two ends of
the old edge become the two new vertex types; the higher faces retain their type. This is precisely the graph-halving
construction of §18.
Finally, translations in Λ preserve both vertex colors and take cubical faces to cubical faces. The same flag pairing
therefore commutes with Λ. Every finite chamber has a lift, and two lifts yield the same finite chamber exactly when
they differ by Λ. Wall adjacencies are the marked reflections modulo Λ. The maximal parabolics are the images of the
corresponding wall subgroups, so the typed coset geometry also agrees. Regularity, thinness and residual connected-
ness of the finite geometry follow from the already proved halving theorem; they are not inferred solely from being a
group quotient. ■
For r≥3, the walls (T4) have the affine fork diagram B̃_r: d and b_1 commute; both meet b_2 with product order three,
successive b_i have order three, and b_(r−1)c has order four. All other distinct wall pairs commute. This follows directly
from (T2)–(T3), or from their mirror angles. Thus its rank-(r+1) quotient has the source’s type {4,3^(r−3),3/3}. Here
the quotient lattice is exactly 4Z^r in the original coordinates; the subscript (4,0,…,0) denotes the lattice generated by
that vector’s signed-permutation orbit. This proves the source’s all-rank toroidal identification, including the marking.
Its group order is [E:4Z^r]|B_r|=(4r/2)2r r!=2^(3r−1)r!, agreeing with the separate parity-module calculation. The order
is a check, not the proof.
The coordinate convention can be checked directly against Ens’s primary paper: its equations (3.1), (4.1) are (T2), (T3)
for r=3; its §2 definition of Λ_s uses the signed-permutation orbit of s, and Theorem 4.3 denotes the corresponding
Coxeter-complex quotient by that lattice. Thus s=(4,0,0) here is literally the same parameter. The target extends this
convention to larger r. No classification theorem about all invariant lattices is needed. Ens, Rank 4 toroidal hypertopes,
pp.70–75.
The rank-three square map and its changed lattice basis
When r=2, reorder the halved types as (even vertex, old square, odd vertex). An old unit square joins its two even
corners as an edge of the new map; the four even neighbors of an old odd vertex bound a new square face. This
describes the ordinary square tessellation with vertex lattice E and edge directions
f_1=(1,1), f_2=(−1,1). (T5)
Every even vertex has four such incident edges, every displayed quadrilateral is a face, and the incidences are exactly
those of halving. Thus this is an isomorphism of maps, not a comparison of their counts. In this basis
4e_1=2f_1−2f_2, 4e_2=2f_1+2f_2.
The quotient lattice has basis (2,−2),(2,2) in square coordinates. Negating the first vector gives the customary pair
(−2,2),(2,2), so the map is {4,4}_(2,2). Lemma T1 and the explicit duality already identify the original map as
{4,4}_(4,0). This completes both p=4 names in the N013 polygon contract.

To identify the rank-three presentation used in L035’s proof as well, add [(𝑎𝑏)4, 𝑐] = [𝑎, (𝑏𝑐) 4] = 1 to the type
