order comparison principle identifies the presentation with that model. Thus no source presentation-order assertion
has been smuggled into the seed.
11. The canonical class-two quotient from graded support
Lemma Q5.1 (descending lift). Let 𝐺 be finite nilpotent and let 𝐴 ∩ 𝐵 = 1 . Put 𝐷𝑗 = 𝛾 𝑗(𝐺), 𝐴𝑗 = 𝐴 ∩ 𝐷 𝑗,
𝐵𝑗 = 𝐵 ∩ 𝐷 𝑗. If the images of 𝐴𝑗, 𝐵𝑗 generate 𝐷𝑗/𝐷𝑗+1 for every 𝑗 ≥ 𝑚 , then 𝐷𝑗 = 𝐴 𝑗𝐵𝑗 for every 𝑗 ≥ 𝑚 .
Proof. Descend from a trivial term. Assuming 𝐷𝑗+1 = 𝐴 𝑗+1𝐵𝑗+1, if 𝑎 ∈ 𝐴 𝑗, 𝑏 ∈ 𝐵 𝑗 have equal images in the next
quotient, write 𝑎𝑏−1 = 𝑎 ′𝑏′ with 𝑎′ ∈ 𝐴 𝑗+1, 𝑏′ ∈ 𝐵 𝑗+1. Then 𝑎′−1𝑎 = 𝑏 ′𝑏 ∈ 𝐴 ∩ 𝐵 , so both images are trivial.
Their images therefore form an internal direct sum in the abelian quotient. Counting gives |𝐷𝑗| = |𝐴 𝑗||𝐵𝑗| = |𝐴 𝑗𝐵𝑗|,
proving equality as sets. No prior normality or permutability of 𝐴, 𝐵 is assumed. ■
Lemma Q5.2 (graded support). Let a positively graded Lie algebra be generated in degree one by disjoint sets 𝑋, 𝑌 ,
with subalgebras 𝐿𝑋, 𝐿𝑌 . If [𝑥, 𝑦] ∈ 𝐿 𝑌 for 𝑥 ∈ 𝑋, 𝑦 ∈ 𝑌 , then 𝐿 = 𝐿 𝑋 + 𝐿𝑌 , degree by degree.
Proof. The derivation ad 𝑥 preserves 𝐿𝑌 because it does so on its generators. Iterated brackets of those derivations
also preserve 𝐿𝑌 , by Jacobi. Hence [𝐿𝑋, 𝐿𝑌 ] ⊆ 𝐿 𝑌 , and 𝐿𝑋 + 𝐿𝑌 is a Lie subalgebra containing all generators. The
argument holds in characteristic two. ■
Theorem Q5.3 (forest quotient). Suppose 𝐺 is a finite two-group generated by involutions 𝑟𝑖, with marked IP, and its
noncommuting pairs form a forest𝑇 on 𝑑 vertices and 𝑚 edges. Then edge commutators are independent in 𝐺′/𝛾3(𝐺),
and 𝐺/𝛾3(𝐺) is the marked group
(𝑣, 𝑧)(𝑤, 𝑡) = (𝑣 + 𝑤, 𝑧 + 𝑡 + 𝛽(𝑣, 𝑤)), 𝑣, 𝑤 ∈ 𝔽 𝑑
2, 𝑧, 𝑡 ∈ 𝔽 𝑚
2 , (Q5.1)
where, after orienting each edge, its component of 𝛽 is 𝑣head𝑤tail. Its order is 2𝑑+𝑚.
Proof. IP gives irredundancy. Theorem S gives 𝐺/𝐺′ = 𝐶 𝑑
2 and 𝐺′ ∩ ⟨𝑟𝑖⟩ = 1 . The lower-central graded Lie ring
is generated by these degree-one elements; all its graded components are 𝔽2-spaces by bilinearity. Edge commutators
span degree two. If they satisfy a nonzero linear relation, root each forest component and choose a participating edge
whose child endpoint has maximal depth in its component. Removing it separates the descendant set 𝑋 from all other
vertices 𝑌 , and no other participating edge is internal to 𝑋. The relation therefore writes the only nonzero cross bracket
as a sum of brackets internal to 𝑌 . Lemma Q5.2 shows every graded component is generated by pure 𝑋- and pure
𝑌 -brackets. Their representatives lie in 𝐺𝑋 ∩ 𝛾𝑗(𝐺) and 𝐺𝑌 ∩ 𝛾𝑗(𝐺).
Actual IP gives 𝐺𝑋 ∩ 𝐺 𝑌 = 1 , so Lemma Q5.1 gives 𝐺′ = (𝐺 ′ ∩ 𝐺 𝑋)(𝐺′ ∩ 𝐺 𝑌 ). For the crossing endpoints
𝑥 ∈ 𝑋, 𝑦 ∈ 𝑌 , write [𝑟𝑥, 𝑟𝑦] = 𝑢𝑣 accordingly. Then 𝑢 ∈ 𝐺 𝑋 ∩ 𝐺{𝑥}∪𝑌 = ⟨𝑟 𝑥⟩, whence 𝑢 = 1 ; next [𝑟𝑥, 𝑟𝑦] ∈
𝐺𝑌 ∩ 𝐺{𝑥,𝑦} = ⟨𝑟 𝑦⟩, so it too is one. This contradicts that the edge is noncommuting. Independence follows.
Bilinearity makes (Q5.1) associative; its coordinate markers are involutions with independent central edge commutators.
It is generated by those markers, and its parabolic on 𝐼 consists exactly of vertex coordinates supported on 𝐼 and edge
coordinates supported on edges internal to 𝐼. Thus it has IP. Collection in the corresponding class-two presentation
gives at most 2𝑑+𝑚 elements, and this coordinate model attains the bound. Independence in 𝐺′/𝛾3(𝐺) gives the same
order for 𝐺/𝛾3(𝐺); hence the marked map is an isomorphism. ■
Corollary Q5.4 (F013 1.1–1.2). For every finite nondegenerate two-group string C-group of rank 𝑑 ≥ 2 , 𝐺/𝛾3(𝐺) ≅
𝐺(𝐶𝑑), of order 22𝑑−1; the associated polytope covers 𝐶𝑑.
Proof. Its diagram is a path, so 𝑚 = 𝑑 − 1 . Conder’s marked presentation has involutions, distant commutation, adja-
cent order four, and adjacent squares central. The model (Q5.1) realizes that presentation with the maximal collected
order, identifying it with 𝐺(𝐶𝑑). The retained marked covering correspondence gives the geometric assertion. This
also proves uniqueness of the minimal object in the declared finite nondegenerate class. ■
Notice that no intermediate quotient was assumed to retain IP. Lemma Q5.1 is the step that prevents that circular
assumption.
A single finite-kernel classification for every rank and excess
Theorem Q5.5 (universal kernel classification). Let T be a forest on the specified d vertices, with m edges, and let
k≥0. Define F_T by involutory generators x_i, imposing [x_i,x_j]=1 for every pair not joined by an edge, and put
𝑈 = 𝐹 𝑇 /𝛾𝑘+3(𝐹𝑇 ), 𝐾 = 𝛾 3(𝑈 ). (Q5.2)
Then U is a finite two-group, its original marking has Boolean deletion maps and IP, and its class-two quotient is the
coordinate group Q_T of (Q5.1). The following objects are in bijection:
1. Ordered marked finite two-groups G with IP, noncommutation graph exactly T, and order 2^(d+m+k).
2. Normal subgroups N of U contained in K, with [K:N]=2^k, for which the subgroups
𝐿𝑆 = (𝑁 𝐾𝑆)/𝑁 ≤ 𝐾/𝑁 , 𝐾 𝑆 = 𝐾 ∩ 𝑈 𝑆 = 𝛾 3(𝑈𝑆) (Q5.3)
satisfy L_S∩L_R=L_(S∩R) for every pair of vertex subsets S,R.
The bijection sends N to the marking on U/N. It does not divide out any further automorphisms for ordered markings.
If generator-set equivalence is desired, it is exactly the orbit relation from Aut(T) on these kernels. For a nondegenerate
path this group consists of identity and reversal.
Proof. The lower-central graded group of F_T is generated as a Lie ring by the degree-one involutions. Multilinearity
shows that every homogeneous component is generated by finitely many elements of exponent two. Consequently
each lower-central factor through degree k+2 is a finite elementary abelian group. More explicitly, the left-normed
commutators of weight j on the d letters give at most d^j generators, so |U|≤2 (∑_(j=1)(k+2) d^j). This written finite
bound suffices for termination independently of the smaller computed orders; the quotient U is a finite two-group.
The involution/commutation relations are respected when any selected subset of the x_i is sent to 1; functoriality of
the lower central series makes this descend to U. The deletion maps e_S commute, with e_S e_R=e_(S∩R), fix U_S
pointwise and have image U_S. An element of U_S∩U_R is therefore fixed by e_(S∩R), proving IP.
Collection in the class-two involution/commutation presentation bounds its order by 2^(d+m). The coordinate group
(Q5.1) realizes all d vertex coordinates and all m edge coordinates independently, attaining this bound. It is a quotient
of U, since it has class at most two. Thus U/K is exactly that marked coordinate group. In particular every edge of T
remains noncommuting in U. Also, for x∈K∩U_S, applying e_S to an expression of x in γ ₃(U) places x in γ ₃(U_S).
The opposite inclusion is immediate, proving the restriction equality in (Q5.3).
For a target G, Theorem Q5.3 gives |γ ₃(G)|=2^k. A nontrivial lower-central term of a finite nilpotent group strictly
decreases at the next step: equality would persist forever, contradicting nilpotence. There can consequently be at most
k nontrivial successive factors starting at γ ₃(G), so γ_(k+3)(G)=1. The marked presentation map F_T→G factors
through U. Its induced map on class-two quotients is the marked isomorphism Q_T→Q_T, hence its kernel N lies in
K. The orders give [K:N]=2^k.
Conversely, such a kernel gives a quotient G=U/N of the required order. Its specified noncommutations survive in the
quotient G/(K/N)=Q_T, and its nonedge commutations are inherited. If q:U→G and D=K/N, then
𝐷 ∩ 𝐺𝑆 = 𝑞(𝐾 ∩ 𝑈 𝑆) = 𝑞(𝐾 𝑆) = 𝐿 𝑆. (Q5.4)
Indeed q⁻¹(q(K))=K because N≤K, so an element of U_S mapping into D must lie in K. The marking on G/D has IP
by the coordinate description of Q_T. Theorem K(a), applied to G→Q_T, now gives exactly the displayed criterion on
the L_S. This proves necessity and sufficiency without assuming IP for a quotient.
An ordered marked isomorphism between two quotients of U commutes with their maps from U because those maps
agree on every original generator; thus their kernels are equal. Conversely equal kernels give the same ordered marked
quotient. A permutation of the generator set in an isomorphism preserves commutation and is an automorphism of T.
Each such permutation is an automorphism of F_T and U, and acts on their kernels. Its orbits are therefore precisely
the claimed generator-set classes. ■
Theorem Q5.6 (complete invariant index-two ladder). Let U be a finite two-group and M◁U. The U-normal sub-
groups L≤M with [M:L]=2 are exactly the preimages of the hyperplanes in
𝑀 /(Φ(𝑀 )[𝑀 , 𝑈 ]). (Q5.5)
For any K◁U, every U-normal N≤K of index 2^k is obtained by k successive such steps. This includes nonabelian K
and M.
Proof. An index-two subgroup L of M is normal in M, contains Φ(M), and has quotient C ₂. If L is U-normal, con-
jugation by U on M/L is trivial because Aut(C ₂)=1, so [M,U]≤L. Conversely, the preimage of a hyperplane contains
both Φ(M) and [M,U]; the latter containment makes it U-normal, and its index in M is two. The quotient in (Q5.5) is
elementary abelian by the Burnside basis theorem.
Suppose N◁U and N<M. The finite two-group U/N acts on the nonzero characters of (M/N)/Φ(M/N). This nonzero
vector space exists because M/N is a nontrivial finite two-group. Its nonzero characters number 2^a−1, which is odd.
Since orbit sizes are powers of two, at least one orbit has size one. The kernel of that fixed nonzero character lifts
to a U-normal subgroup between N and M, of index two in M. Repeating constructs the required chain from K down
to N. Breadth-first generation of every hyperplane preimage at each step, removing only exactly equal subgroups, is
therefore exhaustive. ■
The algorithmic consequence is concrete. For a path on d vertices and a target order 2^n, set k=n−(2d−1). Construct
U by the universal nilpotent-quotient map of class k+2, preserve the original marked generators, enumerate the ladder
in K and test (Q5.3) in its quotient of order 2^k. Full marked-subgroup intersection tests can independently check this
profile criterion. Neither an anticipated group count, an Atlas name nor an abstract-group catalogue enters candidate
generation. A canonical pc subgroup key must encode actual subgroup elements injectively; a binary exponent encoding
is valid only after checking that all ambient relative pc orders are two.
The cases (d,k)=(3,3),(3,5),(4,3),(5,1) respectively give the order-256 seed and the three nondegenerate order-1024
censuses. Their terminal counts and the order-256 word-pattern implications require the complete executed programs
printed with the new manuscript; they are not conclusions of this proof alone.
12. Bounded cyclic cores and all binary extension models
We now prove the additional classification needed by L035. This section is source-specific work, sharing its square
quotient and kernel transport with the earlier construction; its length must remain in the replacement.
The retained cyclic core theorem of Lucchini says: if a cyclic proper subgroup 𝑀 of a finite group 𝐻 has core 𝐾,
then |𝑀 ∶ 𝐾| < |𝐻 ∶ 𝑀 | . Here 𝐾 is the largest normal subgroup of 𝐻 contained in 𝑀 . We use the form recorded
in Isaacs, Finite Group Theory, Theorem 2.20, also stated as L035 Proposition 2.9.
Theorem Q6.1 (bounded cyclic quotient). Let (𝐺; 𝑎, 𝑏, 𝑐) be a rank-three string C-group of order 2𝑡+𝑠, type {4, 2𝑡},
with 𝑠 ≥ 3 and 𝑡 ≥ 𝑠 − 2 . Put
𝑟 = 𝑏𝑐, 𝑚 = 2 𝑡, 𝑑 0 = (𝑎𝑏) 2, 𝐴 = ⟨𝑟 2𝑠−2
⟩.
Then 𝐴 ◁ 𝐺, and 𝐺/𝐴 is a string C-group of order 22𝑠−2 and type {4, 2𝑠−2}.
Proof. The even-word subgroup 𝐻 = ⟨𝑎𝑏, 𝑏𝑐⟩ has index at most two. IP and Theorem S give 𝐺/𝐺′ = 𝐶 3
2 , where its
image has dimension two, so the index is exactly two. For 𝑀 = ⟨𝑟⟩ , the cyclic core theorem gives |𝑀 ∶ Core𝐻 𝑀 | <
2𝑠−1, hence at most 2𝑠−2. Thus 𝐴 is the subgroup of the cyclic core of its prescribed order, so is characteristic there
and normal in 𝐻. The involution 𝑏 inverts 𝑟; consequently 𝐴 ◁ 𝐺. It meets ⟨𝑎, 𝑏⟩ trivially by IP and lies in ⟨𝑏, 𝑐⟩.
Their quotient parabolics still intersect in ⟨𝑏𝐴⟩: equality of cosets gives membership in the original right parabolic,
after multiplication by an element of 𝐴. The left parabolic embeds and the right has its stated dihedral order. This
proves IP, independence, type and order in the quotient. ■

Proof, first branch. For 𝑠 = 3 , Theorem Q6.1 gives a quotient of type {4, 2} and order sixteen, necessarily 𝐷8 × 𝐶2.

Apply Theorems Q5.5–Q5.6 with d=3 and k=3. The universal class-five group has order 2¹ ⁸ and third lower-central
subgroup of order 2¹³. Its invariant index-two ladder has 7, 23 and 61 kernels. Testing all 64 subset pairs for each final
candidate gives 37 ordered IP markings, four with the indicated orientation of type {4,8}. Appendix A evaluates every
displayed word on all four and verifies that each belongs to exactly one row, one marking per row. Section 24.3 states

algorithms, with no abstract-group catalogue. The former SmallGroups identifiers and anchored multiplicities remain
historical records in the unchanged predecessor, and are unnecessary for the relation implications used below.

by normality, so it centralizes 𝑟, contradicting that order. Thus III and IV cannot lift.

{4, 4} group of order 128, hence 𝐻7 = 𝑊 /4ℤ 2. Let 𝑁 = ⟨𝑑 0, 𝑟⟩. Equations (Q6.1) make it normal, with index
four: modulo 𝑁 , 𝑎, 𝑐 commute, and their independence follows in the rank-three Frattini quotient, where 𝑁 has the

𝑟 centralizes its own powers.

2 , with 𝑑3 = 𝑑 0𝑑1𝑑2. Upstairs, every [𝑑𝑖, 𝑑𝑗] is in central 𝑀 and

It remains to recognize the bare presentations, where the target order cannot yet be assumed. Let 𝑃 be one of them
and 𝑧 = 𝑟 𝑚/2, initially of order at most two. For 𝐺1, 𝐺2, if 𝜖 = 1 , 𝑎 centralizes 𝑧 = [𝑑 0, 𝑐] since it centralizes 𝑑0, 𝑐;

Collection gives at most 8𝑚 normal forms, attained by the first model.
For 𝐺3, 𝐺4, 𝑑2 = 𝑑 0𝑧𝜖. If 𝜖 = 0 , applying 𝑎 as in the second-branch argument gives [𝑑0, 𝑑1] = 1 , after which the
model relations and centrality of 𝑧 follow. If 𝜖 = 1 , [𝑑0, 𝑟2] = 𝑧 implies that conjugation by 𝑑0 inverts 𝑧; as 𝑧2 = 1 ,
it centralizes 𝑧. Thus 𝑧 commutes with 𝑟 and all 𝑑𝑖. Write 𝑗 = [𝑑 0, 𝑑1]. Applying 𝑎 to the equation for 𝑑2, and using
(Q6.1), gives 𝑧𝑎 = 𝑗𝑧 . Since 𝑎 fixes 𝑑0, 𝑑1, it fixes 𝑗, and 𝑎2 = 1 gives 𝑗2 = 1 . Hence 𝑑0, 𝑑1 centralize 𝑗. Also
𝑗𝑟 = [𝑑 1, 𝑑2] = [𝑑 1, 𝑑0𝑧] = 𝑗 −1 = 𝑗 , and 𝑐 exchanges 𝑑0, 𝑑1, so 𝑗 is central. Collection yields (𝑑0𝑟)4 = 𝑗𝑟 4. Since
𝑚/8 is even, 𝑧𝑎 = (𝑑 0𝑟)𝑚/2 = 𝑧 , so 𝑗 = 1 . The second model relations now give at most 16𝑚 forms, all attained by
that model.
For 𝐺5–𝐺8, put 𝑤 = [𝑎, 𝑟 2]. If 𝛽 = 1 , conjugation by 𝑎 inverts 𝑤, and so fixes 𝑧 = 𝑤 2. If 𝜈 = 1 , it inverts
[𝑎, 𝑟4] = 𝑧 , again fixing 𝑧. If both are zero, 𝑎 centralizes 𝑟4 and therefore 𝑧. Thus 𝑧 is central in every case, and
[𝑎, 𝑟4] = 𝑧 𝜈 makes 𝑀 = ⟨𝑟 4⟩ normal of order at most 𝑚/4. Modulo 𝑀 , the presentation is 𝑊 with the extra relation
[𝑎, 𝑟2]2 = 1 . In the affine model [𝑎, 𝑟2] is a doubled unit translation, so its square and conjugates kill precisely 4ℤ2.
Thus the quotient has order 128, and |𝑃 | ≤ 128(𝑚/4) = 32𝑚 . The constructed model attains this bound and satisfies
every relator, so the order comparison principle identifies 𝑃 with it. This completes presentation recognition and the
theorem. ■
The preceding theorem proves the full marked interpretation of L035 Theorem 1.3; the listed word invariants distinguish
all the declared families. It does not classify arbitrary two-groups of order 2𝑛, other types, or the source’s unnumbered
exceptional 𝑛 < 10 discussion.
14. Prescribed square sections and their universal presentation
For 𝑑 ≥ 3 and 𝑛3, … , 𝑛𝑑 ≥ 5 , place 𝐻𝑛𝑖+3
on the label bag {𝑖, 𝑖 + 1, 𝑖 + 2} , 0 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑑 − 3 . Glue the successive
bags over their shared 𝐷8 using Theorem Q2.3. At each step the previous group and the new seed embed, with their
given markings. Thus all adjacent orders remain four, distant markers commute, every deletion exists, and the group
𝐺(𝑛3, … , 𝑛𝑑) has IP. Its exact order is

The common regular-polytope correspondence gives a finite regular𝑑-polytope with precisely the specified consecutive
triple sections.
For 𝑑 ≥ 4 , let 𝐴 be its final facet subgroup, on{0, … , 𝑑−2}, and let 𝐵 be its last triple subgroup, on{𝑑−3, 𝑑−2, 𝑑−1}.
The compatible product decomposition gives 𝐺 = 𝐵𝐴 . The stabilizer of the base (𝑑 − 4)-face contains 𝐵. The coset
incidence criterion therefore makes every (𝑑 − 4)-face incident with every facet. Every vertex lies on a (𝑑 − 4)-face
(including the rank-zero case), so the polytope is flat. Boolean deletion gives FAP for every initial or final cut, hence
all three cuts required by F016 5.1(d). For 𝑑 = 3 , the co-(−1)-face cut, if included in that notation, is the tautological
whole-polytope cut; the vertex-figure and facet cuts are genuine seed retractions. This proves every clause of F016
Theorem 5.1.

Indeed realize the group as compatible tuples of seed coordinates. An element of the third lower-central subgroup of
one embedded triple projects trivially into every other bag: the overlap has at most two markers and its image lies in
a dihedral group of class at most two. It occupies exactly its own coordinate, so these kernels are independent normal
factors. On quotienting by their product, each consecutive triple is class two. Every edge commutator then commutes
with its endpoints, its nearest outside markers, and by string commutation with the remaining markers. Hence the entire
quotient is class two. This proves equality with 𝛾3(𝐺), and (Q8.1) gives the canonical quotient order. Thus (Q8.2) is
one construction meeting the bound in Theorem Q5.3 at every rank, rather than an unrelated counting trick.
We next prove that this construction has the exact additional presentation claimed in F016 Theorem 5.3 . The only
extra identity needed is local.
Lemma Q8.1 (four-marker centralization). Suppose 𝑎, 𝑏, 𝑐, 𝑑 are involutions with [𝑎, 𝑐] = [𝑎, 𝑑] = [𝑏, 𝑑] = 1 and
[(𝑎𝑏)2, (𝑐𝑑)2] = 1 . Then 𝑑 commutes with (𝑐𝑏𝑎𝑏)2 and (𝑎𝑏𝑐𝑏)2.
Proof. Put 𝑥 = 𝑏𝑎𝑏 , 𝐸 = (𝑎𝑏) 2 = 𝑎𝑥 , 𝐹 = (𝑐𝑑) 2. The element 𝑎 commutes with 𝐹 , so [𝐸, 𝐹 ] = 1 implies
[𝑥, 𝐹 ] = 1 . Also [𝑥, 𝑑] = 1 , hence [𝑥, 𝐹 𝑑] = [𝑥, 𝑐𝑑𝑐] = 1 . Conjugating by 𝑐 gives [𝑐𝑥𝑐, 𝑑] = 1 . Both factors
of (𝑐𝑏𝑎𝑏)2 = (𝑐𝑥𝑐)𝑥 commute with 𝑑. Finally 𝑎𝑏𝑐𝑏 = 𝑏(𝑐𝑏𝑎𝑏) −1𝑏, and 𝑏 commutes with 𝑑, proving the second
assertion. ■
First let 𝑃0 have the string [4, … , 4] relations and every prescribed triple presentation from §9. All its defining relators
are stable under every deletion assignment: the string Coxeter relators have even exponents, and the local lattice relators
survive every deletion of their three markers by §9. Thus the assignments define endomorphisms of 𝑃0, and Lemma
Q2.2 proves its IP without assuming finiteness. The constructed quotient 𝐺 shows that each local triple embeds with
its exact prescribed marking and that every generator is nontrivial. Hence 𝑃0 itself is a string C-group of the required
local type. Every marked string C-group of that local type satisfies its defining relators, so is its quotient. The standard
marked covering correspondence therefore constructs the universal regular polytope of that type directly. No separate
assumption about a universal polytope’s presentation or existence is needed.
Now let 𝑄 be the quotient of 𝑃0 by
[(𝑟𝑖𝑟𝑖+1)2, (𝑟𝑖+2𝑟𝑖+3)2] = 1 (0 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑑 − 4). (Q8.3)
The constructed group satisfies these relations. For (Q8.3), no tuple coordinate contains both disjoint edges; the edge
missing one endpoint has square one in that coordinate. Hence 𝑄 ↠ 𝐺 . Each triple embeds in 𝑄, since its map onward
to the embedded triple in 𝐺 is injective. Its third lower-central subgroup 𝐾𝑖 is generated by its two squared translations
and has order 2𝑛𝑖+3−5, by (Q3.2). Lemma Q8.1 and its reversed version show that the nearest outside markers centralize
𝐾𝑖, while the farther markers do so by string commutation. Thus every 𝐾𝑖 ◁ 𝑄. Their product 𝐾 is finite of order at
most 2∑𝑗(𝑛𝑗−5). Modulo it all triples are class two; the same local-to-global centralization argument used for (Q8.2)
makes 𝑄/𝐾 class two. Collecting markers and central edge commutators gives |𝑄/𝐾| ≤ 2 2𝑑−1. Therefore
|𝑄| ≤ 2 ∑𝑗(𝑛𝑗−5)+2𝑑−1 = 2 ∑𝑗 𝑛𝑗−3(𝑑−3) = |𝐺|.
The order comparison principle identifies 𝑄 = 𝐺 as marked groups. Since 𝑃0 is the universal type group just con-
structed, this is exactly its quotient by (Q8.3). The finite group presentation, the universal group and both intersection
properties have all been proved using the same deletion calculus.
15. Alternating assembly and infinite extensions
The additional geometry required for the semiregular conclusions is retained with the following precise forms; no target
theorem of F016 is used to justify them.
Assembly input. If regular (𝑑 − 1)-polytopes 𝑃 , 𝑄 have an identified common facet 𝐾 and FAP retractions Γ(𝑃 ) =
𝑁𝑃 ⋊ Γ(𝐾) , Γ(𝑄) = 𝑁 𝑄 ⋊ Γ(𝐾) , the tail-triangle group (𝑁𝑃 × 𝑁𝑄) ⋊ Γ(𝐾) , with distinct final markers, gives
an alternating semiregular 𝑑-polytope with these two facets. Its full automorphism group is that group when 𝑃 ≇ 𝑄 ,
and its extension by the branch-swapping 𝐶2 when 𝑃 = 𝑄 ; the latter polytope is regular. This is the construction and
full-automorphism criterion of Monson–Schulte, The assembly problem for alternating semiregular polytopes (2020),
Theorem 5.3, in the FAP case needed here.
Apply §14 to the two lists (𝑛3, … , 𝑛𝑑−2, 𝑛𝑑−1) and (𝑛3, … , 𝑛𝑑−2, 𝑚𝑑−1). Their common facet 𝐾 has list
(𝑛3, … , 𝑛𝑑−2), or is the square if 𝑑 = 4 . Theorem Q2.3 gives their tail-triangle fiber product, with
|Γ(𝐾)| = 2 ∑
𝑑−2
𝑗=3 𝑛𝑗−3(𝑑−5), |𝑁 𝑃 | = 2 𝑛𝑑−1−3, |𝑁 𝑄| = 2 𝑚𝑑−1−3.
The first expression gives eight when 𝑑 = 4 , with the sum empty. The assembly input supplies the polytope, while
these formulas give exactly its contracted order. Equal last parameters give identical marked constructions, hence the
branch swap; unequal last parameters give different facet orders and exclude isomorphism. This proves both clauses of
F016 Theorem 6.1, including the precise doubling case. Its type prescribes all the listed rank-three sections of facets;
it does not assert an independently prescribed co- (𝑑 − 4)-face.
For the infinite statements we retain the following previously established free-extension identifications , including
their geometric existence, universality and symmetry conclusions. All input ranks here satisfy the source’s standing
convention 𝑑 ≥ 3 :
1. If 𝐾 is regular with facet 𝐹 , its free extension is regular, of rank one higher, with groupΓ(𝐾)∗ Γ(𝐹 )(Γ(𝐹 )×𝐶2).
2. If 𝐾 is oriented chiral with directly regular facets 𝐹 , its free extension is oriented chiral, of rank one higher, with
group Γ(𝐾) ∗ Γ+(𝐹 ) Γ(𝐹 ), where Γ(𝐹 ) = Γ +(𝐹 ) ⋊ 𝐶2.
3. If regular 𝑑-polytopes 𝑃 , 𝑄 have isomorphic facets 𝐾, their universal alternating semiregular (𝑑 + 1)-polytope
has group Γ(𝑃 ) ∗Γ(𝐾) Γ(𝑄), with an additional branch-swapping 𝐶2 precisely if 𝑃 ≅ 𝑄 .
The first is the standard regular free-extension construction; the second is Schulte–Weiss,Free extensions of chiral poly-
topes (1995); the third is the universal alternating construction of Monson–Schulte (2012, 2021). The embeddings are
the indicated face or facet-rotation subgroups, not an assumed embedding of a full facet group in a chiral automorphism
group.
Lemma Q9.1 (torsion and amalgams). Let “two-primary torsion” mean that every element of finite order has two-
power order. This property passes to subgroups, to extensions by finite two-groups, and to amalgamated free products.
If the amalgamated subgroup is proper in both factors, the free product is infinite. If the factors are finitely generated,
so is the amalgam.
Proof. In an extension, a finite cyclic subgroup has two-group intersection with the kernel and two-group image, hence
two-power order. For an amalgam, its normal-form theorem conjugates any element either into a factor or to a cyclically
reduced word of length at least two. Powers of such a word stay reduced with increasing length and cannot have finite
order. Thus torsion is conjugate into a factor. If 𝑎, 𝑏 lie outside the common subgroup in their respective factors, (𝑎𝑏)ℎ
is reduced of length 2ℎ, proving infinitude. Finite generation follows by taking generators of both factors. ■
In each of the three free-extension identifications the common subgroup is proper in both factors, as the face/flag action
or the new reflection shows. In the second factor of the first two constructions its index is two. Every factor has two-
primary torsion: in the chiral case the rotation subgroup of the facet embeds in Γ(𝐾), and its extension by 𝐶2 suffices
to prove the property for Γ(𝐹 ). All factors are finitely generated by their finite marked sets. Lemma Q9.1 and, when
needed, its finite-extension clause prove exactly F016 Theorems 7.1, 7.2 and 7.3 , including infinite input groups. No
assertion that all elements have finite order is made.
16. Corrections and exclusions in the two-group branch
The F013 common-quotient theorem is recovered by a different kernel. Its specified auxiliary subgroup is false in
general. We include the proof of the correction so that no false historical claim is counted as a corollary of this
manuscript.
In the path model (Q5.1), name the central edge coordinates 𝑐1, … , 𝑐𝑑−1, and put
𝜎𝑖 = 𝑟 𝑖−1𝑟𝑖, 𝐴 𝑖 = ⟨𝜎 1, … , 𝜎𝑖−1, 𝜎2
𝑖 , 𝜎𝑖+1, … , 𝜎𝑑−1⟩, 𝐶 = ⋂
𝑖
Φ(𝐴𝑖). (Q10.1)
These are exactly the source’s equations (7)–(9), and 𝜎2
𝑖 = 𝑐 𝑖. The vertex images of the rotations are independent and
span the even-weight subspace. Each 𝐴𝑖 contains all central coordinates, with vertex-image dimension 𝑑 − 2 . Thus
|𝐴𝑖| = 2 2𝑑−3. Squaring a collected class-two word shows its square subgroup is generated by the squares and pair
commutators of the generators; Burnside identifies it with Φ(𝐴𝑖). The endpoint computations give
Φ(𝐴1) = ⟨𝑐 2, … , 𝑐𝑑−1⟩, Φ(𝐴 𝑑−1) = ⟨𝑐 1, … , 𝑐𝑑−2⟩.
All listed coordinates are squares; no missing endpoint coordinate can arise because every remaining rotation has the
missing endpoint vertex coordinate zero. For an internal 𝑖, all coordinates except 𝑐𝑖 are squares, and [𝜎𝑖−1, 𝜎𝑖+1] = 𝑐 𝑖.
Hence Φ(𝐴𝑖) = ⟨𝑐 1, … , 𝑐𝑑−1⟩ internally and
𝐶 = ⟨𝑐 2, … , 𝑐𝑑−2⟩, |𝐶| = 2 𝑑−3 (𝑑 ≥ 3). (Q10.2)
For 𝑑 = 2 , separately, 𝐶 = 1 . For every 𝑑 ≥ 4 , this model has order 22𝑑−1 but 𝐶 ≠ 1 = 𝛾 3(𝐺). It contradicts F013
v1 Theorem 4.2(c)’s kernel order, (d)’s quotient order and prescribed identification, and (e)’s equality with 𝛾3(𝐺). The
actual quotient order is 2𝑑+2; it still has IP, with interior adjacent orders two. Thus this counterexample does not refute
the separate main common-quotient existence conclusion, nor every clause of 4.2(d).
Two F016 proof repairs also remain explicit. In a diagonal square-map quotient the perpendicular squared translation
subgroups can coincide, so their orders cannot be multiplied as in its page-11 argument; (Q3.2) uses the correct lattice
index. In its chiral extension proof the full facet automorphism group need not embed in the chiral group; §15 only
uses its rotation subgroup and a finite index-two extension.
Outside the declared main-result contract are the four papers’ open questions, historical discussion, unnumbered small-
order lists and examples, F016’s separate power-polytope recollections, and assertions about absolute minimum flag
counts among all regular polytopes. The source’s known power constructions have displayed semidirect groups 𝐶 𝑣
2 ⋊
𝐻 and (𝐷2𝑚)𝑣/2 ⋊ 𝐻 , where here the latter dihedral notation means order 2𝑚+1. Conditional on those retained
constructions, their order formulas are immediate, but their geometric construction is not rederived or counted as
another target here. The order-1024 census N009 is excluded entirely.
The proved core supplies short final source corollaries, but its cyclic classification, lattice recognition, finite census
and retained semiregular geometry have real costs. The two-group source contracts require the full preceding text and
bound finite certificate; their inherited review does not certify this changed assembly. This integration requires its own
independent review; no factor is assigned.
17. A single endpoint theorem
Let (G;a,b,(r_i)_(i∈E)) be a C-group marking, with [a,r_i]=1 for all i∈E. No string diagram, finite group, or underlying
geometry is assumed. Define
𝑎′ = 𝑎𝑏𝑎, 𝑅 𝐽 = ⟨𝑟 𝑗 ∶ 𝑗 ∈ 𝐽 ⟩, 𝑈 𝐽 = ⟨𝑎, 𝑏, 𝑅𝐽 ⟩, 𝐻 𝐽 = ⟨𝑎 ′, 𝑏, 𝑅𝐽 ⟩,
𝑃 = ⟨𝑏, 𝑅 𝐸⟩, 𝐻 = 𝐻 𝐸, ℱ = {𝐽 ⊆ 𝐸 ∶ 𝑎 ∈ 𝐻 𝐽 }. (O3)
Conjugation by a exchanges b and a′ and fixes R_J pointwise. Consequently
𝐻𝐽 ⊴ 𝑈 𝐽 = 𝐻 𝐽 ⟨𝑎⟩, [𝑈 𝐽 ∶ 𝐻 𝐽 ] ≤ 2. (O4)
Endpoint theorem O2.1. The new marking (H;a′,b,(r_i)) has IP if and only if
𝑃 ∩ 𝑃 𝑎 = 𝑅 𝐸, ℱ is closed under intersections . (O5)
The group H is normal of index at most two in G. It is G precisely when ℱ is nonempty. Since E is finite, a nonempty
intersection-closed ℱ is precisely the family of supersets of one least support S⊆E.
Proof. The family ℱ is upward closed. Any new marked subgroup is of one of the forms
𝐴𝜖,𝐽 = ⟨𝑏 𝜖, 𝑅𝐽 ⟩ (𝜖 = 0, 1), 𝐻 𝐽 , 𝑄 𝑎
𝐽 with 𝑄𝐽 = ⟨𝑏, 𝑅𝐽 ⟩, (O6)
where b⁰ means that b is omitted. These correspond to omitting a′, using both endpoint labels, or using a′ but omitting
b. Intersections of two A’s are old marked intersections. Also
𝐴𝜖,𝐼 ∩ 𝐻𝐽 ≤ 𝐴 𝜖,𝐼 ∩ 𝑈𝐽 = 𝐴 𝜖,𝐼∩𝐽 ;
all generators on the right belong to H_J, so equality holds. Intersections of two Q^a’s follow by conjugating old IP.
Conjugation by a normalizes H_I, so an H-with-Q^a intersection reduces to the just proved H-with-A case. For an
A-with-Q^a intersection, the first condition in (O5) confines it to R_E. Intersecting there and using that a centralizes
R_E gives R_(I∩J).
It remains to intersect H_I and H_J. Put K=I∩J. Old IP gives
𝐻𝐾 ≤ 𝐻 𝐼 ∩ 𝐻𝐽 ≤ 𝑈 𝐼 ∩ 𝑈𝐽 = 𝑈 𝐾. (O7)
If a∈H_K then H_K=U_K. Otherwise H_K has index two in U_K, so the middle subgroup is either H_K or U_K.
The latter case occurs exactly when a belongs to both H_I and H_J, and intersection closure excludes it. This proves
all new marked intersections. Necessity of the first condition follows by intersecting the two endpoint-deleted new
parabolics; necessity of the second follows by intersecting H_I,H_J. The generators are involutions and the proved
IP supplies independence. Equation (O4) with J=E gives the group and index assertions. A finite nonempty upward
intersection-closed family contains its total intersection S, giving the least-support description. ■
Corollary O2.2 (odd endpoint and proper halving). If m=|ab| is odd, the only test in (O5) is the overlap P∩P^a=R_E,
and H=G. If H is proper in G, the only test is again the overlap, and [G:H]=2. In either case the endpoint product has
order m/gcd(m,2) for finite m; infinite m remains infinite.
Indeed a′b=(ab)², and for odd m,
𝑎 = (𝑎 ′𝑏)(𝑚+1)/2𝑏 ∈ 𝐻 ∅.
Thus ℱ is respectively all subsets or empty. An even endpoint order by itself does not imply that H is proper; the
least-support alternative in Theorem O2.1 cannot be discarded.
Lemma O2.3 (central twist). Suppose b,c are involutions with |bc|=m odd and m≥3, and w is a nonidentity involution
centralizing b,c. Replacing b by wb preserves the generated group and changes |bc| to 2m, provided w belonged to the
original group.
Proof. The commuting factors w and bc have coprime orders, so (wbc)^m=w and |wbc|=2m. Hence the new generators
recover w and b. The new marker wb is a nonidentity involution: wb=1 would make b=w commute with c, contrary
to m≥3 odd. ■
A parabolic containing w,b,c is unchanged by this twist; one omitting b is unchanged. Other intersections and typed
Helly still require proof. Lemma O2.3 is the auxiliary operation needed in the finite application below, not a blanket
geometric preservation theorem.
18. The geometric meaning of the support condition
Let Γ be a finite-rank residually connected incidence geometry, of rank n≥3. Designate types 0 and 1 as vertices and
edges. Assume their truncation is a simple graph Q, and that an edge is incident with a higher object exactly when both
its endpoints are incident with that object. For a flag F of higher objects, let Q_F be its common vertex-edge shadow.
All Q_F are connected and contain an edge.
Lemma O0 supplies connected chamber galleries in every residue. Restricting a gallery to any set of at least two types
shows that its truncation is connected, since successive complete incidence subgraphs overlap. Apply this inside the
residue at F; chamber extension supplies an edge. The edge-shadow hypothesis says Q_F is the induced graph on its
common vertices.
For a connected graph Q, its bipartite double cover D(Q) has vertices (v,ε), ε∈{0,1}, with (v,ε) adjacent to (w,1−ε)
when v is adjacent to w. It is connected when Q has an odd cycle, and has two components otherwise: parity of walks
proves both assertions. Choose a component C of D(Q). Define L_C(Γ) as follows. Its first two types are (v,0) and
(v,1) in C. A higher object is a pair (x,C_x), where C_x is a component of D(Q_x) contained in C. Incidence of the
first two types is adjacency, incidence with a higher object is membership in C_x, and two higher objects are incident
when their old objects are incident and their chosen components meet.
The precise compatibility requirement is
if every 𝑄𝑥 (𝑥 ∈ 𝐹 ) is nonbipartite, then 𝑄𝐹 is nonbipartite, (PC)
for every nonempty higher flag F. It is automatic if the higher shadows of every flag are nested, or if Q is bipartite.
Geometric theorem O3.1. The system L_C(Γ) is always a geometry. It is residually connected exactly when (PC)
holds. It is thin exactly when Γ is thin. A chamber-transitive automorphism group of Γ acts faithfully and chamber-
transitively on L_C(Γ) when Q is nonbipartite; when Q is bipartite its index-two color-preserving subgroup does so.
Proof. For a flag Φ={(x,C_x):x∈F} of higher lifts, write W=∩C_x. If any Q_x is bipartite, restriction of C_x to
D(Q_F) is one component. Any two such restrictions agree: incidence makes them meet over the connected Q_(x,y),
and hence select the same parity class there and over Q_F. Nonbipartite constituents impose no restriction. Thus W is
one component if any constituent is bipartite, and all of D(Q_F) otherwise. In particular W always contains an edge,
and is connected exactly under (PC) for this flag. For F empty use W=C.
An edge of W and a chamber of Γ extending its base edge and F give a chamber extending Φ. Every additional higher
object has a unique component containing the chosen lift of that edge. The same extension works for a flag already
containing one lifted vertex. If it contains both, their base edge is unique by simplicity and incident with all the old
higher objects by the shadow condition. This proves the geometry axiom.
The residue at (v,0) is the old residue Γ_v: a neighbour (w,1) corresponds to the unique edge {v,w}, and the component
for an incident higher object is uniquely specified by (v,0). Simplicity and the shadow condition preserve all incidences.
The other color is identical. Hence residues of flags containing a lifted vertex are connected. For a flag of higher objects
only, its two-type truncation is W; when W is connected, every other residual object attaches to it through a chamber.
This proves residual connectivity under (PC). If (PC) fails, choose its higher flag; all constituents have unique lifts but
W has two components. Were the resulting geometry residually connected, the truncation lemma inside its residue
would make W connected. This proves necessity without treating a truncation as a residue.
Panels containing both new vertex types correspond to old panels missing the same higher type. A panel missing one
new vertex type corresponds to an old panel missing the edge type, at the retained endpoint. Old panels missing the
vertex type already have two objects by simplicity. These correspondences prove both directions of thinness.
If Q is nonbipartite, an old chamber (v,{v,w},x ₂,…,x_(n−1)) corresponds bijectively to the new chamber with
(v,0),(w,1) and the components containing them. Old automorphisms act naturally on this bijection. If Q is bipartite,
the same bijection uses the old chambers whose chosen vertex is in one fixed color class. A chamber-transitive group
contains a color-exchanging element, since it exchanges the endpoints of a base edge through a chamber move. Its
color-preserving subgroup therefore has index two and is transitive on the selected chambers.
For faithfulness apply K(c) to this actual acting group, retaining X consisting of old vertices and all old higher objects.
A new vertex is encoded by its old vertex and its color; (x,C_x) is encoded by x and the two old-vertex subsets of C_x.
These data determine the component, because the double-cover graph is fixed. Forgetting the color or component maps
onto X: when Q is nonbipartite all lifts lie in C, while in the bipartite case C has one lift of every old vertex and one
compatible component of every connected Q_x. Thus the encoding and surjection hypotheses hold. An element fixing
X fixes every old edge by simplicity, so acts trivially on Γ and is the identity. K(c) therefore proves faithfulness. ■
Suppose now Γ is regular and thin, with vertex-changing marker a, edge-changing marker b, and higher markers r_i;
assume a commutes with the higher markers. Under (PC), Theorem O3.1 produces a regular thin residually con-
nected geometry. Directly on a base edge, its panel involutions are b^a,b,r_i: b changes one endpoint, aba changes
the other, and each r_i changes its higher object. Lemma O1.1’s chamber argument identifies their group as the full
type-preserving group H. Conversely, walking within residues identifies the stabilizer of a base flag with the subgroup
generated by its missing-type markers, giving IP and identifying the actual coset incidence with L_C(Γ).
There is also an exact correspondence between the two parity conditions. In the base residue with group U_J from (O3),
the vertex stabilizer is Q_J=⟨b,R_J⟩. Its vertex graph is bipartite precisely when a∉H_J. If H_J is proper, its two cosets
give a coloring, since Q_J≤H_J and a crosses colors. Conversely the action on the two colors supplies a character killing
b and all r_j and sending a to the nontrivial element, so it excludes a from H_J. In a globally nonbipartite geometry,
higher flags reduce by transitivity to base flags with shadows U_(E\S). Condition (PC) says
[𝐸 ∖ {𝑖} ∈ ℱ for all 𝑖 ∈ 𝑆] ⟹ 𝐸 ∖ 𝑆 ∈ ℱ. (O8)
An upward family containing E satisfies (O8) exactly when it is principal: let T be the set of i whose complementary
coatom belongs to ℱ; (O8) gives E\T∈ℱ, and every member of ℱ contains E\T. Thus geometric parity compatibility
is the least-support condition of Theorem O2.1. The mixed overlap in that theorem has a different role: it says the
two adjacent vertices determine one edge. Indeed P and P^a stabilize the endpoints; their intersection is the edge’s
pointwise stabilizer R_E exactly when no other edge has those endpoints, using transitivity on panels and the regular
chamber identification.
19. All lattice-polytope halvings
Corollary O4.1. Let P be a regular rank-n abstract polytope, n≥3, whose face poset is a lattice. Replacing one endpoint
marker a by aba gives a C-group H normal of index at most two in Aut(P). Its maximal-parabolic coset system is a
regular hypertope, with full type-preserving group H. The diagram duplicates the edge labels from b to the remaining
markers, and the a′–b label is m/gcd(m,2) for m=|ab| finite, or infinity for infinite m. The index is two exactly when
the corresponding vertex graph is bipartite; for a final-end operation use the dual graph on facets.
Proof. In a lattice polytope an edge is the join of its two distinct vertices. Thus two vertices determine at most one
edge, and that edge belongs to a higher face exactly when both vertices do. Higher faces of a flag form a chain, so
their shadows are nested and (PC) is automatic. Theorem O3.1 and the panel-marker identification prove the asserted
geometry, full group, and IP without an alternating-semiregular-polytope theorem. Equation (O4) and the coloring
argument give the index. Finally a′b=(ab)² and a commutes with all r_i, so a′r_i is conjugate to br_i; all other products
are unchanged. ■
This is N013 Theorem 3.1, Proposition 3.2 and Corollary 3.3, after reversing their final-end convention. Their termnon-
degenerate is used in the lattice-face-poset sense, as explicitly explained by the later primary halving manuscript cited in
the references; a general abstract polytope with simple diagram is not substituted for that hypothesis. N013’s auxiliary
direct-product Lemma 2.4 follows at once because a marked subgroup of a product with disjoint factor markings is the
product of the corresponding marked subgroups, and intersections are coordinatewise. Its quoted Propositions 2.1 and
2.3 are background; the latter is not needed. Recognition lemma O1.1 supplies the content needed from N013’s quoted
Theorem 2.2.
20. Generalized cubes: one parity module gives every principal application
Retain the following Danzer construction. If K is a finite regular lattice polytope with m vertices, 2^K has binary vertex
set V=F₂^m. A face associated with a face F of K and x∈V allows exactly the coordinates indexed by the vertices of F
to vary. These coordinate subcubes, ordered by inclusion with their improper faces, form a regular lattice polytope of
one higher rank, with vertex figures K and automorphism group
Aut(2𝐾) = 𝑉 ⋊ Aut(𝐾). (O9)
The coordinate action is the natural vertex permutation action. This construction theorem is a substantial retained input;
neither its polytopality nor the identification of the full group in (O9) is inferred solely from an order calculation.
Corollary O5.1. Halving 2^K at its vertex endpoint gives the exact group
𝐸𝑚 ⋊ Aut(𝐾), 𝐸 𝑚 = {𝑥 ∈ F𝑚
2 ∶ ∑ 𝑥𝑖 = 0}, (O10)
of order 2^(m−1)|Aut(K)|, acting as the full type-preserving group of the regular hypertope in Corollary O4.1.
Proof. The vertex graph of 2^K is the m-cube, bipartitioned by coordinate parity. Its color-preserving subgroup of
(O9) is exactly (O10), so Corollary O4.1 identifies the whole halving group. Alternatively, let a flip the coordinate
of a base vertex v of K and let b be its first distinguished reflection, carrying v to an adjacent vertex v′. The product
a′b=(aba)b is translation by e_v+e_v′. The unchanged reflections generate Aut(K). Their conjugates give every edge
difference in the connected vertex graph of K; along paths, these span E_m. This independently proves generation of
the displayed subgroup. ■
N013 uses the dual convention P̂=(2^(K*))*. Consequently m in its formulas is the number of facets of K, and reversing
all types converts (O10) exactly to its construction.
Corollary O5.2 (polygon inputs). For a p-gon K, p≥3, the result is a self-dual orientable regular map of type {p,p},
with
𝑉 = 𝐹 = 2 𝑝−1, 𝐸 = 𝑝2 𝑝−2, | Aut | = 𝑝2 𝑝, 𝑔 = 1 + 2 𝑝−3(𝑝 − 4). (O11)
Proof. The two vertex colors give V and F after ordering the map types as (color 0, old square, color 1). Each edge of
the p-gon specifies 2^(p−2) coordinate squares, giving E. The fork diagram becomes {p,p}. A single coordinate flip
conjugates the two new endpoint reflections and exchanges the colors, supplying the duality. Extend the reflection-sign
character of the dihedral Aut(K) trivially over E_m. Each of the three new distinguished reflections has sign −1, so
the chamber orientation is well defined and reverses across every adjacency. This proves orientability. Euler’s formula
V−E+F=2−2g gives (O11). ■
For p=3 the map is the tetrahedron: the group is E ₃⋊S₃ of order 24 with the spherical A ₃ marking and its universal
Coxeter geometry. For p=4 it is the square toroid {4,4}_(2,2). The Gray-code face-poset proof, explicit cubical duality
and lattice basis change in §9 prove this identification and identify the original dual generalized cube as {4,4}_(4,0).
Corollary O5.3 (all locally spherical inputs and exceptional orders). In output rank n≥4, the locally spherical cases
of N013’s dual generalized-cube construction are obtained from K equal to the (n−1)-simplex or (n−1)-cube, and
additionally the dodecahedron for n=4 and the 120-cell for n=5. Their types are respectively spherical D_n, affine
B̃_(n−1), and the rank-four/rank-five compact-hyperbolic forks. The last two finite groups have orders
211 ⋅ 120 = 245760, 2 119 ⋅ 14400. (O12)
Proof. Put L=K* to use the coordinate model. Each of its two new vertex residues is L itself. If the output is locally
spherical, L must therefore be a universal spherical polytope. Retain the classical finite string-Coxeter list. In rank at
least four a proper three-node fork with equal labels q,q is spherical only for q=3, where q is the first label of L. In rank
five, the remaining candidate F ₄={3,4,3} is excluded because its proper four-node fork is affine. The finite list leaves
exactly the simplex, cross-polytope, icosahedron and 600-cell, the last two only in their respective ranks. For rank at
least six only the first two remain.
Conversely, all proper faces of these L are simplices. At an old j-face of 2^L, j≥2, let F be its associated (j−1)-face of L.
The lower and upper sections vary independently. After halving, the maximal residue is the product of the compressed
cube 2^F with the spherical section L/F. As F has j vertices, the first factor has group E_j⋊S_j and its D_j marking;
its order 2^(j−1)j! equals that of the finite Coxeter group D_j, proving equality with the universal spherical geometry.
Here D₂=A₁×A₁ and D₃=A₃. Vertex residues are L, so all maximal residues, and consequently all proper residues, are
genuinely spherical geometries. A diagram alone is not used as a substitute for those residues.
The new global diagrams follow from Corollary O4.1. The retained finite, affine and compact-hyperbolic Coxeter classi-
fications identify them as stated. Formula (O10), with m=12 or 120 and the classical orders |Aut(dodecahedron)|=120
and |Aut(120-cell)|=14400, gives (O12). For the simplex m=n and |Aut(K)|=n!, giving D_n order 2^(n−1)n!. For
the (n−1)-cube, m=2(n−1) and |Aut(K)|=2^(n−1)(n−1)!, giving 2^(3n−4)(n−1)!. The finite hyperbolic examples are
proper hypertopes because the fork diagrams have a vertex of degree three. ■
The source’s toroidal notation for cube input is {4,3^(n−4),3/3}_(4,0,…,0). Section 9 identifies the original cubical
toroid and its halving as actual marked face/chamber quotients by 4Z^(n−1), proving the lattice names in addition to
the exact group, order and residue assertions above. N013’s claim that its rank-four hyperbolic example differs from
every example in its reference [9] is not used or independently verified. Its auxiliary identification with an alternating
semiregular polytope is likewise unnecessary and retained as background rather than counted as a new corollary.
There is a scope correction to N013 Remark 4.1. Retaining Pellicer’s extension construction, the halved diagram for
endpoint 2s has a three-node fork triangle with labels (p,p,s). For output rank at least four and p,s≥3, this proper rank-
three diagram is not finite, since 2/p+1/s≤1; thus the stated non-local-sphericity follows in those ranks. In rank three all
proper residues are finite dihedral for finite parameters, so the blanket non-local-sphericity wording is not valid under
the source’s own definition. Neither this remark nor its correction is a new open-problem claim.
21. Six finite constructions from one marking
The second application takes the odd branch of Corollary O2.2. Retain the GAP TomLib J1 permutation model, of
order 175560. Appendix B constructs a marking (a,b,c,d) within that model with
|𝑎𝑏|, |𝑎𝑐|, |𝑎𝑑|, |𝑏𝑐|, |𝑏𝑑|, |𝑐𝑑| = (5, 2, 2, 3, 2, 5),
(𝑎𝑏𝑐)5 = ((𝑑𝑐𝑏) 5𝑎)3 = 1. (O13)
It also completes a Todd–Coxeter calculation for the literal presentation with the four involution relations and (O13):
the index over ⟨b,c,d⟩ is 1463. That subgroup is a quotient of the finite Coxeter group [3,5] of order 120, so the presented
group has order at most 1463·120=175560. The found generating tuple in the TomLib model supplies a quotient of
that order; hence this is the source presentation of J1. A valid permutation action alone would not prove the upper
bound: the completed exact coset enumeration is a charged computational input.
Set w=(bcd)^5. The certificate proves that w is a nonidentity central involution of ⟨b,c,d⟩. The six source-defined
markings and their exact invariants are:

Corollary O6.1. Each of these six marked coset systems is a thin regular residually connected rank-four geometry with
full type-preserving group J1. They are pairwise nonisomorphic, even allowing type permutations. These are L088
Theorems 3.2, 4.2, 5.2, 5.4, 6.2 and 7.2, including their generation lemmas.
Proof. Lemma O2.3 gives the central twist Γ1→Γ2 and preserves its first parabolic. Every endpoint operation has odd
endpoint order five, so Corollary O2.2 recovers the same group. Appendix B verifies the two actual subgroup equalities
⟨𝑏, 𝑐, 𝑑⟩ ∩ ⟨𝑏, 𝑐, 𝑑⟩𝑎 = ⟨𝑐, 𝑑⟩, ⟨𝑎, 𝑏, 𝑐⟩ ∩ ⟨𝑎, 𝑏, 𝑐⟩ 𝑑 = ⟨𝑎, 𝑏⟩. (O14)
They have order ten. The second gives Γ3, the first Γ4. In Γ3, ⟨b,c,c^d⟩=⟨b,c,d⟩ because |cd|=5, and ⟨c,c^d⟩=⟨c,d⟩;
hence the same first equality gives Γ5. In Γ2 its first parabolic is unchanged by the twist, so that equality gives Γ6.
Thus after the seed and twist cases, four IP proofs reduce to the two meets (O14). Appendix B additionally verifies all
256 ordered marked-subset meets for each of the six markings, so the finite result does not depend on a mistaken reuse
of a subgroup identity.
For each marking it checks all sixteen equalities (O2), without presuming that a C-group with a nonstring diagram is
a geometry. Recognition lemmas O1.1–O1.2 now give all the geometric conclusions. The six multisets of maximal-
parabolic orders in the table are distinct, so no type-permuting isomorphism identifies two geometries. ■
The definition of Γ3 in L088 §5 duplicates an A5 parabolic, whereas its printed figure labelled Γ3 duplicates C2×A5;
the definition of Γ4 has the reverse discrepancy. The source figure labels Γ3 and Γ4 are therefore transposed relative
to its definitions. The table follows the definitions. This is a source correction, not a seventh construction.
The two source-mentioned failures also follow from the same certificate. The markings (a,wb,c,c^d) and (a,b,c,wd)
still generate J1 and satisfy typed Helly, but each has eight failing ordered IP meets; their panel orders are respectively
(2,4,2,2) and (2,2,2,4). Therefore they are not thin. These failures demonstrate why neither endpoint generation nor
flag transitivity is a substitute for IP.
21.1. Exhaustiveness by a general residue-extension theorem
The original geometry census can be replaced by a short complete search on proper residues. The required group data
are the complete TomLib table of J1 subgroup-conjugacy classes, not a list of its geometries.
Residue-extension theorem O6.2. Let G be finite and let M ₁,…,M_s represent all conjugacy classes of maximal
proper subgroups. For each M_j enumerate, up to M_j-conjugation, all unordered k-element sets of involutions whose
markings satisfy IP and typed Helly. Append every involution of G outside the subgroup generated by each retained
set and apply the same tests. This finds every rank-(k+1) thin regular residually connected geometry whose full type-
preserving group is a subgroup of G. To restrict to group G, retain exactly the extensions generating G.
Proof. For any such rank-(k+1) geometry, its distinguished marking consists of independent involutions. Omitting
one gives a proper subgroup P of its full group and hence of G, so P lies in a conjugate of one M_j. Its marked coset
geometry is the corresponding rank-k residue, thus satisfies both IP and typed Helly. Conjugation into M_j and then
within M_j puts its unordered markers in the enumerated list. Its omitted involution lies outside P and occurs in the
extension loop. Conversely, the tested IP and Helly conditions give exactly the asserted geometries by Lemma O1.1.
Ordering the marker set merely orders the type labels. ■
For completeness, the residue statement used here requires no extension of arbitrary residue automorphisms to G. The
base-flag stabilizer acts transitively and freely on the residue’s chambers, and the residue is thin and connected. Its full
type-preserving group also acts freely, so the stabilizer already is that full group. Walking inside the residue identifies
its panel generators. Thus local Helly is a necessary condition for these genuine residues, not an arbitrary extra filter.
Corollary O6.3. Up to isomorphism allowing a permutation of types, the six geometries in Corollary O6.1 are all the
thin regular residually connected rank-four geometries with group J1. There are none of rank at least five.
Proof. Appendix C executes Theorem O6.2 for k=3 and k=4. The retained complete table has forty subgroup classes.
Its nonzero containment entries determine seven maximal classes of orders 660,168,120,114,110,60,42. The local
counts are:
Maximal

The conjugation permutation action on involutions need not be faithful; its image nevertheless has exactly the required
orbits on subsets. No local generated subgroup is required to equal its containing maximal subgroup. The 95 retained
triples reduce to 70 J1-conjugacy classes. All 100016 possible outside-involution extensions are tested; forty pass with
generated group J1, collapsing to precisely six classes. Their maximal-parabolic order multisets are the six distinct
ones in Corollary O6.1, which already supplies six pairwise nonisomorphic constructions.
Here is the exact conjugacy test, so equality of numerical invariants is not mistaken for isomorphism. There is one
class of 1463 involutions in J1, with centralizer order120. Fix a ₀. For each x in an unordered marker set T choose
g_x with x^g_x=a ₀, enumerate T^(g_xc) for every c∈C_G(a ₀), and take the least sorted tuple of involution indices.
These are exactly the conjugates of T containing a ₀: every conjugator carrying x to a ₀ differs from g_x on the right
by such a c. Consequently keys agree exactly for inner-conjugate unordered markings. There is no assumption that
all automorphisms of J1 are inner. The six different parabolic-order multisets prevent any further isomorphism from
merging those classes, while the complete six-class upper bound prevents any additional geometry.
For k=4, the fifteen local regular quadruple classes remain fifteen after J1-conjugacy deduplication. All 21352 exten-
sions by an involution outside their generated subgroup fail the full regular-geometry tests. This stage does not discard
proper-subgroup targets. If a regular geometry of rank r≥5 existed for J1, fixing r−5 objects of a base chamber would
yield a rank-five regular residue with group contained in J1; Theorem O6.2 would have found it. Thus all higher ranks
are excluded. This does not assert nonexistence of C-group markings that fail typed Helly. ■
The source’s successful central modifications in §8, when they generate J1 and yield a thin regular geometry, are
consequently covered by the same exhaustive classification. The two displayed failures were checked separately above.
Appendix B also verifies the incidental source equality N_G(⟨a,b,c⟩)=⟨a,b,c⟩. Its initial vertex figure is the universal
spherical [3,5] geometry, since that parabolic has the full Coxeter order120; its facet is the antipodal hemi-dodecahedral
quotient of [5,3], since the A5 quotient has order60 and kernel the unique central order-two subgroup of C2×A5. These
conventional spherical identifications use the named classical Coxeter models.
22. Mirror covers and their exact kernel obstruction
22.1. Mirror, mix, and local kernels
Write an oriented polytope group as Γ = 𝑈 𝑛/𝑁 . Conjugation by 𝑟0 induces the involutory automorphism 𝜇 of 𝑈𝑛
given by
𝜇(𝑠1) = 𝑠 −1
1 , 𝜇(𝑠 2) = 𝑠 2
1𝑠2, 𝜇(𝑠 𝑖) = 𝑠 𝑖 (𝑖 > 2).
Put 𝑁 = 𝜇(𝑁 ) , Γ = 𝑈 𝑛/𝑁 , and
Λ = im(𝑈𝑛 ⟶ Γ × Γ).
This is the marked mix: its generators are the pairs of corresponding generators. It is 𝑈𝑛/(𝑁 ∩ 𝑁 ). The isomorphism
Γ → Γ , induced by 𝜇, identifies its marking with (𝜎−1
1 , 𝜎2
1𝜎2, 𝜎3, …).
Universal cover assertion. Λ is the rotation group of the smallest regular maniplex covering the given oriented poly-
tope.
Proof. Its relation kernel 𝑁 ∩ 𝑁 is 𝜇-invariant and contained in 𝑁 , so it is a regular cover. If a regular marked quotient
𝑈𝑛/𝐽 covers Γ, then 𝐽 ≤ 𝑁 and 𝜇(𝐽 ) = 𝐽 , whence 𝐽 ≤ 𝑁 . Thus 𝐽 ≤ 𝑁 ∩ 𝑁 , giving the asserted factorization.
This proves smallestness in the marked cover order. A regular cover of a chiral polytope is orientable: pull back the
two-coloring of its flag graph. Thus the argument includes all regular polytope covers of interest.
Define the chirality subgroup by
𝑋(Γ) = (𝑁 𝑁 )/𝑁 ≤ 𝑈 𝑛/𝑁 .
It is trivial exactly when 𝑁 = 𝑁 , equivalently when the oriented object is directly regular. The product 𝑁 𝑁 is the
least 𝜇-invariant normal subgroup containing 𝑁 .
Kernel identity. Under the first-coordinate identification,
𝑋(Γ) = {𝑔 ∈ Γ ∶ (𝑔, 1) ∈ Λ}. (V1)
Proof. Elements in the right side are exactly images in 𝑈𝑛/𝑁 of words in 𝑁 . Their image is 𝑁 𝑁 /𝑁. This proves
the identity and the chirality interpretation without any counting argument.
Let 𝐴 = Λ 𝑛−1, 𝐵 = Λ 0, and 𝐶 = Λ 0,𝑛−1 denote the subgroups generated respectively by the first 𝑛 − 2, last 𝑛 − 2,
and middle 𝑛 − 3 paired generators. Let 𝐾, 𝐿, 𝑀 be the correspondingly marked facet, vertex-figure, and medial
section of the original polytope. For 𝜋 ∶ Λ → Γ, the identities needed below are
𝐴 ∩ ker 𝜋 = 𝑋(𝐾) × 1, 𝐵 ∩ ker 𝜋 = 𝑋(𝐿) × 1, 𝐶 ∩ ker 𝜋 = 𝑋(𝑀 ) × 1. (V2)
Here the chirality groups are embedded in the same ambient first factor Γ with its fixed base flag. Merely knowing
their abstract isomorphism types is insufficient.
For clarity, the local mirror convention in (V2) is not an unmentioned automorphism of a chiral group. On the facet
universal subgroup, 𝜇 is precisely its intrinsic mirror. On the vertex universal subgroup generated by 𝑠2, … , 𝑠𝑛−1,
the intrinsic mirror is conjugation by 𝑟1. Since 𝑟0 = 𝑠 1𝑟1, global conjugation by 𝑟0 is intrinsic conjugation by 𝑟1,
followed by conjugation by 𝑠1. Conjugating the second coordinate by the image of 𝑠−1
1 therefore identifies the local
paired subgroup with the intrinsic mirror mix, without changing its first-coordinate kernel. The same argument applies
to the medial subgroup. Formula (V1), applied to those local universal subgroups, proves (V2). It also identifies the
end subgroups of Λ with the smallest regular covers of 𝐾 and 𝐿.
22.2. One-line criterion and its local prerequisites
Lemma V3.1 (regular end). An oriented rotary polytope having directly regular facets or directly regular vertex-
figures has a polytopal smallest regular cover.
Proof. Induct on rank; the polygon and directly regular cases are immediate. Suppose the facets 𝐾 are directly regular.
Their mixes are graph subgroups and hence are isomorphic marked copies of their rotation groups, so𝐴 is polytopal and
𝐴∩ ker 𝜋 = 1 . A vertex-figure 𝐿 has directly regular facets, since these are vertex-figures of 𝐾; by induction its mirror
mix 𝐵 is polytopal. The original mirrored polytope has the intersection property, giving 𝜋(𝐴) ∩ 𝜋(𝐵) = 𝜋(𝐶) . The
kernel condition in K is automatic because 𝐴 ∩ker 𝜋 = 1 . Hence 𝐴 ∩ 𝐵 = 𝐶 , and the recursive correspondence gives
polytopality of Λ. Duality handles directly regular vertex-figures. This proves, rather than imports, source Proposition
2.2.
For an arbitrary chiral polytope, the two end groups 𝐴, 𝐵 are polytopal: apply Lemma V3.1 to its facets and vertex-
figures using the depth-two regularity theorem. It follows that Λ is polytopal exactly when 𝐴 ∩ 𝐵 = 𝐶 . This proves
source Proposition 2.3, including its local prerequisites.
Theorem V3.2 (chiral-cover adapter). For a chiral polytope 𝑃 with compatibly marked sections 𝐾, 𝐿, 𝑀 ,
the smallest regular cover of 𝑃 is polytopal ⟺ 𝑋(𝐾) ∩ 𝑋(𝐿) ≤ 𝑋(𝑀 ). (V3)
Proof. The mirrored polytope gives 𝜋(𝐴) ∩ 𝜋(𝐵) = 𝜋(𝐶) . By (V2), the kernel condition in K is exactly 𝑋(𝐾) ∩
𝑋(𝐿) ≤ 𝑋(𝑀 ) . The preceding paragraph converts 𝐴 ∩ 𝐵 = 𝐶 into polytopality. This proves both directions of
source Theorem 3.1.
The following consequences contain all the general conclusions in its Section 3.
1. If either end section is regular, its chirality subgroup is trivial, and (V3) holds. This recovers Remark 3.2 consis-
tently with Lemma V3.1.
2. If the medial section is regular, its chirality subgroup is trivial, so the criterion is 𝑋(𝐾) ∩ 𝑋(𝐿) = 1 . Since this
intersection already lies in Γ𝑛−1 ∩ Γ0 = Γ 0,𝑛−1, this is exactly Corollary 3.3’s condition that its intersection
with the medial subgroup is trivial.
3. In rank four the medial section is a polygon and its group is ⟨𝜎2⟩. Thus the criterion says that 𝑋(𝐾) ∩ 𝑋(𝐿)
contains no nonidentity power of 𝜎2. This is the logically correct reading of Corollary 3.4; the identity belongs
to every subgroup.
4. If the facet and vertex-figure are totally chiral, their chirality groups are their full rotation groups. Their inter-
section is Γ0,𝑛−1, so (V3) holds precisely when 𝑋(𝑀 ) = Γ 0,𝑛−1, which is total chirality of 𝑀 . This proves
Proposition 3.6.
5. Suppose a chiral facet 𝐾 satisfies 𝑋(𝐾) ∩ Γ 0(𝐾) = 1 . In every chiral extension having these facets, the
intersection 𝑋(𝐾) ∩ 𝑋(𝐿) lies in 𝑋(𝐾) ∩ Γ 0,𝑛−1 = 𝑋(𝐾) ∩ Γ 0(𝐾) = 1 . This proves Corollary 3.7 for
every such extension, without assuming the existence of extensions of arbitrary rank.
22.3. The two infinite-family applications and their exact retained cost
Square torus maps. For the chiral maps {4,4}_(b,c) in the source’s standard parameter convention, bc(b−c)≠0,
Lemma L1 gives X(K)∩⟨v⟩=1. Consequence 5 in §22.2 therefore proves source Example 3.8 for every such exten-
sion. No negative or noncanonical parameters are added to the source contract; no degenerate one-cell map is needed.
Five tight families. Here the retained input is substantial and explicit. Cunningham–Pellicer, Tight chiral polytopes, J.
Algebraic Combin. 54 (2021), 837–878, DOI, constructs the five chiral-facet/chiral-vertex rows of its Table 4. Its pub-
lished Proposition 11 is cited by the target; the checked arXiv author version calls the corresponding result Proposition
4.2 (printed page 20). For an atomic tight chiral rank-four polytope with both ends chiral it gives
𝑋(𝑃 ) = ⟨𝜎 𝑞′
2 ⟩, 𝑞/𝑞 ′ prime, 𝑋(𝐾) = 𝑋(𝐿) = 𝑋(𝑃 )
as embedded subgroups. Although part (b)’s sentence uses “isomorphic”, its proof and the immediately following
arguments identify these embedded groups. Alternatively, local chirality is contained in global chirality by (V2), and
its nontriviality together with global prime order gives equality. Thus no inference from abstract isomorphism alone is
being made.

22.4. Two distinct minimal regular polytope covers
Use 𝑎, 𝑏, 𝑐 for the three rotation generators and impose
(𝑎𝑏)2 = (𝑏𝑐) 2 = (𝑎𝑏𝑐) 2 = 1. (V4)
Let 𝑃 be given by (V4) together with
𝑎6 = 𝑏 9 = 𝑐 6 = 1, 𝑏𝑎 2 = 𝑎 2𝑏4, 𝑐 2𝑏 = 𝑏 4𝑐2. (V5)
Let 𝑇 be given by (V4) and
𝑎6 = 𝑏 9 = 𝑐 2 = 1, (𝑎𝑏 −2)2 = 1, 𝑎𝑏 2𝑎(𝑎𝑏−1𝑎)2 = 1, (V6)
and let 𝑇 𝛿 be given by (V4) and
𝑎2 = 𝑏 9 = 𝑐 6 = 1, (𝑐 −1𝑏2)2 = 1, 𝑏𝑐 2𝑏(𝑐−1𝑏𝑐−1)2 = 1. (V7)
These are the three literal presentations in the source, not extra quotients chosen to fit its order claims. Put
𝑅 = 𝑃 ⋄ 𝑃 , 𝑄 = 𝑅 ⋄ 𝑇 , 𝑄 ′ = 𝑅 ⋄ 𝑇 𝛿,
where the mirror marking is (𝑎−1, 𝑎2𝑏, 𝑐).
The complete program in Appendix D starts from the bare finitely presented groups (V5)–(V7), obtains GAP isomor-
phisms to finite permutation groups, and constructs every mix as the subgroup generated by paired generators in a direct
product. Exact GAP group algorithms are retained for this finite proof. RAMP, a group catalogue, random sampling,
and the source’s output are not dependencies. The checker asserts the following full table; all orders are rotation-group
orders.
