In each row ⟨𝑏⟩ has order nine, ⟨𝑎⟩ ∩ ⟨𝑏⟩ = ⟨𝑏⟩ ∩ ⟨𝑐⟩ = 1 , and (V4) holds. The check tests subgroup equality,
not merely numerical resemblance, for the successful end intersections. Thus the retained 𝐶 + correspondence proves
that 𝑃 is chiral, 𝑇 , 𝑇𝛿, 𝑄, 𝑄′ are directly regular polytopes, and 𝑅 is not a polytope. The mirror maps are verified
automorphisms; chirality of 𝑃 is verified by the failure of the specified generator assignment to define a homomorphism.
The first-coordinate images of the second-projection kernels in the two end subgroups are both exactly ⟨𝑏3⟩ ≤ Γ(𝑃 ) ,
of order three, as the checker also asserts. Thus K identifies the same nonpolytopality obstruction directly: the medial
polygon has trivial chirality while its nonidentity element 𝑏3 lies in both end chirality groups.
The checker additionally verifies the marked facet isomorphism between 𝑇 and 𝑅, and the dual isomorphism
(𝑎, 𝑏, 𝑐)𝑇 ↦ (𝑐 −1, 𝑏−1, 𝑎−1)𝑇 𝛿 . Since 𝑇 has two facets, it is the trivial extension of that facet in the source’s sense.
The maps 𝑅 → 𝑃 , 𝑄 → 𝑅 , and 𝑄′ → 𝑅 are marked epimorphisms, each with kernel order three. Multiplying
rotation orders by two gives exactly the source’s 648, 1944, and 5832 flags.
For nonisomorphism there is an explicit short certificate:
𝑤 = (𝑏𝑐 −1)2𝑎𝑐2𝑎−1, ord𝑄(𝑤) = 1, ord𝑄′(𝑤) = 3. (V8)
Hence the marked relation kernels differ. An isomorphism of regular polytopes can be composed with a target auto-
morphism to match any chosen base flags, and would then preserve their marked relations. Therefore (V8) excludes an
unbased polytope isomorphism as well. This assertion concerns isomorphisms preserving face ranks; it does not assert
nonduality.
Minimality proof. Any regular polytope cover of 𝑃 factors through its smallest regular maniplex 𝑅, by §22.1. An
intermediate regular polytope between 𝑄 and 𝑃 therefore gives an intermediate quotient between 𝑄 and 𝑅. The latter
map has prime degree three, so its kernel has no intermediate subgroup: the quotient is 𝑄 or 𝑅. The second is not a
polytope, so 𝑄 is minimal. The same argument proves minimality of 𝑄′. Equation (V8) proves there are at least two
different minimal regular polytope covers. This establishes every numerical and structural assertion about the example
in source Section 4.
More generally, if the smallest regular maniplex is polytopal, its universal property makes it the unique minimal regular
polytope cover. This implication requires no finite hypothesis. Its converse, and the proposed analogous multiplicity
for all five tight families, are not consequences of K or of this finite example.
22.5. Relation to the 2025 general mix criterion and current boundaries
Cunningham–Hubard, Polytopality criteria for the mix of polytopes and maniplexes , arXiv:2506.19334, explicitly re-
covers this chiral criterion in Corollary 6.17. Its Theorem 6.3 treats a polytope mixed with a premaniplex under the
stated symmetry-type admissibility hypotheses: local facet and vertex-figure mixes must be polytopal, and a variance-
group intersection inclusion must hold at every relevant flag. Section transitivity can reduce the multiple flag checks.
The two-orbit specialization has additional hypotheses, including medial-section transitivity and the specified orbit
class with the two end colors excluded. Chiral polytopes satisfy that specialization. Thus the later paper already
records the extension; no priority or novelty claim is made here.
Both criteria express the obstruction to polytopality of a specified smallest mixed cover. Neither, by itself, constructs or
classifies larger minimal regular polytope covers when that cover fails the intersection condition. §22.4 adds precisely
such a construction in one finite case. It does not turn the 2025 criterion into a solution of the target’s Section 4
conjectures.
As of 10 October 2026, the arXiv record for 2506.19334 shows only v1, submitted 24 June 2025, and no journal
reference. This is a statement about the checked primary record, not a claim that no other publication exists. A bounded
current search for the chiral unique/multiple-minimal-cover question, inspection of the full 2025 paper’s concluding
questions, and a text search of Hubard–Schulte’s 2026 Two-Orbit Polytopes did not locate a resolution of the two
multiplicity conjectures. That absence is not a proof of their current open status. We leave them unproved and make
no novelty claim about them.
22.6. Version corrections and compression claim
The source extraction prints “no power” in Corollary 3.4 and Example 3.8; this manuscript explicitly says “no noniden-
tity power”. Definition 2.1’s extracted rank-one base case is incompatible with the displayed 𝑈1; we use the standard
rank-two cyclic base case. The source’s unconditional wording for a mix “polytope” is read as a mix object, which is
polytopal only after checking the intersection property. Lost overbars in web extraction are restored using the universal
mirror convention, not interpreted as equality of marked copies. The publisher acknowledgement mentions an earlier
error in Corollary 3.3; we use and prove the author version’s stated condition through (V3), and do not certify the text
of an unacquired publisher PDF.
23. Set-valued Petrials: the exact kernel and thin-case recovery
The operation in this section is that of Bonheure–Buekenhout–Leemans (2001), with equal geometric Petrie sets iden-
tified. Its principal theorem and valid numbered structural conclusions are the targets. The unconditional embedding
assertion is corrected below. No source conclusion is used as a hypothesis, and neither a finite group nor finite residues
are assumed.
Let Γ be a thin regular residually connected rank-three geometry. Label its types vertex, edge and face. At a base
chamber C=(v,e,f), let a change the face, b the edge and c the vertex. Lemma O0 gives
𝐺 = Aut(Γ) = ⟨𝑎, 𝑏, 𝑐⟩, 𝐺 𝑣 = ⟨𝑎, 𝑏⟩, 𝐺 𝑒 = ⟨𝑎, 𝑐⟩, 𝐺 𝑓 = ⟨𝑏, 𝑐⟩, 𝐺 𝑣 ∩ 𝐺𝑒 = ⟨𝑎⟩. (P1)
The other two pair intersections are respectively ⟨b⟩ and ⟨c⟩, and the chamber stabilizer is trivial. Any two of a,b,c
generate a dihedral group, possibly infinite: a word in two involutions alternates after cancellation and is a power of
their product or such a power times one involution. For distinct nonidentity involutions x,y, the intersection ⟨xy⟩∩⟨x⟩
is trivial. Otherwise x is a power of xy and commutes with it, forcing xy=yx and |xy|=2; then x=xy would force y=1.
This proves the rotation/reflection fact used below without a finiteness assumption.
23.1. Geometric operation and its faithful image
Put w=cab. Starting at a chamber, change vertex, face, then edge; at the base chamber the result is wC. At gC, successive
chamber flips act by right multiplication on g, so iteration gives gw^iC. The Petrie face at C is the actual ordered pair
𝑃 (𝐶) = ({𝑤 𝑖𝑣 ∶ 𝑖 ∈ ℤ}, {𝑤 𝑖𝑒 ∶ 𝑖 ∈ ℤ}) . (P2)
Equal pairs are one face; the vertex–edge incidence induced on these sets is understood. Define ΠΓ by retaining old
vertices, edges and their incidence, and adjoining these distinct faces, with incidence to a face given by membership.
This is equivariant: P(gC)=gP(C). In particular wP(C)=P(C), and
𝑤𝑣 = 𝑐𝑣, 𝑤 −1𝑒 = 𝑏𝑒. (P3)
Thus a Petrie face contains v and cv at e, and e and be at v. Each pair is distinct because the corresponding old panel
change is nontrivial.
Lemma P1 (connectivity and exact action). ΠΓ is a residually connected geometry. Its natural automorphism action
ρ has kernel
ker 𝜌 = core𝐺⟨𝑎⟩ = { ⟨𝑎⟩, 𝑎 ∈ 𝑍(𝐺),
1, 𝑎 ∉ 𝑍(𝐺). (P4)
Proof. Every incident old vertex–edge pair extends through an old chamber to its Petrie face. For an object belonging
to a Petrie face, a step of the defining walk supplies an incident object of the other retained type. These facts extend
every flag to a chamber.
Write v_i=w^iv and e_i=w^ie. Equation (P3) says that e_i joins v_i and v_(i+1). The walk therefore connects every
object in its face residue; additional induced incidences preserve connectivity. In the old residue at v, replace every
two-step path e₁–f–e₂ with distinct edges by e ₁–P(v,e₁,f)–e₂. Thinness and (P3) justify the replacement. It connects all
retained edges in the new vertex residue, and every new incident face meets one of them. The analogous replacement
v₁–f–v₂ proves connectivity of each edge residue. Finally an old path through a face can be replaced by a path in its
connected vertex–edge residue. Thus the old vertex–edge truncation, and consequently the new geometry with every
face attached, is connected.
For the action apply K(c) with X the disjoint union of old vertices and edges. They are retained in Y, and every new
face is uniquely encoded by its two actual subsets of X. Equations (Kc2) and (P1) give the core in (P4). A normal
subgroup of ⟨a⟩ is either trivial or all of ⟨a⟩. The latter is normal in G exactly when its unique nonidentity element a is
central. This proves the exact formula and constructs the faithful image G/kerρ. ■
This application uses the same core identity as N008’s faithful coset action. It keeps the action kernel that a set-valued
construction may lose. It asserts neither chamber transitivity of ΠΓ nor injectivity of ρ without further proof.
For an ordinary map, the three successive changes move across an edge to its other endpoint, change the incident face,
and continue along that face. These are exactly the alternating corner choices in the conventional Petrie walk. This
proves the walk-definition assertion of source Lemma 3.1; it does not forbid repetitions or replace geometric faces by
labelled walks.
23.2. The thin obstruction and all conditional conclusions
Theorem P2 (Petrial obstruction and recovery). If ΠΓ is thin, then |ab|=2 or |ac|=2. The action ρ is faithful and
regular and is the full automorphism group of ΠΓ. Applying Π again recovers Γ by an isomorphism fixing all retained
vertices and edges.
Proof. Set P=P(C). Suppose first that bP≠P. Both P and bP contain e and be at v, so the vertex residue of ΠΓ contains
the four-cycle e–P–be–bP–e. A finite cycle in a connected graph of degree two is the entire graph: its vertices already
have their two neighbours, so no path can leave it. Lemma P1 therefore says that precisely two old edges are incident
with v. In the old thin connected vertex residue both edges have degree two, and each incident face meets both. It is
also a four-cycle. Its regular dihedral group ⟨a,b⟩ has four chambers, so |ab|=2.
Suppose instead that bP=P. Since w fixes P, ca=wb fixes P and e. By Lemma O0, the automorphism group of the
thin ΠΓ acts freely on chambers. Its edge–face panel stabilizer embeds in the permutation group on that panel’s two
completions, so ρ(ca)^2=1. Equation (P4) puts (ca)^2 in ⟨a⟩. The rotation/reflection intersection in ⟨a,c⟩ is trivial,
hence (ca)^2=1. Since c≠a, this gives |ac|=2. This proves the obstruction without presuming that b always fixes P or
that the new action is regular.
Interchanging vertex and edge interchanges b,c and changes cab to bac=w^(-1), preserving the same Petrie sets after
that type interchange. We may consequently assume [a,c]=1. Then bwb=w^(-1), bv=v and be=w^(-1)e, so bP=P. The
subgroup ⟨b,w⟩=⟨b,ca⟩ is transitive on the incident vertex–edge pairs of P: its elements give (v_i,e_i) and (v_i,e_(i−1))
for every i. These are distinct pairs, and thinness makes them all pairs at v_i. Every object of P occurs in the walk.
The original chamber-transitive G is transitive on the faces P(gC), so its induced action on ΠΓ is chamber-transitive.
K(c) identifies its image with the full regular automorphism group. The preimage of the new panel stabilizing (v,e) is
exactly G_v∩G_e=⟨a⟩. Equation (Kc3) forces kerρ=1.
At (v,e,P), the new panel involutions are therefore a,b,ca: the last fixes (e,P) and sends v to cv; b fixes (v,P) and changes
e; a exchanges the two faces at (v,e). The new generators generate the same group since c=(ca)a. Their geometric
marking is established by this action, not by assuming a homomorphism from the old marking. Their second Petrie
step is (ca)ab=cb. The cb-orbits of v and e exhaust exactly the old face’s vertex–edge shadow: use the alternating
normal form in its residue group ⟨b,c⟩ from (P1).
Those old face shadows are distinct. Otherwise let f≠f′ have the same shadow and choose (v,e) in it, moving the
base chamber if needed. Thinness gives f′=af. Both f,f′ meet e and be at v. Their four-cycle exhausts the old vertex
residue, as above, forcing [a,b]=1. Together with [a,c]=1 this makes a central in G, and (P4) contradicts kerρ=1. Thus
old faces correspond bijectively to the second Petrie faces, with precisely their original incidences. This proves the
required isomorphism. The vertex–edge-swapped case gives the same conclusion. ■
The commuting pair is the source’s linear-diagram conclusion. Theorem P2 proves its Theorem 1 and the thin-only
formulation in its abstract, together with Lemma 3.2(iii),(iv). Lemma P1 gives Lemma 3.2(i). No finite-residue as-
sumption occurs in the walk, connected-cycle, dihedral or gallery arguments.
23.3. Corrected assertions and exact controls
The unconditional Lemma 3.2(ii) must be read as the natural homomorphism with kernel (P4), not as an embedding.
Indeed take the complete tripartite geometry with two objects of each type. Its full type-preserving group is C ₂³,
regularly permuting eight chambers. Let a,b,c independently change the face, edge and vertex coordinates. The element
cab changes all three, so every Petrie face consists of both vertices and both edges. Deduplication leaves one face. Thus
ΠΓ is the complete tripartite geometry with type sizes (2,2,1), its full group is C ₂², and the kernel is ⟨a⟩. An order-eight
group cannot embed in this order-four automorphism group. This also gives a commuting-pair diagram with nonthin
Petrial, so the principal implication has no converse in this diagram convention.
The unnumbered geometric length/order equality also cannot be unconditional. Take C ₂×S₃, with a generating its
central C₂ and b,c distinct transpositions generating S ₃. The coset geometry with stabilizers in (P1) is a six-cycle on
three vertices and three edges, joined to two faces. It is thin, residually connected and regular: its twelve chambers
form the regular C ₂×S₃ action. On retained objects, cab acts as cb, a rotation of order three, while cab itself has order
six. Its geometric Petrie face has three vertices and three edges, not length six. This assertion is unnecessary for every
proof above.
The optional finite controls construct these examples, a tetrahedron and its vertex–edge interchange, a nonlinear star-
transposition S₄ geometry, and two cube markings from explicit permutations. They use the literal chamber changes and
deduplicate vertex–edge sets; no changed-marking or faithfulness conclusion is assumed. Their full standard-library
program is charged in Appendix E. The two counterexamples above are proved directly, and the infinite theorems do not
follow from finite sampling. No source atlas classification, external map-classification theorem, new open-conjecture
solution, priority claim or complete Lean certificate is asserted.
24. Central quotients and the complete order-1024 classification
This branch concerns the principal nondegenerate classification in Gomi–Loyola–De Las Peñas [GLP18], with the
marking retained. Its equivalence sends the distinguished generator set onto the other set. For a connected string
diagram this permits precisely the two orders related by reversal. It does not identify arbitrary markings of an abstractly
isomorphic group. The rank-two case is the unique dihedral marking of order 1024. The three higher-rank totals below
are 125, 105 and 8; all stated types, facets and universal orders are part of the conclusion.
The specifically retained prior results in this branch are the standard string intersection criteria quoted as [GLP18]
Lemmas 2.2–2.4, the universal geometric interpretation quoted as its Theorem 4.2, the finite smooth square-quotient
classification quoted as its Lemma 5.1, and the exceptional infinite universal decomposition quoted as its Theorem 5.3.
Their relevant precise forms are stated below. Exact finite-group and complete coset-enumeration algorithms in GAP,
and NQ’s universal nilpotent quotient algorithm, are computational inputs. The Atlas supplies definitions of named
marked presentations, all printed in Appendix G; neither its completeness nor a SmallGroups catalogue is assumed.
24.1. Preliminary adapters and exact quotient intersections
Proposition N1 (endpoint cosets). A finite string C-group of rank d and adjacent orders m ₁,…,m_(d−1) has order at
least 2∏mi. In particular, a nondegenerate string two-group of order 1024 has rank at most five.
Proof. Put a=r ₀, b=r ₁ and H=⟨r ₁,…,r_(d−1)⟩. IP gives H∩⟨a,b⟩=⟨b⟩. If H(ab) ⁱ=H(ab)ʲ, then (ab)^(i−j) lies in the
intersection of the rotation subgroup with ⟨b⟩, which is trivial. The m ₁ such cosets, with 0≤i<m₁, are therefore distinct.
Iterating gives the bound. Each m i is at least four in the stated two-group case, so 2^(2d−1)≤1024, forcing d≤5. ■
The smooth Coxeter quotient assertion in source Theorem 2.7 is the homomorphism from the string Coxeter presen-
tation sending each distinguished generator to its marked involution; the specified adjacent orders make it smooth. A
disconnected string diagram yields a direct product of its component subgroups: they commute and IP makes each
meet the subgroup on the other components trivially. This gives the source’s preliminary reduction; a separate census
of every degenerate product is not added to the principal contract. Source Lemma 2.8 is the coordinate-character con-
sequence of Burnside’s basis theorem: an irredundant generating list of a finite two-group maps to a basis of G/Φ(G),
and every binary assignment extends to a homomorphism to C ₂. No infinite-group assertion is made here.
For rank three write L=⟨a,b⟩ and R=⟨b,c⟩. The retained rank-three criterion says that these two rank-two marked
string C-groups, together with [a,c]=1 and L∩R=⟨b⟩, give full IP. Precisely, for d=4 or 5, the versions quoted in source
Lemmas 2.3–2.4 require the prefix H=⟨r₀,…,r_(d−2)⟩ to have IP and each equality H∩⟨rj,…,r_(d−1)⟩=⟨rj,…,r_(d−2)⟩
for j=1,…,d−1, with the final right side trivial. These are respectively all four and all five source hypotheses. All finite
certificates below instead test every subset pair directly.
If N◁G and N∩RL=1, Theorem K(d) says the quotient facets have exactly their old intersection and both embed; their
product orders are unchanged. This is source Lemma 3.1. If N≤L and the displayed images of a,b still give a rank-two
marking on L/N, then N∩R≤⟨b⟩ and b∉N, so R embeds. The last clause of K(d) gives the same facet intersection and
the rank-three criterion gives IP. This is source Lemma 3.2, including its symmetric case. An unrelated marking on the
abstract quotient L/N would not suffice.
24.2. A safe central quotient and the three complete lift patterns
Assume throughout this subsection that G is a finite marked string two-group ⟨a,b,c⟩, with m=|ab|≥4 and n=|bc|≥4.
Put x=(ab)^(m/2) and y=(bc)^(n/2).
Lemma N2 (central elements in an end product).
𝑍(𝐺) ∩ 𝑅𝐿 ⊆ {1, 𝑥, 𝑦, 𝑦𝑥, 𝑦𝑏𝑥}. (N1)
If yx is central, x and y are separately central. The element ybx is never central.
Proof. Every element of RL has a unique expression h(ab)ʲ with h∈R and 0≤j<m. Equality of two expressions would
put a rotation in L∩R=⟨b⟩. Conjugation by b negates j, so centrality implies j=0 or m/2. The former case gives an
element of Z(R)={1,y}. In the latter case write z=hx; since x commutes with b, h lies in C_R(b)={1,b,y,yb}. The
choice h=b fails because the reflection bx does not commute with a when m≥4. This proves (N1).
If yx commutes with a, then y does, because x already does; commuting with c similarly makes x commute with
c. Thus both half-rotations are central. Finally x,y∈Φ(G), since they are even powers of rotations. If z=ybx were
central, then a,z,c would have the same Frattini basis as a,b,c and would generate G. A central z is an involution
here: z∈C_G(x) implies y commutes with x, and y,b,x are commuting involutions. Thus a,z,c are three commuting
involutions, contradicting |ab|≥4. ■
Theorem N3 (central descent with IP). G has a central order-two subgroup N such that the displayed images of a,b,c
give a rank-three string C-group on G/N. Successive such descents, allowing degenerate intermediate markings, stop
at C₂³.
Proof. If x is central, take N=⟨x⟩. The marked dihedral quotient L/N has rotation order m/2≥2 and R embeds because
N∩R=1. The N≤L clause of K(d) proves the quotient intersection. The same reasoning applies to central y. If neither
half-rotation is central, choose any central involution z, which exists in every nontrivial finite two-group. Lemma N2
puts z outside RL; now the injective-ends clause of K(d) proves IP and preserves both end groups. A degenerate rank-
three marking is C₂ times a dihedral marking. Unless it is C ₂³, the central rotation involution of its nonabelian dihedral
factor can be factored out while preserving rank three. Orders strictly decrease by two, so this process terminates
exactly at C₂³. ■
This proves source Corollary 3.3, Lemmas 3.4–3.5 and Theorem 3.6. The bottom boundary is necessary in source
Corollary 3.7: a rank-three order-eight group cannot have a rank-three order-four quotient. Its literal unrestricted
wording is not asserted.
Theorem N4 (three lift patterns with complete presentations). Let Q be a finite rank-three marked string C-group
with a complete presentation in its involutions a ₀,b₀,c₀, including Coxeter relations and additional relators R ₁,…,Rt.
Let G=⟨a,b,c⟩ be a central extension with kernel ⟨z⟩ of order two, mapping those involutions respectively to a ₀,b₀,c₀.
Suppose the lifts are involutions, [a,c]=1 and G has IP. Exactly three order patterns are possible: the left adjacent
order doubles, the right adjacent order doubles, or neither doubles. In each case a complete presentation is obtained
by adjoining central z with z²=1, each defining Coxeter word of Q set equal to its actual value 1 or z (in particular
(ab)^p=z when its quotient order p doubles), and equations R i=z^(εi), εi∈{0,1}. At least one relation expresses z as a
word in a,b,c, so z can be eliminated.
Proof. Each adjacent order is unchanged or doubled. If both doubled, z would be the halfway rotation in both L and
R and hence in their intersection ⟨b⟩, impossible for a nontrivial dihedral rotation. Every defining word of Q lifts to 1
or z. The indicated presentation P maps onto G and has P/⟨z⟩=Q; centrality and z²=1 imply |P|≤2|Q|=|G|, proving that
map an isomorphism. When an adjacent order doubles, its half-power is z. If neither does and every additional relator
lifted to 1, the generating tuple would define a surjection Q→G, contradicting the orders. ■
This is source Theorem 3.8 and Table 1. It classifies successful extensions; it does not assert that every binary lift
choice has the required order or IP. Those properties and equality of ordered markings must still be checked.
24.3. One exhaustive computation for both the seed and all three ranks
Apply Theorems Q5.5–Q5.6 to the path on d vertices with k=log ₂|G|−(2d−1). Appendix A is a single parameterized
program for the four cases in the following table. NQ computes the universal nilpotent quotient of class k+2. The
original generators are transported through its epimorphism and pc isomorphism; they are never replaced by an arbitrary
generating list. Every normal kernel in the invariant ladder is generated, with only equal subgroups deduplicated. The
binary canonical subgroup key is used only after all ambient relative pc orders have been checked to be two.

The ladder sizes are respectively (7,23,61), (7,23,61,147,401), (31,267,1483) and (127). These are computed outputs,
never candidate filters. All 539264 subset-pair intersections are tested, including every candidate rejected by the kernel-
profile criterion, and the two IP decisions agree for every candidate. Burnside’s orbit formula gives (ordered+fixed)/2
reversal classes. The four ordered type-{4,8} markings in the first case satisfy the four distinct word rows in §13,
one each; this is the same finite input used in the eight-family completeness proof. The last three cases prove the
classification at order 1024, with the rank bound in Proposition N1 proving that no further nondegenerate rank is
omitted.

was checked on printed page 16; it is not an inferred change to class counts.
For each of the 106 finite rows, the complete universal presentation is the union of the two complete facet presentations,
shifted to overlapping generator lists, together with the distant-end commutation relation. Completed Todd–Coxeter
enumeration at the trivial subgroup gives 101 orders of 1024 and five orders of 2048. The finite checker also verifies
actual IP and both exact facet embeddings. The remaining row is the explicitly retained infinite universal of §24.6; it
is never sent to an unbounded finite-order test.
24.4. Every printed rank-three presentation and its corrections
The complete finite-presentation protocol in Appendix F takes the bare displayed relators, performs complete trivial-
subgroup Todd–Coxeter enumeration, and checks that the resulting permutation action has the table’s full order. It
evaluates every relator, proves generation by the specified marked involutions, and tests all 4^d marked-subgroup
intersections. Thus its order bounds concern the raw presented groups, not just finite images or nilpotent truncations.
Write C(p,q) for the rank-three Coxeter relations of type {p,q}. In source Table 2, adjoin to C(4,8) the following pairs,

groups has that marked universal string C-group and every member is its smooth quotient. At the group-presentation
level the epimorphism is immediate from the defining words; the abstract-polytope interpretation remains an explicit
background input.

24.6. Square-lattice centers and the exceptional infinite row
Retain the finite smooth square-quotient classification quoted in source Lemma 5.1: a finite smooth quotient of [4,4]
lies in one of the two families obtained by adding (abcb)^s=1, of order 8s², or (abc)^(2s)=1, of order 16s², for a positive
integer s. The former has |abcb|=s and |abc|=2s; the latter has |abcb|=2s and |abc|=2s. The retained decompositions are
(⟨a,cb⟩×⟨c,ab⟩)⋊⟨b⟩ in the first case, and (⟨b,b a⟩×⟨bc,b^(ac)⟩)⋊⟨a,c⟩ in the second. Each displayed factor is dihedral of
order 2s; the complements have orders two and four respectively. These structural identifications are part of the quoted
prior theorem, rather than inferred from the orders. Only the finite scope is used; the infinite Coxeter group itself is not
asserted to admit such an s.
Use the common affine model of §9 after reversing its endpoints and conjugating all three generators by b. Since the
lattices are invariant under the coordinate swap, the same square and diagonal quotients have coordinates
𝑎(𝑥, 𝑦) = (1 − 𝑥, 𝑦), 𝑏(𝑥, 𝑦) = (𝑦, 𝑥), 𝑐(𝑥, 𝑦) = (𝑥, −𝑦). (N5)
Its group is W=ℤ²⋊D ₈; t=abcb translates by (1,0), (abc)² translates by (1,1), (bc)² is (0,−I), and (ab)² is ((1,1),−I).
The two finite families are W/sℤ² and W/L_s, where L_s=⟨(s,s),(s,−s)⟩. The §9 affine normal forms establish these
identifications; equivalently the displayed source relations give surjections onto these affine images of the stated orders,
hence isomorphisms.
Proposition N5 (all source center formulas). For s=2^m, in W/sℤ² the center is ⟨(ab)²,(bc)²⟩ when m=1 and ⟨(abc)^s⟩
when m≥2. In W/L_s it is ⟨ac,(ab)²,(bc)²⟩ when m=0 and ⟨(abcb)^s⟩ when m≥1.
Proof. In a central affine pair (v,M), commutation with all translations forces M to act trivially on T=ℤ²/L. If the
signed-permutation action on T is faithful, M=I and the center is T^(D ₈).
For L=sℤ², s≥4, faithfulness is immediate on the two coordinate vectors. Invariance gives 2x=2y=0 and x=y modulo
s, leaving precisely 0 and (s/2,s/2). For s=2, the action kernel is the diagonal sign subgroup; to commute also with
the linear D ₈ subgroup, M must lie in {I,−I} and v in {0,(1,1)}. This gives the first exceptional center. No first-family
formula at m=0 is claimed in the source or here.
For L=L_s with s≥2, a nontrivial diagonal sign change cannot act trivially, since (2,0) and (0,2) do not lie in L_s. A
signed swap cannot act trivially because its difference on a coordinate vector has a coordinate ±1, not divisible by s.
A fixed translation satisfies (2x,0),(0,2y)∈L_s, hence s divides both x,y; modulo L_s its class is 0 or (s,0), both swap-
invariant. For s=1 the lattice consists of vectors with even coordinate sum, so T=C ₂ with trivial D ₈ action and the
group is C ₂×D₈. Here ac=(t,−I), (bc)²=(0,−I), and (ab)²=(bc)² in the quotient, giving exactly the claimed generating
set. Its three displayed generators are not independent. ■
For the sole infinite row of Table 6 retain the exact source Theorem 5.3, attributed there to McMullen–Schulte: the
universal type-{4,4,4} group with extra relations (abcb) ⁴=(bcd)⁴=1 has
𝑈 = (𝑊 1 × 𝑊2) ⋊ ⟨𝑏, 𝑑⟩, ⟨𝑏, 𝑑⟩ ≅ 𝐶 2
2 , 𝑊 1 = ⟨𝑐 𝑏𝑑, 𝑎, 𝑐𝑏⟩ ≅ [4, 4], 𝑊 2 = ⟨𝑐, 𝑎𝑏, 𝑐𝑑⟩ = 𝑊 𝑏
1 . (N6)
In particular it is infinite. This theorem, including its marked decomposition, is retained and is not a result of finite
coset enumeration.
Proposition N6 (the exceptional kernel location). If π:U→G is a marked epimorphism to a finite string two-group
with IP, then kerπ≤W₁×W₂ and
𝐺 ≅ ((𝑊 1 × 𝑊2)/ ker 𝜋) ⋊ ⟨𝜋(𝑏), 𝜋(𝑑)⟩. (N7)
Proof. IP makes the four marked generators irredundant. Their images form a basis of G/Φ(G). The b- and d-coordinate
characters, composed with π, agree on every generator with the projection U→C₂² in (N6): the displayed W i generators
have even b- and d-parities. Thus every element of kerπ lies in W ₁×W₂. The image of ⟨b,d⟩ still has order four by these
same coordinate characters and meets the image of W ₁×W₂ trivially, proving the split quotient formula. ■
This proves source Lemma 5.4 without assuming quotients preserve IP. At order 1024 the first factor in (N7) has order
256. Finally the two source Theorem 5.5 presentations share the type-{4,4,4} Coxeter relators and (abcb) ⁴=(bcd)⁴=1.
The first adds (dcbabdc)²=1. The second adds
(𝑏𝑑𝑐𝑑𝑏𝑎) 2(𝑐𝑏𝑎𝑏)2 = 1, (𝑎𝑏𝑐𝑏) 2(𝑏𝑎𝑏𝑑𝑐𝑑) 2 = 1. (N8)
Appendix F proves directly that these two raw groups have order 1024 and full IP, and identifies them with the two and
only two census markings whose ordered ends are {4,4} 128 and {4,4}64. Unequal end orders rule out their becoming
equal under reversal. This proves the exact exceptional classification without repeating the source’s separate case
analysis or presuming its presentations finite.
The finite execution in Appendix F totals 214 complete presentations and 43840 actual IP meets: 72 defining Atlas
models, 34 source example/literal-table presentations, the two exceptional presentations, and 106 finite universal pre-
sentations. Its distinct logical role from Appendix A is preserved: the universal-kernel proof gives exhaustion, while
complete raw coset tables and exact marked isomorphisms give presentation and name recognition. All code and math-
ematical data are printed. These are exact finite computations conditional on the stated algorithms, not Lean proofs or
experimental extrapolations. The concluding source suggestion about geometric realizations, a separate classification
of all degenerate products, and historical novelty of the corrections are outside the contract.
25. Every source result as an explicit corollary
The labels below preserve the complete Unified source contracts. The operation contracts are already explicit in Corol-
laries O4.1, O5.1–O5.3, O6.1 and O6.3; the mirror-cover contract is Theorem V3.2 with its five consequences, classified
applications and the complete finite minimality proof in §22. A1–A4 recover the four 1998 theorem clauses, B1–B8
the eight numbered 2002 results in the declared scope, and C1–C11 the stated 2005 consequences, with its false un-
restricted infinite converse labeled as corrected. Each proof uses only common results proved above and the explicit
retained background.
Corollary A1 — N001 Theorem 1(1). Every inductively minimal pair satisfies RWPRI, (2𝑇 )1, and (𝐼𝑃 )2.
Proof. B97 and Lemma D identify it with an unweighted tree geometry. Apply Proposition I, including its leaf argument
in every residue and its explicit two-edge residues. ■
Corollary A2 — N001 Theorem 1(2). An inductively minimal pair of even rank is PRI.
Proof. Its tree has one more vertex than its rank, hence an odd number of vertices. No edge cut is balanced, so
Proposition I applies. ■
Corollary A3 — N001 Theorem 1(3). If an inductively minimal pair is not PRI, it is QPRI; failure of PRI occurs
exactly when its diagram has a middle. In fact every inductively minimal pair is QPRI.
Proof. Proposition I translates a middle into a balanced nonleaf cut, and Lemma O gives the unique chain from that
stabilizer to the full group. All other stabilizers are maximal, so their intervals are chains too. This also disambiguates
the source’s phrase “this is the case”: the middle characterizes failure of PRI, not QPRI by itself. ■
Corollary A4 — N001 Theorem 1(4). An inductively minimal pair is RPRI if and only if its diagram is complete.
Proof. Proposition I gives RPRI exactly for stars, whose line graphs are exactly the complete diagrams. ■
Corollary B1 — N003 Theorem 1.1, finite-group scope. For 𝑁 ⊴ 𝐺, 𝜇(𝐺) ≤ 𝜇(𝐺/𝑁 ) + 𝜇 ′(𝑁 ); if 𝑁 is abelian,
𝜇(𝐺) ≤ 𝜇(𝐺/𝑁 ) + 𝜇 ′
𝐺(𝑁 ).
Proof. Apply Theorem R(a). The finiteness restriction is part of this intake contract; no assertion about infinite maxima
is added. ■
Corollary B2 — N003 Theorem 2.1. If 𝑛 ≥ 7 , every independent generating set 𝑆 of 𝑆𝑛 of size 𝑛 − 1 has, for some
tree 𝑇 on {1, … , 𝑛}, one of the forms
𝑆 = 𝑆(𝑇 ) = {𝜏 𝑒 ∶ 𝑒 ∈ 𝐸(𝑇 )}, 𝑆 = {𝑠} ∪ {(𝑠𝜏 𝑒)𝜖𝑒 ∶ 𝑒 ≠ 𝑑}, 𝑠 = 𝜏 𝑑, 𝜖 𝑒 ∈ {1, −1}.
Conversely every displayed set is an independent generating set.
Proof. Theorem E is the forward direction; Theorems F and P give generation and independence in both converse
directions. ■
Corollary B3 — N003 Corollary 2.2. For 𝑛 ≥ 7 , the number of actual unordered generating sets of the first form is
𝑛𝑛−2. The number of actual unordered sets of the second form is (𝑛 − 1)𝑛 𝑛−2. If a three-cycle and its inverse are
identified independently in such a set, the second number becomes
(𝑛
2)(𝑛 − 1)𝑛−3.
Proof. Ordinary sets recover their unique edge trees, so Cayley’s formula gives the first count. A twisted set has a
unique odd member 𝑠 = (𝑎 𝑏) . Multiplying each of its other actual members ℎ by 𝑠 recovers a transposition. If
ℎ = (𝑠𝜏 𝑒)−1, the recovered edge is the conjugate 𝑠𝜏𝑒𝑠; for an adjacent edge this switches its attachment from 𝑎 to 𝑏,
and for a disjoint edge it changes nothing. Contracting {𝑎, 𝑏} gives the same tree before and after any switches. Every
such independent switch therefore preserves the tree property. Consequently actual twisted sets are in bijection with
trees having one distinguished edge, via the canonical set {𝑠} ∪ {𝑠𝜏 𝑒 ∶ 𝑒 ≠ 𝑑} . This gives (𝑛 − 1)𝑛 𝑛−2. Modulo
inversion of the three-cycles, the data are exactly the pair {𝑎, 𝑏} and a labelled tree on the 𝑛 − 1 blocks obtained by
contracting it. Cayley’s formula gives the last count. ■

independent.
Proof. Use Theorem E to recognize the marking and Theorems F and P for all subset intersections, including the empty
subset. ■

the panel choices (A4.3) give a strongly independent relative generating family.
Proof. This is Theorem R(b), including equality (A4.4) for every parabolic. ■

generating families in a finite group descend to and lift from its Frattini quotient, relative to the image of 𝐵. This
is E4. Its printed unrestricted infinite converse is replaced by E4’s explicit additional hypothesis and the preceding

the pregeometry of their images in 𝐺/𝑁 . This is E3 without any flag-transitivity assumption.
Corollary C4 — N008 Corollary 3.3. Every firm RWPRI coset geometry is isomorphic to its Frattini-quotient coset
geometry. Combine C2 with E3.

Apply C4 and the maximal-subgroup correspondence following E3. Quotient and pullback give the fixed-indexed-
family bijection motivating the paper.

This is the coset-product calculation after E5, with no finiteness or normality assumption.

Proof. Apply D4 and the retained marked quotient/cover correspondence. The false auxiliary source kernel is not
needed; §16 proves its precise failure. ■
Corollary D6 — F016 Theorem 5.1(a–d). For 𝑑 ≥ 3 and 𝑛3, … , 𝑛𝑑 ≥ 5 , there is a finite regular rank- 𝑑 polytope
whose consecutive rank-three sections are exactly{4, 4}(𝑛3), … , {4, 4}(𝑛𝑑), where each square map has automorphism
group of order 2𝑛𝑗. Its group has order 2∑
𝑑
𝑗=3 𝑛𝑗−3(𝑑−3). For 𝑑 ≥ 4 it is (𝑑 − 4, 𝑑 − 1)-flat and hence flat. It has FAP
with respect to its co- (𝑑 − 4) -faces, co-(𝑑 − 3) -faces and facets, with the rank-three co- (−1)-face interpreted as the
whole-polytope cut.
Proof. The square recognition theorem and compatible product Q2.3 give the exact local sections. Equation (Q8.1)
gives the order. The last product factorization gives flatness through the retained coset-face criterion, and Q2.2 supplies
the actual deletion retractions and all FAP cuts. These arguments, including the rank-three convention, are all in §14.

Corollary D7 — F016 Theorem 5.3. The automorphism group in D6 is precisely the quotient of the universal regular
polytope group of generalized type {{4, 4}(𝑛3), … , {4, 4}(𝑛𝑑)} by
[(𝑟𝑖𝑟𝑖+1)2, (𝑟𝑖+2𝑟𝑖+3)2] = 1 (0 ≤ 𝑖 ≤ 𝑑 − 4).
Proof. Section 14 proves Boolean deletions and IP for the bare local presentation, so the universal regular polytope
exists by the ordinary C-group correspondence. Lemma Q8.1 makes the third-lower-central triple kernels normal.
The local lattice indices, global class-two bound and order comparison identify the quotient with the actual product
construction. This retains the source’s entire presentation claim and repairs the diagonal-translation order step. ■
Corollary D8 — F016 Theorem 6.1(a–b). Let 𝑑 ≥ 4 , 𝑛3, … , 𝑛𝑑−1 ≥ 5 , and 𝑚𝑑−1 ≥ 5 . There is an alternating
semiregular rank-𝑑 polytope with regular facet types
(𝑛3, … , 𝑛𝑑−2, 𝑛𝑑−1), (𝑛 3, … , 𝑛𝑑−2, 𝑚𝑑−1).

𝑑−2
𝑗=3 𝑛𝑗+𝑛𝑑−1+𝑚𝑑−1−3(𝑑−3)
if the last parameters differ, and twice this order if they agree. At 𝑑 = 4 , the shared list is empty and the common facet
of the two facets is a square. The remaining co- (𝑑 − 4)-section is not separately prescribed.
Proof. Use D6 for the two facet groups, Q2.3 for the branched compatible product, and the exact retained Monson–

Corollary D9 — F016 Theorem 7.1. For a regular input polytope of rank 𝑑 ≥ 3 whose group is a finite or infinite two-
group in the source’s convention, its free regular extension has rank 𝑑 + 1 and finitely generated infinite automorphism
group with every finite-order element of two-power order.
Proof. The retained regular free-extension theorem gives the geometry and its amalgam. Lemma Q9.1 proves infinitude,
finite generation and the finite-torsion assertion, including infinite input. ■
Corollary D10 — F016 Theorem 7.2. For an oriented chiral input polytope of rank 𝑑 ≥ 3 with directly regular facets
and finite or infinite two-group automorphism group, its free oriented chiral extension has rank 𝑑 + 1 and a finitely
generated infinite two-group in the same convention.
Proof. The retained Schulte–Weiss theorem gives the geometry and amalgam over the facet rotation group. That
rotation group embeds in the input group; its full facet group is an index-two extension, so Lemma Q9.1 transfers the
torsion property and proves the extension conclusions. No full-facet embedding in the chiral group is assumed. ■
Corollary D11 — F016 Theorem 7.3. Let two regular rank-𝑑 polytopes, 𝑑 ≥ 3 , have isomorphic facets and finite or
infinite two-group automorphism groups. Their universal alternating semiregular extension has rank 𝑑 + 1 and finitely
generated infinite two-group automorphism group, with the additional branch swap precisely when the two facets of
the extension are isomorphic.
Proof. The retained universal alternating-extension theorem gives the group amalgam and possible index-two extension.
Lemma Q9.1 gives all three group properties in both cases. ■

Theorem K(0) replaces the two copies of the same image-lifting proof; K(a) is its marked corollary. K(b) retains its

identification. The relative-generation and primitive-coordinate core remains shared by the finite-p and two-group
branches. Endpoint conjugation shares geometric recognition with the J1 census and generalized-cube applications,
but is not assumed to be a homomorphic image of the old marking.
The laminar forest proof cannot be deleted: adjacent tree transpositions satisfy an odd braid relation that Boolean
deletion would destroy. The cyclic-model proof, its bounded order-256 word test, all FAP cuts, the complete J1 residue
census and all mirror/local-polytopality arguments also remain. No theorem of this manuscript claims to replace their
distinct hypotheses by a generic matrix computation. The newer marked parabolic comparison theorem is not used: no
application-specific product injectivity has been established that would shorten the endpoint proof.
The necessary finite programs are printed in full in Appendices A–D and F–H. Appendix E’s optional Petrial controls
remain fully charged. Appendix A now uses the common rank/excess theorem to supply both L035’s order-256 seed
and N009’s order-1024 exhaustion, removing the former SmallGroups order-256 completeness input. Appendices B–
C retain the complete TomLib J1 subgroup table and exact algorithms; Appendix D is unchanged. Appendix F uses
complete raw presentations and exact marked isomorphisms for every N009 source label and example. Appendix G
prints all 72 primary named presentations and all 107 Table 6 rows; duplicate permutation arrays are unnecessary
because the primary labels explicitly define these finitely presented models. Appendix H is the complete fresh replay
runner. GAP/NQ and the named-presentation transcription boundary remain explicit. No source totals or label list
filters the kernel search.
The new K(d) is an exact downward image-intersection theorem; the existing K(a) recognizes the quotient marking after
transport to the canonical class-two base. The common forest quotient supplies the bound in all four census cases, and
the same affine model supplies the center formulas. Central-involution selection, successful extension presentations
and finite marked recognition still require their printed source-specific proofs. This is an actual shared theorem with
two classification applications, not an identification of arbitrary finite examples with a universal matrix model.
All four previous nonseed programs are byte-identical to the reviewed predecessor; the new complete executions are
separately required. Runtime versions, data hashes, logs and output certificates remain charged audit artifacts. No
new Lean formalization, historical priority or numerical compression ratio is asserted. A future common measurement
must count all prose, code and mathematical data, charge wrappers/outputs consistently and declare every retained
background.

in the ten-source assembly remains part of that earlier frozen record. The current branch uses no atlas or map classifica-
tion. Ens’s full toroidal classification, the older tight-family classification and the phase46 minimal-cover exploration

Appendix A. Complete rank/excess kernel enumeration
Theorems Q5.5–Q5.6 prove the written bound and completeness of this finite search. All accepted and rejected terminal
kernels receive all actual IP meets. This single program is charged once for the seed and the three order-1024 ranks;
exact versioned execution is recorded by Appendix H.
# Complete path-marked two-group census, parameterized by rank and excess.
# Theorems Q5.5/Q5.6 prove candidate completeness and profile recognition.
# Adapted from the reviewed phase21/22 ladder; original markings are preserved.
# Every candidate, including profile rejections, receives all 4^d actual meets.
# Four parameter cases share this program; no source count or name filters it.
# The order-256 case additionally checks the four exact L035 seed word patterns.
# Use a fresh working directory. Existing mathematical output paths are refused.
if LoadPackage("nq")= fail then Error("NQ required" );fi;
SizeScreen([240,40]);;
Run78:= function(d,excess78,prefix78)
local F,f,rel,i,j,Q,epi,iso,G,r,lc,K,pc,key,levels,M,C,pi,V,
maxes,L,N,known,nxt,k,edges,maxdim,dim,at,start,allChecks,ss,
pars,good,accepted,hist,typ,kernelType,rev,selfdual,dualclasses,
acceptedKernels,totalCandidates,gi,gj,pos,typcounts,typekey,
inds,ambientRows,pcweights,ks,ps,kg,qp,kpars,kchecks,profileGood,
rejectedProfiles,acceptedSelfTypes,selfTypeKey,facetOrders,facets,
acceptedDetails,checkpointName,a78,b78,c78,r78,d078,z78,patterns78,seed78;
if not (d>= 3 and excess78>= 1) then Error("Rank/excess input" );fi;
for k in [ 1..excess78] do
if IsExistingFile(Concatenation(prefix78,".level",String(k),".json")) then Error("Existing
checkpoint");fi;
od;
if IsExistingFile(Concatenation(prefix78,".accepted.json")) or
IsExistingFile(Concatenation(prefix78,".seed.json")) then Error("Existing output" );fi;
seed78:= [];
F:= FreeGroup(d);f:= GeneratorsOfGroup(F);rel:= List(f,x->x^2);
for i in [ 1..d] do for j in [ i+1..d] do
if j-i>1 then Add(rel,Comm(f[i],f[j]));fi;
od;od;
Q:= F/rel;epi:= NqEpimorphismNilpotentQuotient(Q,excess78+2);
iso:= IsomorphismPcGroup(Image(epi));G:= Image(iso);
r:= List(GeneratorsOfGroup(Q),x->Image(iso,Image(epi,x)));
lc:= LowerCentralSeries(G);K:= lc[3];pc:= Pcgs(G);pcweights:= List([1..Length(pc)],t->2^(t-1));
if not ForAll(RelativeOrders(pc),n->n= 2) then Error("Binary canonical-pcgs key requires
relative orders two" );fi;
if Index(G,K)<>2^(2*d-1) then Error("Universal canonical quotient order" );fi;
key:= function(H)
return List(CanonicalPcgs(InducedPcgsByGenerators(pc,GeneratorsOfGroup(H))),
x->ExponentsOfPcElement(pc,x)*pcweights);
end;
Print("VERSION GAP=" ,GAPInfo.Version," NQ=" ,PackageInfo("nq")[1].Version,"\n");
Print("UNIVERSAL rank=" ,d," excess=" ,excess78," order=" ,Size(G)," gamma3=" ,Size(K),"
abelianization_type=" ,AbelianInvariants(K),"\n");
levels:= [K];start:= Runtime();
for k in [ 1..excess78] do
known:= NewDictionary([],true);nxt:= [];edges:= 0;maxdim:= 0;at:= 0;
for M in levels do
C:= ClosureGroup(CommutatorSubgroup(M,G),FrattiniSubgroup(M));
pi:= NaturalHomomorphismByNormalSubgroup(M,C);V:= Image(pi);
if not IsElementaryAbelian(V) then Error("Coinvariant square quotient" );fi;
dim:= LogInt(Size(V),2);maxdim:= Maximum(maxdim,dim);
maxes:= MaximalSubgroups(V);
if Length(maxes)<>2^dim-1 then Error("All hyperplanes" );fi;
for L in maxes do
N:= PreImage(pi,L);edges:= edges+1;
inds:= key(N);
if LookupDictionary(known,inds)= fail then
if Index(K,N)<>2^k or not IsNormal(G,N) then Error("Invariant ladder kernel" );fi;
AddDictionary(known,inds,Length(nxt)+1);Add(nxt,N);
fi;
od;
at:= at+1;
if at mod 100= 0 then Print("PROGRESS depth=" ,k," parents=" ,at,"/",Length(levels),"
unique=" ,Length(nxt)," elapsed_ms=" ,Runtime()-start,"\n");fi;
od;
Print("LEVEL index=" ,2^k," parents=" ,Length(levels)," hyperplane_edges=" ,edges,"
unique=" ,Length(nxt)," max_coinvariant_dimension=" ,maxdim,"
elapsed_ms=" ,Runtime()-start,"\n");
levels:= nxt;
if true then
checkpointName:= Concatenation(prefix78,".level",String(k),".json");
PrintTo(checkpointName,List(levels,key),"\n");
fi;
od;
# Exact actual-IP checks for every final candidate, including every meet.
ss:= Combinations([1..d]);allChecks:= 0;accepted:= 0;selfdual:= 0;
ks:= List(ss,S->Intersection(K,Subgroup(G,r{S})));
kchecks:= 0;rejectedProfiles:= 0;acceptedSelfTypes:= [];acceptedDetails:= [];
acceptedKernels:= [];hist:= [];typcounts:= [];totalCandidates:= Length(levels);
rev:= GroupHomomorphismByImages(G,G,r,Reversed(r));
if rev= fail or not IsBijective(rev) then Error("Marked reversal automorphism" );fi;
at:= 0;
for N in levels do
# The kernel quotient has order 2^excess78 and need not be abelian.
qp:= NaturalHomomorphismByNormalSubgroup(K,N);kg:= Image(qp);
if Size(kg)<>2^excess78 then Error("Small kernel quotient" );fi;
kpars:= List(ks,H->Image(qp,H));profileGood:= true;
for i in [ 1..Length(ss)] do
for j in [ i+1..Length(ss)] do
pos:= Position(ss,Intersection(ss[i],ss[j]));
kchecks:= kchecks+1;
if Intersection(kpars[i],kpars[j])<>kpars[pos] then profileGood:= false;break;fi;
od;
if not profileGood then break;fi;
od;
if not profileGood then
rejectedProfiles:= rejectedProfiles+1;
if at mod 100= 0 then Print("PROFILE_PROGRESS candidates=" ,at,"/",totalCandidates,"
accepted=" ,accepted," elapsed_ms=" ,Runtime()-start,"\n");fi;
fi;
pi:= NaturalHomomorphismByNormalSubgroup(G,N);Q:= Image(pi);
f:= List(r,x->Image(pi,x));
if Size(Q)<>2^(2*d-1+excess78) or Subgroup(Q,f)<>Q then Error("Candidate order/marking" );fi;
pars:= List(ss,S->Subgroup(Q,f{S}));good:= true;
for i in [ 1..Length(ss)] do for j in [ 1..Length(ss)] do
pos:= Position(ss,Intersection(ss[i],ss[j]));
if Intersection(pars[i],pars[j])<>pars[pos] then good:= false;fi;
allChecks:= allChecks+1;
od;od;
if good<>profileGood then Error("Kernel-profile and actual-IP criteria disagree" );fi;
if good then
accepted:= accepted+1;Add(acceptedKernels,key(N));
if Image(rev,N)= N then selfdual:= selfdual+1;fi;
typ:= List([1..d-1],i->Order(f[i]*f[i+1]));
if Minimum(typ)<4 then Error("Degenerate marking escaped canonical quotient" );fi;
kernelType:= AbelianInvariants(Image(pi,K)/DerivedSubgroup(Image(pi,K)));
Add(hist,[Length(LowerCentralSeries(Q))-1,kernelType]);
typekey:= Minimum(typ,Reversed(typ));Add(typcounts,typekey);
if Image(rev,N)= N then Add(acceptedSelfTypes,typekey);fi;
facetOrders:= [Size(Subgroup(Q,f{[1..d-1]})),Size(Subgroup(Q,f{[2..d]}))];
Add(acceptedDetails,[key(N),typ,Length(LowerCentralSeries(Q))-1,kernelType,Image(rev,N)= N,
facetOrders]);
if d= 3 and excess78= 3 and typ= [4,8] then
a78:= f[1];b78:= f[2];c78:= f[3];r78:= b78*c78;d078:= (a78*b78)^2;z78:= r78^4;
patterns78:= [];
if Comm(a78,r78^2)^2= One(Q) and Comm(a78,r78^4)= One(Q) then Add(patterns78,1);fi;
if Comm(a78,r78^2)^2= z78 and Comm(a78,r78^4)= One(Q) then Add(patterns78,2);fi;
if Comm(d078,r78^2)= z78 then Add(patterns78,3);fi;
if Comm((r78^2)^a78,r78)= z78 then Add(patterns78,4);fi;
if Length(patterns78)<>1 then Error("Unclassified or overlapping order-256 word
pattern");fi;
Add(seed78,[key(N),patterns78[1]]);
fi;
fi;
at:= at+1;
if at mod 100= 0 then Print("IP_PROGRESS candidates=" ,at,"/",totalCandidates,"
accepted=" ,accepted," elapsed_ms=" ,Runtime()-start,"\n");fi;
od;
dualclasses:= (accepted+selfdual)/2;
Print("RESULT rank=" ,d," excess=" ,excess78," candidates=" ,totalCandidates,"
ordered=" ,accepted," selfdual=" ,selfdual," up_to_reversal=" ,dualclasses,"
actual_meets=" ,allChecks," elapsed_ms=" ,Runtime()-start,"\n");
Print("CLASS_KERNEL_ABELIANIZATION_HISTOGRAM " ,Collected(hist),"\n");
Print("ORDERED_CANONICAL_TYPE_HISTOGRAM " ,Collected(typcounts),"\n");
Print("SELFDUAL_TYPE_HISTOGRAM " ,Collected(acceptedSelfTypes),"\n");
Print("KERNEL_PROFILE_CHECKS comparisons=" ,kchecks," rejected=" ,rejectedProfiles,"
directly_verified=" ,accepted,"\n");
if true then
PrintTo(Concatenation(prefix78,".accepted.json"),acceptedDetails,"\n");
fi;
PrintTo(Concatenation(prefix78,".seed.json"),seed78,"\n");
if Length(seed78)>0 then Print("SEED_WORD_PATTERNS " ,Collected(List(seed78,x->x[2])),"\n");fi;
Print("PASS: complete invariant-kernel ladder and all-candidate actual-IP cross-check; counts
are output, never candidate filters.\n" );
return rec( rank:= d,ordered:= accepted,selfdual:= selfdual,dualclasses:= dualclasses,kernel_keys
:= acceptedKernels);
end;;
if not IsBound(cases78) then cases78:= [[3,3],[3,5],[4,3],[5,1]];fi;
results78:= [];
for case78 in cases78 do
Add(results78,Run78(case78[1],case78[2],Concatenation("phase78_d",String(case78[1]),"_k",
String(case78[2]))));
od;
QUIT;
Only the four tested seed word implications enter the infinite-parameter proof in §13. No order-1024 assertion is used to
justify that seed. The original SmallGroups IDs and anchored counts are not conclusions required by the new contract.
Appendix B. Complete finite construction certificate
The following code is part of this manuscript, not an excluded implementation appendix. It searches for its own source
marking in the retained ambient group, rather than reading an earlier geometry census or a hard-coded witness. It then
verifies the presentation upper bound, every marked intersection and all required typed-Helly equalities.
# Entire finite certificate for Compress_C. No geometry census is read.
LoadPackage("tomlib");;
SizeScreen([100000,24]);;
CheckC:= function(test,msg) if not test then Error(msg);fi;end;;
G:= UnderlyingGroup(TableOfMarks("J1"));;
CheckC(Size(G)= 175560,"ambient J1 order" );;
FindSeedC:= function(K)
local a,b,c,d,iv,bs;
a:= GeneratorsOfGroup(K)[1];
CheckC(Order(a)= 2,"ambient first generator" );
iv:= Filtered(Elements(Centralizer(K,a)),x->Order(x)= 2);
for d in iv do
if d= a then continue;fi;
bs:= Filtered(Elements(Centralizer(K,d)),x->Order(x)= 2 and Order(a*x)= 5);
for c in iv do
if Order(c*d)<>5 then continue;fi;
for b in bs do
if Order(b*c)= 3 and (a*b*c)^5= One(K)
and ((d*c*b)^5*a)^3= One(K) and Group(a,b,c,d)= K then
return [ a,b,c,d];
fi;
od;
od;
od;
Error("no source seed" );
end;;
s:= FindSeedC(G);;
pairs:= Combinations([1..4],2);;
CheckC(List(pairs,p->Order(s[p[1]]*s[p[2]]))= [5,2,2,3,2,5],"seed pairs" );;
CheckC(Normalizer(G,Group(s{[1,2,3]}))= Group(s{[1,2,3]}),"seed facet normalizer" );;
# Exact upper bound for the literal source presentation.
F:= FreeGroup("a","b","c","d");; u:= GeneratorsOfGroup(F);;
rels:= Concatenation(List(u,x->x^2),List([1..6],i->
(u[pairs[i][1]]*u[pairs[i][2]])^[5,2,2,3,2,5][i]),
[(u[1]*u[2]*u[3])^5,((u[4]*u[3]*u[2])^5*u[1])^3]);;
Q:= F/rels;; q:= GeneratorsOfGroup(Q);;
ct:= CosetTable(Q,Subgroup(Q,q{[2,3,4]}));;
CheckC(Length(ct[1])= 1463,"presentation coset index" );;
CheckC(Size(Group(List([1,3,5,7],i->PermList(ct[i]))))= 175560,"presentation image" );;
w:= (s[2]*s[3]*s[4])^5;;
CheckC(Order(w)= 2 and ForAll(s{[2,3,4]},x->Comm(w,x)= ()),"twist center" );;
marks:= [s,[s[1],w*s[2],s[3],s[4]],
[s[1],s[2],s[3],s[3]^s[4]],
[s[2]^s[1],s[2],s[3],s[4]],
[s[2]^s[1],s[2],s[3],s[3]^s[4]],
[(w*s[2])^s[1],w*s[2],s[3],s[4]]];;
CheckC(Intersection(Group(s{[2,3,4]}),Group(s{[2,3,4]})^s[1])
= Group(s{[3,4]}),"left overlap" );;
CheckC(Intersection(Group(s{[1,2,3]}),Group(s{[1,2,3]})^s[4])
= Group(s{[1,2]}),"right overlap" );;
VerifyC:= function(t)
local ss,subs,a,b,ip,pars,i,j,J,rows,T,common,expected,h,pan;
ss:= Combinations([1..4]);
subs:= List(ss,J->Group(Concatenation([()],t{J})));
ip:= 0;
for a in [ 1..16] do for b in [ 1..16] do
if Intersection(subs[a],subs[b])<>subs[Position(ss,Intersection(ss[a],ss[b]))]
then ip:= ip+1;fi;
od;od;
pars:= List([1..4],i->Group(t{Difference([1..4],[i])}));
h:= [];
for i in [ 1..4] do
rows:= [];
for j in Difference([1..4],[i]) do
T:= Intersection(pars[i],pars[j]);
rows[j]:= Set(List(RightTransversal(pars[j],T),x->RightCoset(pars[i],x)));
od;
for J in Combinations(Difference([1..4],[i])) do
if Length(J)<2 then continue;fi;
common:= Length(Intersection(rows{J})); T:= Intersection(pars{J});
expected:= Index(T,Intersection(T,pars[i]));
Add(h,[i,J,common,expected]);
od;
od;
pan:= List([1..4],i->Size(Intersection(pars{Difference([1..4],[i])})));

parabolics:= List(pars,Size),types:= List(pars,StructureDescription),
ip_failures:= ip,helly:= h,borel:= Size(Intersection(pars)),panels:= pan);
end;;

[120,120,20,60],[120,120,60,60],[120,120,20,660]];;

Appendix C. Complete exhaustive residue-extension certificate
This program reads no geometry representative list. It derives the maximal-subgroup classes from the complete group
table, enumerates local unordered marker orbits, tests full IP and typed Helly, and extends every retained proper residue
by all possible outside involutions. Early rejection only uses failure of the necessary irredundance condition. All six
rank-four classes and the absence of rank-five residues are outputs of the search; their asserted final counts are checked
after enumeration.
# Complete finite J1 geometry census by proper-residue extension.
# Relative to the complete TomLib J1 subgroup table, not a geometry census.
LoadPackage("tomlib");;
SizeScreen([100000,24]);;
tom:= TableOfMarks("J1");;G:= UnderlyingGroup(tom);;
ids:= MaximalSubgroupsTom(tom)[1];;
ns:= Length(OrdersTom(tom));; incidence:= SubsTom(tom);;
derived:= Filtered([1..ns-1],i->not ForAny([i+1..ns-1],j->i in incidence[j]));;
if derived<>SortedList(ids) or ns<>40 then Error("maximal subgroup catalogue" );fi;
Print("MAXIMAL_CLASSES " ,derived," ORDERS
",List(ids,i->Size(RepresentativeTom(tom,i))),"\n");;
CheckCensus:= function(t)
local n,I,i,j,k,K,L,ss,subs,P,rows,J,common,T;
n:= Length(t); I:= [1..n];
# Irredundance is necessary for IP and rejects most global extensions.
for i in I do
K:= Group(t{Difference(I,[i])});
if t[i] in K then return fail;fi;
od;
ss:= Combinations(I);
subs:= List(ss,J->Group(Concatenation([()],t{J})));
for i in [ 1..Length(ss)] do for j in [ i+1..Length(ss)] do
if Intersection(subs[i],subs[j])<>subs[Position(ss,Intersection(ss[i],ss[j]))]
then return fail;fi;
od;od;
P:= List(I,i->Group(t{Difference(I,[i])}));
for i in I do
rows:= [];
for j in Difference(I,[i]) do
K:= Intersection(P[i],P[j]);
rows[j]:= Set(List(RightTransversal(P[j],K),x->RightCoset(P[i],x)));
od;
for J in Combinations(Difference(I,[i])) do
if Length(J)<2 then continue;fi;
T:= Intersection(P{J});
if Length(Intersection(rows{J}))<>Index(T,Intersection(T,P[i]))
then return fail;fi;
od;
od;
return rec( marking:= t,order:= Size(Group(t)),parabolics:= List(P,Size));
end;;
localReps:= rec();;
for k in [ 3,4] do

od;

",count,"\n");
od;

od;
Print("LOCAL_ENUMERATION_DONE\n");
involClasses:= Filtered(ConjugacyClasses(G),c->Order(Representative(c))= 2);;
if Length(involClasses)<>1 then Error("single involution class required" );fi;
ivG:= SortedList(Elements(involClasses[1]));;
if Length(ivG)<>1463 then Error("involution count" );fi;
a0:= ivG[1];; C0:= Elements(Centralizer(G,a0));;
if Length(C0)<>120 then Error("involution centralizer" );fi;
conjCache:= [];;
CanonicalCensus:= function(t)
local ans,x,p,g,c,key;
ans:= fail;
for x in t do
p:= PositionSorted(ivG,x);
if not IsBound(conjCache[p]) then
g:= RepresentativeAction(G,x,a0);
if g= fail or x^g<>a0 then Error("involution conjugator" );fi;
conjCache[p]:= g;
fi;
g:= conjCache[p];
for c in C0 do
key:= SortedList(List(t,y->PositionSorted(ivG,y^(g*c))));
if ans= fail or key<ans then ans:= key;fi;
od;
od;
return ans;
end;;
globalClasses:= [];;fiveClasses:= [];;classParabolics:= [];;
for k in [ 3,4] do
keys:= [];unique:= [];
for t in localReps.(String(k)) do
key:= CanonicalCensus(t);
if not key in keys then Add(keys,key);Add(unique,t);fi;
od;
Print("GLOBAL_LOCAL_ORBITS " ,k," " ,Length(unique),"\n");
tested:= 0;passed:= 0;start:= Runtime();

if x in K then continue;fi;

Add(globalClasses,key);Add(classParabolics,SortedList(r.parabolics));

elif k= 4 and not key in fiveClasses then

" PASSED " ,passed," SECONDS " ,(Runtime()-start)/1000,"\n");

Appendix D. Complete chiral-cover finite certificate
This complete program begins with the literal source presentations, constructs their mirror mixes, checks every relevant
C+ intersection and marked cover, verifies local chirality, and supplies the distinguishing word. The abstract universal-
property and minimality proofs are in §22. Its bytes are identical to the frozen phase41 program.
# All source presentations are literal. GAP's exact finitely presented/permutation
# group algorithms are retained; this is not a Lean kernel certificate.
SetInfoLevel(InfoWarning,0);;
check:= function(ok,label) if not ok then Print("FAIL " ,label,"\n");QUIT_GAP(1);fi;end;;
F:= FreeGroup("a","b","c");; a:= F.1;; b:= F.2;; c:= F.3;;
base:= [(a*b)^2,(b*c)^2,(a*b*c)^2];;
prs:= rec(P:= Concatenation([a^6,b^9,c^6],base,[b*a^2*(a^2*b^4)^-1,c^2*b*(b^4*c^2)^-1]),T:=
Concatenation([a^6,b^9,c^2],base,[(a*b^-2)^2,a*b^2*a*(a*b^-1*a)^2]),Td:= Concatenation([a^2,b^9
,c^6],base,[(c^-1*b^2)^2,b*c^2*b*(c^-1*b*c^-1)^2]));;
make:= function(name)
local fp,iso,g,s;
fp:= F/prs.(name);iso:= IsomorphismPermGroup(fp);g:= Image(iso);
check(IsBijective(iso),Concatenation(name," fp isomorphism" ));
s:= List(GeneratorsOfGroup(fp),x->Image(iso,x));
check(ForAll(prs.(name),w->MappedWord(w,GeneratorsOfGroup(F),s)= One(g)),Concatenation(name,"
literal relators" ));
return rec( g:= g,s:= s);
end;;
mix:= function(A,B)
local d,e1,e2,s;
d:= DirectProduct(A.g,B.g);e1:= Embedding(d,1);e2:= Embedding(d,2);
s:= List([1..3],i->Image(e1,A.s[i])*Image(e2,B.s[i]));
return rec( g:= Group(s),s:= s);
end;;
P:= make("P");;T:= make("T");;Td:= make("Td");;
Pm:= rec(g:= P.g,s:= [P.s[1]^-1,P.s[1]^2*P.s[2],P.s[3]]);;
R:= mix(P,Pm);;Q:= mix(R,T);;Qd:= mix(R,Td);;
expected:= rec(P:= [324,[6,9,6],[54,54],9,false],T:= [324,[6,9,2],[162,18],9,true],Td:= [324,[2,9,
6],[18,162],9,true],R:= [972,[6,9,6],[162,162],27,true],Q:= [2916,[6,9,6],[162,162],9,true],Qd:=
[2916,[6,9,6],[162,162],9,true]);;
A:= P;;
for name in [ "P","T","Td","R","Q","Qd"] do
A:= ValueGlobal(name);f:= Group(A.s{[1,2]});v:= Group(A.s{[2,3]});m:= Group([A.s[2]]);
reg:= GroupHomomorphismByImages(A.g,A.g,A.s,[A.s[1]^-1,A.s[1]^2*A.s[2],A.s[3]]);
row:= [Size(A.g),List(A.s,Order),[Size(f),Size(v)],Size(Intersection(f,v)),reg<>fail];
check(row= expected.(name),Concatenation(name," exact row" ));
check(Size(m)= 9,Concatenation(name," medial" ));
check(Intersection(Group([A.s[1]]),m)= TrivialSubgroup(A.g),Concatenation(name," facet IP" ));
check(Intersection(Group([A.s[3]]),m)= TrivialSubgroup(A.g),Concatenation(name," vertex IP" ));
check(ForAll(base,w->MappedWord(w,GeneratorsOfGroup(F),A.s)= One(A.g)),Concatenation(name,"
rotation relations" ));
if reg<>fail then check(IsBijective(reg),Concatenation(name," mirror automorphism" ));fi;
if name<>"R" then check(Intersection(f,v)= m,Concatenation(name," end IP" ));else
check(Intersection(f,v)<>m,"R fails IP" );fi;
Print("ROW " ,name," " ,row,"\n");
od;
for pair in [[ R,P],[Q,R],[Qd,R]] do
cov:= GroupHomomorphismByImages(pair[1].g,pair[2].g,pair[1].s,pair[2].s);
check(cov<>fail and IsSurjective(cov),"marked cover" );
check(Size(Kernel(cov))= 3,"degree three" );
od;
p1:= GroupHomomorphismByImages(R.g,P.g,R.s,P.s);;

check(p1<>fail and p2<>fail,"two mirror projections" );

check(Xf= Group([P.s[2]^3]) and Xv= Xf,"local chirality obstruction b^3" );
Print("CHIRALITY facet=vertex=<b^3> ORDER 3\n" );

check(dualiso<>fail and IsBijective(dualiso),"Td is literal dual of T" );
w:= (b*c^-1)^2*a*c^2*a^-1;;

witness");

Appendix E. Complete Petrial operation and kernel controls
This standard-library program constructs the geometry and its actual set-valued Petrial from explicit permutations. It
checks all finite incidence/residue conditions, both branches of the face-stabilizer split, the exact action kernel against
the intersection of conjugates, the central-involution criterion and thin-case bidual shadows. The two source boundaries
and every general theorem already have full proofs in §23. The program is a strengthened, fully charged version of the
phase25 checker; its seven examples are not a census.
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""Finite controls for the source-defined Petrial and exact retained-object kernel.
Standard library only. The operation acts on chambers of incidence structures,
not on a presumed replacement generating set. Original examples use explicit
permutations, without a group catalogue or external presentation library.
"""
import argparse
import hashlib
import itertools
import json
from pathlib import Path
def mul(p, q):
return tuple(p[q[i]] for i in range(len(p)))
def inv(p):
out = [0] * len(p)
for i, v in enumerate(p):
out[v] = i
return tuple(out)
def cyc(n, *cycles):
out = list(range(n))
for cycle in cycles:
for a, b in zip(cycle, cycle[ 1:] + cycle[:1]):
out[a] = b
return tuple(out)
def closure(gens):
one = tuple(range(len(gens[0])))
seen, queue = {one}, [one]
for g in queue:
for s in gens:
h = mul(g, s)
if h not in seen:
seen.add(h)
queue.append(h)
return frozenset(seen)
def order(p):
return len(closure([p]))
def connected(vertices, adjacent):
if not vertices:
return False
seen, queue = {vertices[0]}, [vertices[ 0]]
for v in queue:
for w in vertices:
if w not in seen and adjacent(v, w):
seen.add(w)
queue.append(w)
return len(seen) == len(vertices)
class Geometry:
def __init__(self, sizes, pairs):
self.sizes = sizes
self.pairs = {k: frozenset(v) for k, v in pairs.items()}
self.vertices = [(i, j) for i, s in enumerate(sizes) for j in range(s)]
self.chambers = tuple(c for c in itertools.product(*map(range, sizes))
if all((c[i], c[j]) in self.pairs[i, j]
for i, j in ((0, 1), ( 0, 2), ( 1, 2))))
self.panels = {}
for c in self.chambers:
for i in range(3):
key = (i, tuple(c[j] for j in range(3) if j != i))
self.panels.setdefault(key, []).append(c)
def adjacent(self, x, y):
i, a = x
j, b = y
if i == j:
return a == b
if i > j:
i, j, a, b = j, i, b, a
return (a, b) in self.pairs[i, j]
def properties(self):
geometry = bool(self.chambers)
for i, s in enumerate(self.sizes):
geometry &= all(any(c[i] == a for c in self.chambers) for a in range(s))
for (i, j), edges in self.pairs.items():
geometry &= all(any(c[i] == a and c[j] == b for c in self.chambers)
for a, b in edges)
thin = geometry and all(len(v) == 2 for v in self.panels.values())
rc = connected(self.vertices, self.adjacent)
for v in self.vertices:
residue = [w for w in self.vertices if w[0] != v[0] and self.adjacent(v, w)]
rc &= connected(residue, self.adjacent)
return {'geometry': bool(geometry), 'thin': bool(thin),
'residually_connected': bool(rc), 'type_sizes': list(self.sizes),
'chambers': len(self.chambers)}
def flip(self, c, i):
key = (i, tuple(c[j] for j in range(3) if j != i))
choices = self.panels[key]
assert len(choices) == 2 and c in choices
return choices[choices[0] == c]
def petrial(self):
assert self.properties()['thin']
successors = {}
for c in self.chambers:
d = c
for i in (0, 2, 1):
d = self.flip(d, i)
successors[c] = d
assert len(set(successors.values())) == len(successors)
remaining = set(self.chambers)
faces, by_chamber, cycles = set(), {}, []
while remaining:
start = min(remaining)
walk, c = [], start
while c not in walk:
walk.append(c)
c = successors[c]
assert c == start
remaining.difference_update(walk)
face = (frozenset(c[0] for c in walk), frozenset(c[1] for c in walk))
faces.add(face)
for c in walk:
by_chamber[c] = face
cycles.append({'chambers': len(walk), 'vertices': len(face[0]),
'edges': len(face[1])})
faces = tuple(sorted(faces, key = lambda f: ( sorted(f[0]), sorted(f[1]))))
pairs = {(0, 1): self.pairs[0, 1],
(0, 2): {(v, f) for f, face in enumerate(faces) for v in face[0]},
(1, 2): {(e, f) for f, face in enumerate(faces) for e in face[1]}}
return Geometry((self.sizes[0], self.sizes[1], len(faces)), pairs), faces, by_chamber,
cycles
def coset_geometry(gens):
a, b, c = gens
G = closure(gens)
subgroups = [closure([a, b]), closure([a, c]), closure([b, c])]
cosets = []
for H in subgroups:
cs = {frozenset(mul(g, h) for h in H) for g in G}
cosets.append(tuple(sorted(cs, key = lambda x: sorted(x))))
lookups = [{C: j for j, C in enumerate(cs)} for cs in cosets]
actions = {g: tuple(tuple(lookups[i][frozenset(mul(g, h) for h in C)]
for C in cs) for i, cs in enumerate(cosets)) for g in G}
pairs = {(i, j): {(x, y) for x, X in enumerate(cosets[i]) for y, Y in enumerate(cosets[j])
if X & Y}
for i, j in ((0, 1), ( 0, 2), ( 1, 2))}
geom = Geometry(tuple(map(len, cosets)), pairs)
base = tuple(lookups[i][H] for i, H in enumerate(subgroups))
return G, geom, base, actions
def run_case(name, generators, expected_thin):
G, geom, base, actions = coset_geometry(generators)
prop = geom.properties()
assert prop['geometry'] and prop['thin'] and prop['residually_connected']
assert len(geom.chambers) == len(G)
assert {tuple(actions[g][i][base[i]] for i in range(3)) for g in G} == set(geom.chambers)
new, faces, by_chamber, cycles = geom.petrial()
new_prop = new.properties()
assert new_prop['geometry'] and new_prop['residually_connected']
assert new_prop['thin'] == expected_thin
P = by_chamber[base]
a, b, c = generators
bP = (frozenset(actions[b][0][v] for v in P[0]), frozenset(actions[b][1][e] for e in P[1]))
kernel = [g for g in G if all(actions[g][i] == tuple(range(geom.sizes[i])) for i in (0, 1))]
# Compute the core independently from actual conjugates, then compare the
# literal retained-object kernel with both forms of the uniform theorem.
A = closure([a])
core = set(A)
for g in G:
core.intersection_update(mul(mul(g, h), inv(g)) for h in A)
assert set(kernel) == core
central = all(mul(a, g) == mul(g, a) for g in G)
one = tuple(range(len(a)))
assert core == ({one, a} if central else {one})
induced = set()
face_lookup = {P: i for i, P in enumerate(faces)}
for g in G:
face_action = tuple(face_lookup[(frozenset(actions[g][0][v] for v in P[0]),
frozenset(actions[g][1][e] for e in P[1]))] for P in faces)
act = actions[g][:2] + (face_action,)
induced.add(act)
assert all(tuple(act[i][C[i]] for i in range(3)) in new.chambers for C in new.chambers)
out = {'name': name, 'generators_zero_based': list(map(list, generators)),
'group_order': len(G), 'pair_orders_ab_ac_bc': [order(mul(a, b)), order(mul(a, c)),
order(mul(b, c))],
'original': prop, 'petrial': new_prop, 'action_kernel_order': len(kernel),
'induced_action_order': len(induced), 'base_face_fixed_by_b': bP == P,
'computed_core_order': len(core), 'face_marker_central': central,
'petrie_cycles': sorted(cycles, key = lambda x: (x[ 'chambers'], x[ 'vertices'],
x['edges']))}
if new_prop['thin']:
assert len(kernel) == 1
base_new = base[:2] + (face_lookup[P],)
orbit = {tuple(act[i][base_new[i]] for i in range(3)) for act in induced}
assert orbit == set(new.chambers)
second, second_faces, _, _ = new.petrial()
old_shadows = tuple((frozenset(v for v, f0 in geom.pairs[0, 2] if f0 == f),
frozenset(e for e, f0 in geom.pairs[1, 2] if f0 == f))
for f in range(geom.sizes[2]))
assert len(set(old_shadows)) == len(old_shadows)
assert set(second_faces) == set(old_shadows)
assert second.properties()['thin'] and second.properties()['residually_connected']
out['bidual_face_shadows_exact'] = True
out['induced_action_chamber_regular'] = True
assert 2 in out['pair_orders_ab_ac_bc'][:2]
return out
def main():
parser = argparse.ArgumentParser()
parser.add_argument('--output', type= Path, default = Path(__file__).with_suffix('.json'))
args = parser.parse_args()
cases = [
('C2_cubed', [cyc( 6, ( 0, 1)), cyc( 6, ( 2, 3)), cyc( 6, ( 4, 5))], False),
('C2_times_S3', [cyc( 5, ( 0, 1)), cyc( 5, ( 2, 3)), cyc( 5, ( 3, 4))], False),
('S4_tetrahedron', [cyc( 4, ( 2, 3)), cyc( 4, ( 1, 2)), cyc( 4, ( 0, 1))], True),
('S4_tetrahedron_vertex_edge_swap', [cyc( 4, ( 2, 3)), cyc( 4, ( 0, 1)), cyc( 4, ( 1, 2))],
True),
('S4_star', [cyc( 4, ( 0, 1)), cyc( 4, ( 0, 2)), cyc( 4, ( 0, 3))], False),
('B3_cube', [cyc( 6, ( 2, 4), ( 3, 5)), cyc( 6, ( 0, 2), ( 1, 3)), cyc( 6, ( 0, 1))], True),
('B3_cube_vertex_edge_swap', [cyc( 6, ( 2, 4), ( 3, 5)), cyc( 6, ( 0, 1)), cyc( 6, ( 0, 2),
(1, 3))], True),
]
results = [run_case(*case) for case in cases]
assert results[0]['action_kernel_order'] == results[1]['action_kernel_order'] == 2
assert results[0]['petrial']['type_sizes'] == [2, 2, 1]
assert results[2]['base_face_fixed_by_b'] and not results[3]['base_face_fixed_by_b']
report = {'status': 'PASS', 'scope': 'Seven explicit finite examples; no exhaustion or
uniform proof claim.' ,
'checker_sha256': hashlib.sha256(Path( __file__).read_bytes()).hexdigest(),
'cases': results, 'summary': { 'examples': len(results),
'original_chambers': sum(x['original']['chambers'] for x in results),
'thin_petrials': sum(x['petrial']['thin'] for x in results),
'nonfaithful_petrial_actions': sum(x['action_kernel_order'] > 1 for x in
results)}}
with args.output.open('x', encoding = 'utf-8') as stream:
stream.write(json.dumps(report, indent = 2, sort_keys = True) + '\n')
print(json.dumps(report['summary'], sort_keys = True))
print('PASS')

# Source-bound raw presentations, all N009 finite table labels and universal orders.
# All orders below come from COMPLETE Todd--Coxeter coset tables, not NQ bounds.
SizeScreen([240,40]);;
report24:= [];;meet24:= 0;;tc24:= 0;;
Cox24:= function(f,typ)
local n,rels,i,j;n:= Length(typ)+1;rels:= List(f{[1..n]},x->x^2);
for i in [ 1..n] do for j in [ i+1..n] do
if j= i+1 then Add(rels,(f[i]*f[j])^typ[i]);else Add(rels,Comm(f[i],f[j]));fi;
od;od;return rels;
end;;
TableWords24:= function(f)
local a,b,c;a:= f[1];b:= f[2];c:= f[3];
return [[( a*b*c*b)^4,(c*a*(b*c)^3*a*b)^2],
[(c*a*b)^8,(a*(b*c)^3*b)^2],
[(c*(a*b*c*b)^2)^2,(c*a*(b*c)^3*a*b)^2],
[(a*(b*c)^3*b)^2,(c*a*b)^5*c*(b*a)^2*(c*b)^2*a*b]];
end;;
Build24:= function(name,F,rels,rank)
local fg,table,n,gs,G,mark,sets,pars,i,j,typ;
fg:= GeneratorsOfGroup(F);table:= CosetTableFromGensAndRels(fg,rels,[]);n:= Length(table[1]);
gs:= List([1..Length(fg)],i->PermList(table[2*i-1]));G:= Group(gs);mark:= gs{[1..rank]};
if Size(G)<>n or Subgroup(G,mark)<>G then Error("Complete regular coset
action/order/generation",name);fi;
if not ForAll(rels,w->MappedWord(w,fg,gs)= One(G)) then Error("Raw relators" ,name);fi;
sets:= Combinations([1..rank]);pars:= List(sets,S->Subgroup(G,mark{S}));
for i in [ 1..Length(sets)] do for j in [ 1..Length(sets)] do
if Intersection(pars[i],pars[j])<>pars[Position(sets,Intersection(sets[i],sets[j]))] then
Error("Actual IP" ,name);fi;
meet24:= meet24+1;
od;od;
typ:= List([1..rank-1],i->Order(mark[i]*mark[i+1]));
Add(report24,[name,n,typ]);tc24:= tc24+1;
Print("PRESENTATION " ,name," order=" ,n," type=" ,typ," IP=PASS\n" );
return rec( name:= name,group:= G,mark:= mark,allgens:= gs,order:= n,F:= F,rels:= rels);
end;;
Same24:= function(A,B)
local h;h:= GroupHomomorphismByImages(A.group,B.group,A.mark,B.mark);return h<>fail and
IsBijective(h);
end;;
